--- Page 1 ---
This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the <term>public domain</term> <note>A legal status where a work is no longer protected by copyright and belongs to the public.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other <term>notes in the margins</term> <note>original: "marginalia"</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

*   **Make non-commercial use of the files:** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

*   **Refrain from automated querying:** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, <term>optical character recognition</term> <note>Technology used to convert images of text into machine-readable data.</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

*   **Maintain attribution:** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

*   **Keep it legal:** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

### About Google Book Search

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

small good mind

**Y**OU are **sick in body, or soul, or both; it is the gracious care of your merciful Creator to prepare and apply means for the cure of them both. It is, and ought to be, your duty principally to respect the more worthy part, and give all diligence to attend to the most noble part of yourself: Dead bones and rottenness are but such and no better in a painted sepulcher <note>sepulcher: a tomb or burial chamber.</note>; whatever else is neglected, do not let your soul run to ruin. You have plenty of provision set before you; do not starve at a feast of rich things <note>original: "feast of fat things." This is a biblical allusion to Isaiah 25:6, signifying a lavish spiritual banquet.</note>; even if it is not served in the best**

<summary>The translator opens the preface by urging the reader to prioritize the health of their soul over their body, framing the book as spiritual and physical "medicine" provided by the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Translator's Apology, spiritual health, soul vs body, religious metaphor, Isaiah 25:6, 17th-century medicine, preface</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translator's preface from the previous page, finishing the sentence regarding the "fashion" in which spiritual food is served.</meta>

fashion, do not reject good meat because of the cook’s lack of skill. Indeed, the princely delicacies of this royal banquet deserve a more refined hand to present them to the world; and I (out of humility) have for a long time excused myself from the task, *so that it might be given to one more worthy* <note>original Latin: "ut detur digniori"</note>. I preferred and offered it to others with greater abilities, who were more fit to have undertaken it. Had they accepted, these incomparable mysteries and unsearchable truths would have been more *chemically* <note>original: "Chymically." In this context, it refers to the process of alchemical distillation—refining a substance to its purest essence through spiritual chemistry.</note> prepared, rather than offered to you in the coarse form of such a dull language. This is my only *apology*: namely, that I have been more careful to be *faithful* than *ornate* <note>original: "curious." In the 17th century, this meant excessively detailed, complicated, or artistically wrought.</note>, not striving so much to decorate the garment as to make it fit the body. If others had shown their skill, I would not be judged as a bungler.

But, Reader, I am not so focused on making my own excuses that I would leave you entirely without a *caution*. Therefore, I advise you as that most profound *Teutonic Philosopher* <note>Teutonic Philosopher: A common title for the German mystic Jakob Böhme (1575–1624), whose works often blended theology with alchemical philosophy.</note> **Jacob Behmen** says somewhere in his writings: *I counsel you, namely, if your mind is not spiritual, stop reading these things, for they will do you more harm than good. If you are not enlightened in the true mystery of*

<meta>catchword: Emanuel</meta>
<sig>3</sig>

<summary>The translator defends his plain, unadorned translation style, arguing that the spiritual "meat" of the text is more important than the "cook's" skill. He introduces a stern warning from Jakob Böhme that the reader must be spiritually prepared to understand these mysteries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, specifically completing a warning—likely drawn from the mystic Jakob Böhme—that the reader must approach these spiritual mysteries with a holy mind rather than a worldly or "carnal" one.</meta>

<lang>English</lang>
<sig>A 4</sig>

Immanuel <note>original: "Emanuel." A Hebrew name meaning "God with us."</note>; if you do not know the extent of the Incarnate Word; if you do not understand the *angelic ascent and descent* on *Jacob’s ladder* <note>A reference to Genesis 28:12, symbolizing the connection between heaven and earth.</note>, how can you cast your vote <note>original: "suffrage." In this context, it means a voice in prayer or a declaration of agreement.</note> into that *seraphic acclamation*: **"Holy, Holy, Holy is the Lord of hosts; the whole earth is full of His glory"** (Isaiah 6:3)?

Let no carnal <note>original: "carnall." Referring to a mind focused on worldly or physical desires rather than spiritual truth.</note> mind cling to the doorposts of *Wisdom’s house*, or come near the gates of her *temple*. The *oratories* <note>places of prayer</note> of old were, with prophetic <note>original: "Oraculous"</note> care, guarded from profanity with this inscription over the doors: **"Go away, you unholy ones; it is a temple, a temple; let nothing unholy enter"** <note>original Latin: "Ite profani; Fanum est, Fanum, nihil ingrediatur profanum"</note>. *This place is holy; let no filthy thing come into it.* I may say of this following preface: it is *holy*; let no carnal mind profane or pervert it. 

The learned and religious author would have you wise unto salvation, so that you may be saved from your own wisdom—your greatest enemy. I cannot say whether he was a better *physician* or *theologian* <note>original: "Divine"</note>; whether he may do your body more good or your soul. I am sure his prescriptions may well be called **Royal Chemistry** <note>original: "Basilica Chymica." This is likely a reference to the famous work by Oswald Croll (1609), which blended medicine and alchemy.</note> or **Royal Recipes**, being the rare extracts of the most select spiritual truths drawn out of the coarse <note>original: "grosse"</note> body of natural things. Bear with the wordiness <note>original: "prolixity"</note> of his *introduction*; the rest will recom— <note>The word completes on the next page as "recompense."</note>

<summary>The translator exhorts the reader to approach the text with spiritual purity, comparing the author's work to a "Royal Chemistry" that extracts spiritual truth from the natural world.</summary>

I ask for your patience: I once thought about shortening this work, but I did not dare meddle with such excellent symmetry. I have released it without any alteration, other than what a *German* wears when dressed in *English fashion* <note>The translator is explaining that this is a translation of a German work into English.</note>.

<margin>Romans 1:20</margin>
<margin>
The whole world 
is nothing else 
but... 
<note>original Latin: "Universus mundus nihil aliud cunque." This is an incomplete sentence likely meaning "The whole world is nothing else but a mirror of God," referencing the idea that God is visible through His creation.</note>
</margin>

The best *medical method* <note>original: "Physicall Method"</note> for your *eternal welfare* is followed here. The *main aim* and *principal intention* is, first, to correct the *internal life force* <term>Archeus</term> of the *inner man*, so that it may attract health from the heavenly *prime matter* <term>Iliaster</term> and distribute it to all the faculties of your *soul* and parts of your *body*. Then, it aims to drive out <note>original: "profligate"</note> and correct the unhealthy fluids <term>peccant humors</term> of your *outward* and *natural self*. All of this is accomplished through the *grace of God* revealed in the great *mysteries of the Incarnation, life, death, resurrection, ascension, and mediation* <note>original: "Mediatorship"</note> of our blessed Lord and Redeemer, *Jesus Christ*. 

Do not be offended (though many have stumbled and fallen over this) that the *Father* and the *Son* should be so near to you in *all things*; that *God* and *Christ* should condescend and humble themselves to walk before you, with you, and in you; or that *invisible* things should be seen in *visible* things. It was the common faith of the *ancients*, both *church authorities* <note>original: "Ecclesiasticall"</note> and...

<summary>The translator explains that he has preserved the original structure of the work while translating it from German to English. He outlines the book's dual purpose: healing the "inner man" through spiritual life forces (Archeus and Iliaster) and the "outer man" through divine grace and the mysteries of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>translation, Archeus, Iliaster, Paracelsian medicine, Incarnation, inner man, peccant humors, spiritual health</keywords>

...men of this world! Their minds have been so blinded by the god of this world <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 4:4, implying that worldly distractions or Satan prevent people from seeing spiritual truth.</note> that even though the true God is visible in all His works—which people may see and behold from afar—they still do not magnify Him. (Job 36:24, 25).

<margin>Proverbs 1:20, 21 & 8:1–9, etc.</margin>
The Lord *Jesus Christ*, the true and only *Wisdom of God*, cries out and lifts His voice in the streets, in the main gathering places, at the opening of the gates, and in the city. He speaks from the tops of high places, along the road, in the paths, at the city entrance, and at the doorway. He speaks with plain words of righteousness, without any stubbornness <note>original: "frowardnesse"</note> or perverseness, to us—the sons of men. Does *Christ Jesus* not stand all day long in the broad road to stop people and turn them away from destruction, calling and crying out to them, "*Turn back, turn back! Why will you die, you children of Adam?*" Yet they, with stiff necks like their fathers before them, always resist the *Holy Spirit* <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note>. Is any part of Creation lacking <note>original: "destitute of"</note> the glorious presence <margin>Psalm 19:6</margin> and power of *Christ*? Can anything be hidden from the heat and light of this Sun of <margin>Romans 10:18</margin> Righteousness? Is there any place where the *Gospel Trumpet* <note>original: "Evangelicall Trump"</note> has not sounded? Why, then, do we close our eyes and stop our ears against the good news <note>original: "glad tydings"</note> and ministry of...

<summary>The author argues that Christ, as the Wisdom of God, is universally revealed through both nature and public preaching, yet humanity remains willfully blind and resistant to this truth. He emphasizes that no part of creation is hidden from the light of the "Sun of Righteousness."</summary>

<margin>God explained <note>original: "Deus explicatus"</note>, Athenagoras.</margin>

It is *profane* to think that the *Creation* is anything other than the *revelation* of the *Creator*. Can *God* be seen in all things, and yet not *Christ* as well, by whom all things were made? If we follow the *Father*, He will draw us to the *Son*. All things call out loudly to us to behold the *mystery* of the *Father* and the *Son*; they call us to hear their *voice* and cry shame upon us because we do not listen or obey.

The *grain* <note>original: "corne"</note>, *wine*, and *oil*, the *silver* and *gold* which *God* multiplies for men—they prepare these for *Baal* <note>A reference to the Canaanite god, here representing any idol or worldly obsession</note> and spend on their *lusts* 
<margin>Hosea 2:8.</margin>
the very things which should be *motives* for their love. 

*Times* and *seasons* are 
<margin>Psalm 19:2; Acts 14:17.</margin>
the *faithful tutors* and *witnesses* of *God*, but men do not respect their teaching or receive their testimony.

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page contains a postscript by Henry Pinnell, clarifying that his emphasis on the natural world (Creation) is meant to complement, not replace, the authority of the Bible (Scripture).</meta>

# Postscript.

## READER,

**W**hereas I have proposed and pressed for the Creation of God as an object of your admiration and a motive for his adoration, it is not, in the least, intended in any way to diminish the due reverence rightfully belonging to that richer mercy of the Lord granted to man in giving and preserving those sacred records of heavenly mysteries contained in the most holy Scriptures; my desire, rather, is that both those books of God (*Nature* and *Scripture* <note>original: "Nature and Scripture" — a common seventeenth-century theological concept suggesting God reveals himself through two "books": the natural world and the Bible.</note>) might be better studied and more observed. Do I seek to make void the Word of the Lord by his works <note>term: "works" refers here to the physical, created world and the laws of nature.</note>? God forbid; instead, I establish the one by the other.

***

<summary>In this postscript, the author clarifies that admiring God's physical creation does not detract from the importance of the Bible, arguing that both "Nature" and "Scripture" are essential ways to understand the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Nature vs Scripture, theology, Henry Pinnell, natural revelation, biblical authority</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues from a postscript by Henry Pinnell regarding the relationship between Nature and Scripture. It transitions into a celebratory poem about the works of the alchemists Oswald Croll (Crollius) and Paracelsus.</meta>

# *Added by a Friend of the Publisher of this work of Crollius and Paracelsus in English.*

---

## **CROLLIUS.**

Two things are seen within this volume small:
*the origins of both the great and the lesser worlds.* <note>original: "the great and the lesse Worlds" refers to the Macrocosm (the universe) and the Microcosm (the human being).</note>
Here a man may see his own shape as if in a mirror,
*by which he may completely escape corruption.* <note>original: "glasse" meaning mirror.</note>
The author who wrote this was divinely taught,
*and so was the person who translated it.*
Here may be seen what nature is, and what grace is,
*what God’s back parts are, and what is his face.* <note>original: "back parts... and face" is an allusion to Exodus 33:23, where Moses is allowed to see God's back but not his face, representing partial versus full divine revelation.</note>
Here both heaven and earth are in harmony,
*a cure to ease us of our vanity.*
The true Elixir is here, the stone that
*transforms both the outward and the inward self;* <term>the stone: References the "Philosopher's Stone," a legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and achieving spiritual perfection.</term>
And makes all things heavenly, like fine crystal,
*indeed, like Christ, the divine prototype.*
What is above is likewise here below,
*as this anatomy of man shows.* <note>original: "What is above is likewise here below" is the fundamental principle of Hermeticism: "As above, so below," suggesting that the patterns of the heavens are mirrored in the human body.</note>
Man, in all his parts, consists
*of the same things the Macrocosm is composed of.* <term>Macrocosm: The "great world" or the universe as a whole.</term>
The world itself is a man, though great and large,
*and man himself is a world; only dig here,*
And a treasure will appear, worth more than all else,
*by which he may be ransomed from his fall.*

<summary>This page features a commendatory poem summarizing the Hermetic and alchemical philosophy of Oswald Croll and Paracelsus, emphasizing the "as above, so below" connection between the universe (Macrocosm) and humanity (Microcosm).</summary>
<keywords>Crollius, Paracelsus, alchemy, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Philosopher's Stone, Hermeticism, spiritual anatomy, divine prototype</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory poems praising the works of Oswald Croll and Paracelsus. It concludes the argument for the necessary union between medicine (physick) and theology (divinity) before transitioning into a poem specifically celebrating Paracelsus and the importance of translating his secrets into English.</meta>

*The medicine then that is of use to man,* <term>Physick: An archaic term for the art of healing or medicine.</term>
  *none can prescribe but he who is a theologian;* <term>Divine: A clergyman or student of theology.</term>
*He too who is quite unskilled in Nature,*
  *has filled the world with dark error:*
*Both were joined in one by God at first,*
  *and in One again they shall be found at last.*
*Nature, grace, medicine, and divinity,*
  *thus returning to their original unity:*
*God be blessed forever, from whom all good comes,*
  *which only devils and wicked men resisted.*

### THE
## Admonitory Preface
->OF<-

### COURTEOUS READER;

ALTHOUGH the Romans praised *Angerona* <note>The Roman goddess of silence and secrecy.</note>, and the Greeks admired *Harpocrates* <note>original: "Hapocrotes"; the Greek god of silence, often depicted with a finger to his lips.</note> for their silence; and all the ancient philosophers, by the example of *Actaeon* <note>In mythology, Actaeon was punished for seeing the goddess Diana bathing; Croll uses him here to warn against "seeing" or exposing forbidden secrets of nature.</note>, have strictly forbidden anyone to profane the secrets or rashly expose <term>original: "prostitute"</term> the treasures of Nature to unworthy and foul-minded men; nevertheless, seeing that our Heavenly Father, who

<margin>
Men imitate God most when they do good. — *Strabo* <note>original: "Strabe"; referring to the Greek geographer and philosopher.</note>

...is that Sun which richly blesses us all beforehand <note>original: "preventeth"; from the Latin *praevenio*, meaning to come before or anticipate with favor/grace</note> with his gifts, shining on everyone alike. For he shares his light, without favoritism or envy, with the good and the bad, with the thankful and the ungrateful. We are bound to imitate him, whose children we ought to be—especially those of us who, by his gracious mercy, have been brought back and recovered from the dark labyrinth and maze of toil and error to the plain and clear path and center of rest and truth.

<margin>
The gifts of God grow and are improved by sharing them. — *Victor* <note>Likely referring to Victor of Antioch or Victor of Capua, early Christian commentators.</note>
</margin>

Therefore, I thought it was not right, out of a kind of inhuman ill-will, to hide the talent committed to me by the Father of Lights any longer—like a gift wrapped in a napkin <note>A reference to the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where a servant is punished for hiding his master's money in a cloth rather than using it.</note>—through a wicked silence. Since the gates of wisdom ought always to stand open to men of talent, even though the doors of the Muses <note>A metaphor for the arts, sciences, and the inspiration of knowledge.</note> have never been unlocked without envy attempting to shut them again. It is, undoubtedly, a most civil and humane duty to willingly instruct and guide our neighbor who has gone astray, and to keep those who are already on the right path within it.

For this reason, *friendly reader*, for the praise and glory of the Divine Majesty and his bounty—I both desire and hope to be an instrument or pen in the publishing of this work, as I am far less than all the mercies and benefits he has bestowed upon me—and also for the profit and advantage of my neighbor in the study and profession of <term>Chemistry</term> <note>original: "Chymistry"; here referring specifically to iatrochemistry, the Paracelsian practice of using chemical and mineral preparations for medicine.</note>,

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Admonitory Preface" by Oswald Croll. On the previous page, Croll discussed the duty of scholars to share their knowledge rather than hiding it like a buried talent. Here, he explains his motivation for publishing his alchemical and medical secrets, which he gathered through decades of travel and personal experimentation.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>B 2</sig>

<margin>I voluntarily shared these with Emperor Rudolf <note>Rudolf II (1552–1612), Holy Roman Emperor and a famous patron of alchemy and the occult.</note>.</margin>
...[I have published these] lest these <term>Spagyric</term> <note>original: "Spagyrik"; a term coined by Paracelsus referring to alchemical medicine produced by separating and then recombining the components of a substance.</note> mysteries—whose preparations I have personally verified through my own hands-on experience after many errors in expense, time, and labor—should remain hidden. I have never found these secrets published all together at once, either in reports or ancient records, until this very day. I have always avoided burdening the reader with trifles and falsehoods, which this present age (the scum and dregs of the world) produces in abundance. Much less would I fill up a huge volume with copies of long medical recipes—even though I have many on hand that I collected with great labor and effort—with 

<margin>The longest medical recipes are, for the most part, the biggest deceptions.</margin>
which "hodge-podge" physicians are already so entangled and overwhelmed that they are almost crushed to death under the heavy weight of their own recipes. Instead, I offer those things that I have obtained over nearly twenty years of many troubles and difficult travels <note>original: "peregrinations"</note> of varying fortune through *France, Italy, Germany, Hungary, Poland, and Bohemia*. Through tireless diligence and exact testing, I gained this knowledge from men of the greatest learning and experience in chemistry—partly by request, and partly by purchase or exchange <note>original: "permutation"</note>—not to mention the many things I discovered in medical practice through the blessing of God upon my efforts. 

<margin>It is a courteous thing, and full of honest modesty, to acknowledge from whom we have gained anything. — Pliny in his *Letter to Vespasian* <note>original: "Plin in Epist. ad Vesp."; referring to Pliny the Elder’s preface to his *Natural History*.</note></margin>
I am preferring the public good over my own private advantage, being moved also by charity, Christian compassion, and the needs of sick people, in accordance with the trust placed in my profession.

<summary>Croll explains that his book contains alchemical secrets he personally tested during twenty years of travel across Europe. He criticizes physicians who use overly complex recipes and asserts that he is publishing his findings out of a sense of Christian duty to help the sick.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Oswald Croll’s explanation of his methodology. He contrasts his hands-on experimental approach with the "trickery" and "fallacious opinions" of other practitioners, emphasizing that he has refined and perfected the secrets he gathered during his travels through rigorous laboratory testing.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>

...profession <note>original: "session"</note> and practice of *medicine* <note>original: "Physick"</note>, I would first "take my eyes in my hand," as the saying goes, and by the help of **fire** <note>original: "Vulcan," the Roman god of fire; in alchemy, this refers to the use of the furnace to test and purify substances.</note> search and diligently examine the finest and most choice things before I would recommend them for public use and benefit. In this way, the "children of knowledge"—those searchers and lovers of Truth—may obtain at a low cost what I paid for dearly in the learning. That is, they will receive not deceptive <note>original: "fallacious"</note> opinions (of which they have complained in so much **trickery** <note>original: "Legerdemain"</note> until now), but the truth itself, so long desired, and most certainly discovered and known through many experiments.

As a result, many things have been returned for free—and with interest—to their owners, whole and entire, and far more complete, after the cycle of the *Platonic year* <note>The "Platonic Year" or Great Year is an ancient concept of a long cycle of time (roughly 25,000 years) after which the stars return to their original positions; here, Croll uses it to suggest a long period of study and refinement.</note>. These were the same things I previously received from them in a "ragged and torn" state at a high price. For many people, whose reputations for knowing hidden secrets enticed me to visit them (despite difficult travel and no small expense), seeing them in person greatly diminished the rumors about them. As often happens in such cases, those things they boasted of as great secrets seemed to me either common or so faulty and defective that I had to repair the imperfections of their many descriptions. I reduced them all into a single, better version, which I first proved through my own *chemical* <note>original: "Chymicall"</note> testing. Also, by an exchange that worked to their advantage (following that common *alchemical* custom: "give something and take something"), I frequently compared my studies...

<summary>Croll describes his rigorous process of verifying medical and alchemical secrets using fire and experimentation, often finding that the "secrets" he traveled far to learn were flawed until he perfected them himself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Oswald Croll’s critique of alchemists who rely on theory and hearsay rather than practical laboratory work. He describes how his own investigations into the "secrets" of others often proved disappointing, leading him to advocate for personal experimentation over blind belief in authors.</meta>

<sig>B 3</sig>

...with theirs. In our discussions, they received an egg in exchange for a nut <note>This likely refers to an exchange where they received something of substance (the egg) while Croll received something small and hard to open (the nut).</note>. However, when I had cracked that nut with long and fruitless effort, I found at last nothing but a worm-eaten kernel—or instead of a kernel, I received only husks and shells.

Consequently, it happens that without Nature's fire <note>original: "Vulcan," the Roman god of fire; in alchemy, this refers to the use of the furnace to test and purify substances.</note>—which poets praise as the truest inventor and teacher of the mysteries of art—most writers of our time have written about secret chemical <note>original: "Spagyrical," referring to the alchemical process of separating and recombining substances.</note> preparations based on the reports of others rather than their own hands-on experience. They have offered such results to students of chemistry <note>original: "Chymistry"</note> that, for the most part, after spending great sums in vain, these students ultimately blame these authors for their wasted labor and time.

<margin>Learn from the warnings of others to be cautious, lest you regret your wasted labor.</margin>
Those who are not satisfied with my example or the losses of others will easily agree with me. They would rather be made more cautious by their own experience and examine these things again with the help of the art of fire <note>original: "Pyrotechnye," from the Greek *pyr* (fire) and *techne* (art); an early term for chemistry.</note> or chemistry. Just as many things are discussed that are never actually achieved, so too many things differ <note>original: "fall out"</note> between theory and practice. Anyone who wishes to adopt another's experience as their own will find this to be true; they will be deceived by those who have themselves been deceived by others. Therefore, in this study, no one should be believed any further than what each person finds through their own personal experience—handling and seeing through the proper...

<summary>Croll warns that most chemical texts are based on hearsay rather than practical experience, leading students to waste time and money on failed experiments. He argues that in the "art of fire," theory often fails to match practice, and truth can only be found through personal laboratory work.</summary>

( 6 )

...the proven trial of fire, which discerns and separates that which is false from that which is true.

<margin>He who knows useful things, not he who knows many things, is wise. <note>original Greek: ὁ χρήσιμα, ἐκ’ ὁ πολλ’ εἰδώς</note></margin>
And because, according to *Aeschylus*, it is not he who knows many things, but he who knows those things that are most profitable, who is to be considered a wise man, I would rather present only a few things that are rare and choice. This is in consideration of the words of *Damascene* <note>original: "Damascen"; likely referring to the medieval medical authority Johannes Mesue Damascenus</note>: *Let your medicines be few, whose virtues and operations you have often tested.* However, despite this <term>paucity</term> <note>original: "paucity or fewnesse"; a small amount or scarcity</note> of medicines, I dare confidently affirm that among all the riches and plenty of Nature, there do not lie hidden any more choice and effective remedies than these chief secrets of the more hidden medicines offered here. This excludes only that universal and most famous medicine <note>This is a reference to the "Philosopher's Stone" or "Universal Medicine," believed by alchemists to cure all diseases and prolong life</note> which the most ancient wise philosophers had from the beginning of the world and have extolled as a miracle to their 

<margin>See Anaxagoras in his book *On things beyond nature*. <note>original Greek: περι τῶν ὑπερ φυσικων</note></margin>
just and lawful heirs. For that which is good is not always to be found in that which is great, 

<margin>For the good is not in the great, but the great in the good. <note>original Greek: ἐ γὰρ ἐν τω μεγαλω τὸ ἐυ, etc.</note></margin>
but that which is great in that which is good. He that devotes himself to philosophy shall sincerely, and as he ought, enter the inner rooms of Nature by a holy persistence in his preparations. By joining to this a diligent contemplation of natural causes, and by refusing no pains and difficulties to gain experience, he shall—by the industry of his handiwork (if the grace of the favor of the Most High is infused into him)—bring forth far greater things out of this open bosom of Nature than they seem

And although I have proven only some of those virtues and operations which are assigned to each medicine in order—according to which I value the medicine, as I have set down and shown their preparations in this work itself—nevertheless, I have found the most important parts to be true in the practice of <term>physic</term> <note>original: "Phisick"; the art of healing or medicine</note>.

Such men can, by a glance and sight alone, make a good interpretation and easily guess the nature of these things. 

These preparations require such an Artist, and not one of a stubborn and foolish disposition. Such fools have nothing to do with the common rabble of alchemists, who bring about that unworthy reproach and slander: *the errors of the practitioner are blamed on the art.* <note>original: "The errors of the Artists are imputed to the Art"; likely a translation of the Latin maxim *Erratis artificis imputatur arti*</note>

To the aforementioned Artists, I say, who are masters of their craft, there will be no doubt that the other operations published here—which have been proven by the long practice and certain experiments of the most expert chemists—will satisfy their desires and earnest expectations by undoubtedly achieving the intended effect.

...their sick neighbors, of whatever rank or condition they may be, who, in the fear of God, shall also use these medicines. When these are rightly prepared by the practitioners' own labor, in a convenient and skillful method—not trusting (unless they wish to be unsuccessful) as many do, the deceptive and fraudulent preparations of others—they will know by experience far greater efficacy and results in the cures they perform than I can or ought to describe. This is especially true if the medicines are administered according to the nature of the diseased person and with the approval of a <term>physician</term> <note>original: "Phisitian"</note> present; all of which, by God's help, **Truth, the daughter of time**, <note>original: *Veritas filia temporis*; a popular Renaissance proverb meaning that the truth will eventually be revealed as time passes</note> will make manifest.

<margin>
Ignorance is the inseparable companion of pride and mischief.
</margin>

But to what danger do I now expose myself by this public service? What, I ask, is to be done in this diversity and hazard of opinions? What shall a man do in the midst of so many carping critics, who cast the worst censure on the best things? I shall oppose them all with the shield of my sincerity and my earnest endeavor to serve well in the <term>Spagyric</term> <note>original: "Spagyrik"; a branch of alchemy focused on extracting the "essential" healing virtues from plants and minerals</note> profession. With this, I can defend myself without fear; for this shield does not care for the proud censures of ignorant men. It will easily blunt the edge of all the foreseen darts of the adversary—namely, anger, evil speaking, envy, and scornful contempt, which is the truest testimony of ignorance.

<meta>This page continues the author's warning against those who are unworthy of chemical secrets. It focuses on the deceptive nature of "sophists" and fraudulent practitioners who steal knowledge from others to build their own reputations.</meta>

<margin>
These people seek praise for their cleverness by stealing from and slandering their own benefactors.
</margin>
...experience, counterfeit friendship, disguised piety, and various empty promises made at a high price to those who never knew how to look out for themselves or detect the deceit of others. Now, just as it is the calculated trickery of some—who do not immediately reveal their true nature through their facial expressions or clothing—to steal phrases from the conversation and company of other men, and afterward seek a reputation for learning, wisdom, and prudence among great men while hiding their actual sources; and just as they use many wiles to fish information out of other good men; so, through the fraud and subtlety of a deceptive and **treacherous** <note>original: "turn-coat"</note> craft, they sell these things as they usually do at the common price, further distracting people. Even more so do they sell, with a pompous show, their forged **evil-chemical** <term>original: Caco-chymicall; referring to fraudulent, failed, or harmful chemistry</term> and **sophistical** <term>deceptive or fallacious reasoning</term> arts, which do not contain even a spark of truth but deserve to be utterly rejected. They do this because they fear that their **Pharisaic** <term>hypocritical; acting with outward righteousness to hide inner wickedness</term> skin—which hides a fox-like and cursed mind—would otherwise be stripped away. But before I come to describe the **remedies**, I suppose it will not be a completely wasted effort if first, by the assistance of the great **God**, I handle some things in this *Admonitory Preface*. <margin>I.</margin>
*What that medicine <term>Phisick</term> <note>original: "Phisick"; the traditional term for the art of healing or medical science.</note> is which cures men's diseases, though few physicians know it. <margin>IV.</margin>

<margin>V.</margin>
*What kind of physician that medicine requires as its minister.*

<margin>VI.</margin>
*Concerning the universal and chief medicine of the most ancient philosophers—commended by many, possessed, and even seen or believed in by very few.*

*Something in defense of the published truth, as an epilogue or conclusion.*

<page-num>22</page-num>

## CHAP. I.

# Of the True Medicine <term>Physick</term>.
<note>original: "Physick"; in this context, it refers to the art and science of medicine.</note>

<margin>1</margin>
<margin>See Paracelsus's *Labyrinth of Physicians* <note>original: "Paracelf. his Labyrinth of Phyfitians"; a reference to the work *Labyrinthus Medicorum Errantium* by the Swiss physician and alchemist Paracelsus (1493–1541).</note>.</margin>
<margin><insert>Possibly Platonic principles—Plenitude or Goodness</insert></margin>
<margin>The master teaches that student <term>scholar</term> in vain whose birth <term>nativity</term> is not disposed toward that art by the stars <note>The author suggests that a true physician must have an innate, astrological predisposition for the healing arts.</note>.</margin>

**T**he true medicine, which I intend to treat here with divine assistance, is the mere <note>meaning pure or absolute</note> gift of the most high God. It is not to be sought for or learned from the heathens <note>The author is likely referring to the ancient Greek and Roman medical authorities like Galen, whose works dominated medical school curricula at the time.</note>, but from God alone—the Ancient of Days, the Father of Lights, who cannot err, and the only Governor of the supreme universe. 

Wisdom, therefore, is not to be obtained from created things <term>the Creatures</term>, but from God. Being the first inventor of all secrets, He alone knows with what properties He has endowed every creature. Therefore, no mortal master or "dead letter" <note>A term often used by spiritualists to describe book-learning that lacks life or spiritual insight.</note> can ever teach it as well as He can, who is the perfect artist of all things—even the most high Creator and glorious God. From Him, knowledge flows unto us like heat from the sun's beams, which produces all kinds of flowers and herbs. For what does man have that he has not received from above? 

All learn from the first human <note>Likely referring to Adam, who in the Hermetic and Paracelsian tradition was believed to have possessed a perfect, innate knowledge of nature before the Fall.</note> by regression (tracing knowledge back to its source), and this first man learned from God, who gave him knowledge at his creation. A physician should be born out of the Light of Grace and the Nature of the inward and invisible man—the internal angel and the Light of Nature <note>The "Light of Nature" (Lumen Naturae) is a central concept in this period, referring to an innate wisdom and divine intelligence present within both the natural world and the human mind that allows one to "read" the secrets of creation.</note>. This Light, like a sound doctor, teaches and instructs men, just as the Holy Spirit taught...

<margin>The knowledge of medicine in the outward world is to be gathered from the Limbus <note>original: "Limbovn"; likely referring to the Paracelsian "Limbus," the primordial matter or matrix from which all things were created.</note>, and depends upon the knowledge of Man.</margin>

**Paracelsus** <note>A famous Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who argued that diseases were caused by external forces rather than internal imbalances of humors.</note> says that to avoid an **Emptiness** in all the four elements, God created living creatures that are "inanimate"—that is to say, without an **intellectual soul**. These were intended to be the four kinds of inhabitants of the elements. They differ from Men (who were created after the **Image of God**) in their understanding, wisdom, arts, work, and dwellings.

To the **Water** belong **Nymphs**, **Undines**, and **Melusines** <note>original: "Nimphs, Undens, Melosyns"; these are elemental spirits of the water.</note>, whose **monsters** or offspring are the **Sirens** that swim upon the water.

To the **Earth** belong **Gnomes**, **Lemurs**, **Sylphs**, **Mountain-dwellers** <note>original: "Montans"</note>, and **Zonnets**, whose **monsters** are the **Pigmies**.

<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of natural philosophy, completing the word "[begin]ning" to explain the composition of all matter according to alchemical principles.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

[begin]ning of their composition, and also those parts of which they consisted: No body composed by Nature can, by any skill of dissolution <note>original: "dissolving skill"; the alchemical process of breaking a substance down into its basic components</note>, be separated into more or less than *three* parts: namely, into *Mercury* (or liquid), *Sulphur* (or oil), and *Salt*. Every created thing is generated and preserved in these three. For the *Holy Trinity* <note>original: "Triunity"</note>, when it spoke that *triune* word **FIAT** <note>Latin: "Let there be"</note>, created all things as *triune* <note>Having three parts</note>, as is plainly seen in a *spagyric* <note>original: "Spagiricall"; referring to alchemy, specifically the separation and recombination of natural substances</note> analysis.

<margin>That is: a separation of purity from impurity, or truth from falsehood.</margin>

By the word **FIAT** (or "Let there be"), God produced the primary matter, which is threefold in respect to the three principles contained in it. Afterward, these three types are separated into four diverse bodies or elements, just as a skillful artist might make red lead, white lead, glass, and the spirit of lead out of raw lead. So the world, with all the created bodies in it, is nothing more than a vapor or smoke **coagulated** <note>thickened or curdled</note> together from the three substances: Sulphur, Salt, and Mercury. These three are the matter out of which all physical things are created. The *spagyrists* <note>original: "Spagyricks"; alchemists who followed the teachings of Paracelsus</note> can make this plain through visible experience and undeniable certainty.

In green wood, there are also three kinds of moisture. The first is watery, like fleeting Mercury (or quicksilver), which protects the wood from burning. Another is very fat and oily, acting like **brimstone** <note>Sulphur</note> to make it flame and burn; both of these are consumed by fire. The third, namely the Salt, is oily <note>original: "unctuous"</note>, very minimal, thin, and lasting, and it remains behind in the ashes. Thus the Earth, as it is endowed with that threefold substance of Salt, Mercury, and Sulphur, is the cause of the mate- <note>The text continues on the next page: "material."</note>

<summary>This page explains the Paracelsian alchemical theory that all matter is composed of three fundamental principles—Mercury, Sulphur, and Salt—reflecting the Holy Trinity. It uses the example of burning green wood to demonstrate how these three components (liquid, oil, and ash) comprise all physical things.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the three alchemical principles (Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury) as they relate to the composition of the material body of man. The previous page ended with an analogy of green wood containing three types of moisture.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<sig>D</sig>

<margin>When the Salt or Mummy <note>In alchemy, "mummy" refers to the vital essence or life force found in a physical body</note> is used up, things produce nothing but worms.</margin>
...material body of man: The Salt, through coagulation <note>The process of thickening or becoming solid</note>, gives solidity, color, and taste to all bodies. The Sulphur, through a pleasant mixture, tempers the coagulation of the Salt and gives the body substance and the ability to transform <note>original: "Transmutation"</note>. Mercury—which, like the Elixir, provides the virtues, operations, and secrets—nourishes the other two through a diligent and constant supply of vital and vegetative moisture. Because the first two continually grow dry and old through frequent action, Mercury sustains every mixture with its fluid and slippery substance.

These three principles, which are found in all bodies, are entirely distinct in their use and properties because of how their power or operation is mixed, even though to our senses they appear as a single, uniform substance.

<margin>The Spirit of God moving over the face of the waters.</margin>
Some *Theophrasteans* <note>Followers of Theophrastus Paracelsus, a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note>, who have more closely and precisely investigated the causes of hidden things, add a *fourth* principle, which they call the Spirit. Although this spirit can be extracted from minerals and vegetables, in animals it is so subtle that it is bound within them; it cannot be extracted or separated by any artistic skill, and therefore cannot be possessed. Thus, Sulphur (or brimstone) corresponds to Fire, Salt to the Earth, Mercury to Water, and Spirit to Air.

And since we have entered into a discussion of the elements, we shall add a few things concerning them from that short treatise by *Severinus* <note>Petrus Severinus (1541–1602), a Danish physician and influential proponent of Paracelsian ideas</note>. The true and purely spiritual elements are the guardians, nurturers, locations, mines, wombs, and containers of the entire Creation; indeed, they are the very

Knowledge of the Elements cannot be attained without these Seeds, because they declare or reveal the use and services of the Elements. Just as the Seeds are related to the Elements, so the Principles of bodies are related to the Seeds. These Principles, being the inseparable companions of the Seeds, cling to them as if joined by an unbreakable tie; they are furnished with an incomprehensible variety of gifts for the service of Generation <note>The process of creation, growth, and reproduction</note>. For the Seeds and Principles of things receive the strength for reproduction and multiplication from the authority of **His Word**, whose command all things obey. But just as the Seeds and Elements can hardly be separated from one another by even the sharpest intellect, so neither can the Elements and Principles of bodies; the laws of Nature have scarcely ever allowed them to be perfectly separated by any industry of Art <note>Here, "Art" refers to human skill, specifically alchemy or chemistry</note>.

...from the weak, and the pure from the impure.

There are, for our understanding, four <term>Elementated Elements</term> <note>Physical manifestations of the elements, as opposed to their pure spiritual essences</note>, namely: Fire (or the Firmament), Air, Water, and Earth. These conceive, bring forth, and again receive or take back into themselves all things. They are the fruit of the Seeds and the other Elements, which serve the process of generation <note>The process of creation and growth</note> through a constant and perpetual flowing and watering. From the first three are all compound bodies made, and into them they are again resolved <note>Broken back down into their components</note>. These three are found in every matrix <note>A womb or formative environment where something is created</note>, and in every birth from every matrix.

<margin>In living creatures, the bones resemble the earth, the flesh air, the vital spirit fire, and the humors <note>bodily fluids</note> water.</margin>

The Soul in man is a celestial fiery element; the solid and spermatick <note>original: "Spermatick"; meaning the structural parts related to the foundational "seeds" of the body</note> parts are the Earth; the moist parts, such as the blood and other humors, are of the element of Water; the Air is everything that is hollow and without substance. But these things, as we have said, are to be understood as <term>Elementated Elements</term> (for the true Elements are spiritual) because even the smallest seeds strive to imitate the economy <note>The organized system or management</note> of the world and hold forth an obscure resemblance of the Elements and Principles. In this way, we acknowledge that the Elements are in all things, and that they are mingled with and preserved by the <term>Balsam</term> <note>A natural preservative or life-extending essence</note> and <term>Radical Tincture</term> <note>The fundamental vital essence or life-force</note>. Thus, water itself, having the four elements within it, cherishes its seeds with fruitful nourishment and multiplication. This much is from the writings of *Severinus* <note>original: "Severinus"; likely Petrus Severinus, a 16th-century Danish physician and follower of Paracelsus</note>; but lest what he has said should seem obscure to the casual reader, we will now speak more clearly of the Elements.

For the stars support and supply their fruits just as the trees of the Earth do theirs.

Hence it is plain that diseases are not cured by opposites <note>original: "contraries"; a direct challenge to the traditional Galenic medical theory that "contraries cure contraries" (e.g., treating a fever with cooling herbs).</note>—as if heat were meant to expel cold, or as if a person needed the Elements banished and driven out of them. Instead, they are cured by the secret essences or *Astras* which the Chemist can extract <note>original: "reduce"</note> from the "last matter" back into the "first matter" <note>In alchemy, reducing a substance to its "first matter" means stripping away its physical dregs to reach its pure, energetic essence.</note>. These *Arcana* <note>original: "Arcana"; hidden or secret medicinal powers within a substance.</note> or hidden things are actually neither cold nor hot, yet they remove all diseases—just as an axe cuts down a tree while being neither cold nor hot itself. Of this sort are the Fifth Essences <note>original: "Fift Essences"; also known as the Quintessence, the purest and most concentrated part of a substance.</note>, Magisteries <note>In alchemy, a preparation that captures the full power of its source material.</note>, and the like.

So that man, when he rightly understands himself, may in himself, as in a kind of Deified glass <note>original: "glasse"; meaning a mirror reflecting the divine.</note>, behold and understand all things. **No art can be perfected without regeneration**: True philosophy must be grounded on Christ the cornerstone. We ought, therefore, to be most wary that we do not allow the philosophical errors of the heathens to overbear or domineer over the rules of Christian philosophy. Only Christians in whom the Truth is planted—who have their seed from God through the means of regeneration, which the heathens do not have—truly know how to use or teach philosophy without mistake or error, and how to manage all other faculties correctly. Believers shall be taught by God when the Holy Spirit is poured forth.

To be brief, the knowledge of God is the treasury of the whole world wherein all things are stored, so that without this knowledge no one can come to eternal life. For faith, hope, and love follow knowledge; adhesion <note>original: "Adhæsion"; a mystical term for "cleaving" or sticking fast to God in spiritual union.</note> or cleaving to God follows love; union follows adhesion; and in union is blessedness and wisdom. This regeneration that holy man *Hermes* <note>original: "Hermes"; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure from antiquity whose mystical writings were often cited by Renaissance and early modern thinkers to show that even ancient "heathens" had glimpses of divine truth.</note> and others

<meta>This page continues the discussion of man as a microcosm, specifically exploring the "trine" (threefold) nature of human existence—body, spirit, and soul—and how this mirrors the Holy Trinity of the Creator.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>

Just as God is one in essence, yet threefold <note>original: "Trine"</note> (or three) in persons, so man is one in person, but threefold in distinct essences. That is, **he is composed of three parts: a terrestrial <note>original: "Terrene"</note> body, an ethereal spirit of the heavens, and a living, life-giving <note>original: "vivifying"</note> soul which God breathed into him, and which is the house of God.**

1 Thessalonians 5:23.

Paul, the greatest philosopher and theologian <note>original: "Divine"</note>, shows the three parts of man: spirit, soul, and body.
</margin>

The Holy Scripture testifies to this, showing the wonderful agreement between the Creator and the creature. In man, the great Creator wished to show himself to be "unitrine" <note>three-in-one</note> or triune—one in three, or three in one. This is also the unanimous consensus of all those who truly practiced philosophy by the "light of nature" <note>the knowledge of the world gained through observation and reason, rather than divine revelation alone</note>.

If, by chance, there are any who deny these three parts, they must still acknowledge that man was created "from the mud of the earth" <note>original: *è Limo Terra*</note>—out of the clay or dust of the earth—by the word "let it be" <note>original: *FIAT*</note>. They must also acknowledge that he received an eternal spirit or breath of life from the mouth of God, which is the "slime of the heavens" <note>original: *Limum Calorum*</note> from the Lord.

<margin>

The spirit is the life of the soul; the spirit and soul together are the life of the body.
John 14.

The "mud of the earth" <note>original: *Limus Terræ*</note>, or dust of the earth, is twofold: visible and invisible. Man has his body, or "cottage," from the earth and water; but the life that dwells within it is from the air and the firmament of fire. **This life is the sidereal spirit <note>original: "Syderiall Spirit"; the spirit derived from the stars or heavens</note>, and is properly the true man, not the flesh and blood.** Just as the sidereal spirit is the life of the body, so the Spirit of the Lord is the life of the intellectual soul.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of man as a "microcosm" (a little world), explaining how the human soul and spirit serve as a dwelling place for God and mirror the structure of the entire universe.</meta>

<page-num>[ 62 ]</page-num>

<margin>God created man to be His tabernacle <note>a temporary dwelling or tent; a place where God resides</note> both in this life and in the world to come.</margin>
The invisible is himself the firmament and has all things in him. So the Spirit of the Lord—the **WORD** of God, the eternal man—dwells in the soul. The house [the body] is the home of the soul, and the soul is the home and cottage of God. 

Therefore, man is the most perfect completion of all God’s works, the most complete representation of the world, and the exact image of God. In man, God rested from creating, as He had nothing more honorable to create. All the wisdom and power of the Creator were enclosed and perfected in man as the supreme masterpiece <note>original: "artifice"</note>, because he contains within himself everything that is in God. 

When, as I say, man was made on the sixth day, he was composed of all things. He was the last of the creatures and an image not only of the eternal God but also of the "great world" <note>the macrocosm or the entire universe</note>, because he understands and contains all things within himself. For example, in a clear sky, a great cloud suddenly arises, from which come rain, mud, snow, dew, thunder, and hail; though these were "nothing" before they were produced, by being created out of invisible things, they become great physical bodies. God accomplished this in a moment, for His works cannot be delayed by time. All things proceeded out of the invisible darkness and were called into the visible light by the speaking **WORD** and the nurturing Spirit. 

Now, since man received his **sidereal body** <note>A subtle, "star-like" body believed to connect the physical human to the celestial realm</note> from the stars of the firmament, and because the entire imagination of man depends on the stars of the firmament—indeed, is one and the same and remains one with them—it must be that the firmament also possesses an imagination, though it lacks reason, whereas man (the offspring of the world) possesses imagination along with reason.

## CHAPTER II.

### Where that True Medicine is to be Found.

<margin>
Wheat does not grow without a place, nor can we have meal without bran, neither is honey without a sting.

Such Secrets are regenerated without any mixture of qualities.
</margin>
**E**very thing that God created good is extremely perfect and incorruptible, as the heavens are; but whatsoever is found in these sublunary <term>sublunary|meaning "under the moon"—referring to the earthly realm which is subject to decay, unlike the eternal heavens</term> inferior things has a twofold nature: a perfect one and an imperfect one. That is to say, there is a "fifth essence" <term>fift Essence|the quintessence; the purest, most potent medicinal core of a substance</term> and there are dregs, which may be separated from one another by fire. 

Seeing, therefore, that the true medicine is wrapped up in rinds, barks, wombs <note>original: "matrixes"; the environment or vessel in which a substance is formed.</note>, receptacles, husks, garments, and dwellings—just as almonds and all kernels are covered with bark and rind (for nature does not bring forth the kernel of the chestnut without its shell and prickly husk)—it is necessary that the same must be separated from the impure elements by the artificial anatomy <note>In this context, "anatomy" refers to the chemical dissection or breaking down of a substance into its parts.</note> of chemists <note>original: "Chymiſts"; here referring to alchemists who practiced laboratory medicine.</note> before we can reach the pure medicine. For the bonds are loosened by art and industry, and the faculties of healing are set at liberty.

Therefore, in all orders of things that are cherished in the bosom of the elements—namely, in those three families of nature: the *Vegetable*, *Animal*, and *Mineral* (out of which are commonly...

As has been said, this does not consist of the four outward, naked, superficial, and drossy <term>Relollaceous|likely referring to 'recrementitious' or waste matter; the external, non-medicinal parts of a substance</term> qualities (as *Theophrastus* <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> learnedly discusses). Instead, it is a certain specific virtue concealed in the very seeds, naturally occurring without human art. The Creator—that great master craftsman of all things—planted this in every growing thing at the beginning of Creation by the power of that omnipotent *Word*, through which all things were brought out of darkness into light.

<margin>Heaven is the architect of the external structures, not of the secrets, wonders, and mysteries that dwell within them.</margin>

The faculties and virtues with which complex bodies are endowed (like the soul in a human body from the beginning of Creation) do not come from the outside. They are not infused into them by a fleeting alignment of the stars, nor are they made up of a chance meeting of atoms. These virtues do not proceed from the physical body or the mixture of the body's visible form; if they did, they could not be separated without the destruction and corruption of the body and form. We see this in cinnamon and pepper, where the medicinal virtues are either extracted by art or vanish through age and long storage. For just as all natural actions proceed from spirits and spiritual tinctures <term>Tinctures|In alchemy, the essential medicinal or spiritual essence extracted from a substance</term> (in which the practical applications of the Three Principles <term>Three principles|The alchemical triad of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, believed to be the fundamental components of all matter</term> have their strength), so the actions of healing proceed from the spirits and vital essences of those spirits, not from the physical bodies or dead, drossy qualities.

# CHAPTER 3.

## How the True <term>Medicine</term> <note>original: "Physick"</note>, which is covered by bark and rind, is to be extracted and rightly prepared by Fire.

<margin>The <term>physician</term> <note>original: "Physiti-an"</note> perfects the creation of God through the use of fire.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 39:26, etc.</margin>
**A**ll things were created perfect in respect to their original substance <term>first matter</term> <note>In alchemy, the "first matter" is the primordial, unformed base of all things</note>, but the chemist <term>Chymist</term> <note>original: "Chymist"; in this context, an alchemist who uses laboratory processes</note> perfects the final product <term>last matter</term> through the use of fire. This is because nothing in this lower world (which is subject to <term>generation and corruption</term> <note>The natural cycle of birth, growth, decay, and death</note>) is so noble that it does not contain poison within itself—or in relation to something else—situated very close to its essence or medicinal power <term>Physick</term>. In all the primary works of God, where there is harm there is also help; where there is venom, there is virtue. Therefore, nothing was created in vain, but all things were made for some use. For Nature has ordained that Good and Evil, which the earthly <term>sublunary</term> elements <note>The "sublunary elements" are the four elements (earth, water, air, fire) that make up the world below the moon, which were believed to be subject to change and decay</note> produce, should always be joined together in all things, to remind us daily of the fear of God.

...mixture of superficial and external elements, so that that pure and crystalline matter may be administered to our bodies. But to deliver this from prison and captivity, *“This is the work, this is the labor”* <note>original: *Hoc opus, hic labor est*, a famous line from Virgil's Aeneid, referring to the difficulty of returning from the underworld; here used to describe the difficulty of chemical extraction.</note> is a hard task to perform.

It is an honorable calling when the <term>physicians</term> <note>original: "Physitians"</note> live long and are not idle in it; for without this chemical philosophy, all <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "Physick"</note> is but lifeless. Without alchemical skill, there can be no theoretical <note>original: "Speculative"</note> or practical medicine. He who rejects that knowledge, being disheartened by the difficulty of it, shall never find where the disease lies. In this, therefore, our common sort of physicians are not to be followed, who shield their sloth under the labor and study of other men, and are accustomed to leave the preparation of their medicines most commonly to some careless and greedy <note>original: "covetous"</note> apothecary, to the great damage of their patients. I speak not against the conscientious apothecaries who, by their faithful diligence, serve the commonwealth as the alchemy of *Vulcan* <note>The Roman god of fire and metalworking, used here as a metaphor for the transformative power of the chemist's furnace.</note>.

By this artificial breakdown <note>original: "resolution," the process of separating a substance into its component parts.</note> of bodies, the properties which previously lay hidden within their compositions are now brought to light. By it also, as if by a certain kind of artificial Cynosure <note>original: "Tynosure," referring to the North Star or a guiding principle.</note> or celestial patterns, the chemists have not only drawn back curtains extending to all the borders of Nature, but also reached the very wonder, contemplation, and perception of the whole of creation <note>original: "the whole Creature"</note> and of every hidden <note>original: "obstruce," likely meaning abstruse or concealed.</note> virtue within it, and have attained a noble knowledge in most things; and not without cause. Therefore, a physician should be well-practiced in this true

Analysis and vital anatomy of bodies (as has been said) is necessary because there is no constant quality of any body which is not to be found either in the Salt, or Mercury, or Sulphur of that same body.

<margin>
Vegetables <note>In this period, "vegetables" referred to the entire plant kingdom.</note> include plants, trees, and zoophytes <note>original: "Zoophyts"; organisms like coral or sea sponges that appear to be halfway between plants and animals.</note>. Animals are the beasts in their order: creeping, swimming, flying, and four-footed creatures.
</margin>

But first, all compound bodies of the lower globe <note>The Earthly realm, as opposed to the celestial spheres.</note> are to be divided into three orders or categories: into animals, vegetables, and minerals. The individuals of all these, and the parts of those individuals, are to be diligently examined; in doing so, we shall find the notable differences of the "Three First Things" (namely: Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury) in every particular order. For in the workshop of Nature, there is animal, vegetable, and mineral Salt; vegetable, animal, and mineral Sulphur; and vegetable, animal, and mineral Mercury.

The first face of things was pure, sound, and perfect, without corruption and death. For the sake of His infinite glory, the great and all-working God created all things good by His will, so that all things might glorify Him and live holily and incorruptibly according to the prescribed order.

<margin>
<insert>contrast</insert>
</margin>

Man was at first created healthy (sickness entered through the Woman, not through the Man), but when he came into the world, he found an entrance unto death because there appeared two contraries: the external corruptible and the internal complete <note>original: "compleat," meaning perfect or whole.</note>, which could not long continue as one without unavoidable corruption. Therefore, after the transgression and the Fall from unity to alterity <note>The state of being "other" or different; here referring to the move from divine oneness into the fragmented and decaying material world.</note>, by the curse of God, new [tinctures...] <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword for the next page is "Tinctures."</note>

<summary>The author explains that all matter consists of the three chemical principles—Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury—across the three kingdoms of nature, and argues that death and decay resulted from the Fall of Man, which introduced "alterity" and corruption into a previously perfect world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Three First Things" (Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury) and how the Fall of Man introduced corruption into the previously pure state of nature, necessitating the study of chemistry to extract purity from the now-tainted world.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

<margin>
The innate mixture <note>original: <term>κρᾶσις</term> (krasis), meaning a blending of qualities or humors.</note>
The displacement of the Created being occurs through the calamity and the arrival of sickness upon it. Since the Fall, we carry within our own bosoms the enemy of both our creation and our reproduction, which causes sickness and death through in-bred conflict and corruption.
</margin>

Corrupting qualities <note>original: "Tinctures"; in Paracelsian medicine, these are spiritual or physical essences infused into matter.</note> entered in—even infinite evils—and through their mixing with the miserable state of our life, along with that troublesome companion, the world, the beauty of the whole Creation was displaced. Impurity was joined to the pure roots, which was the foreordained source of diseases. For the roots of sicknesses in a certain individual or species do not exist separately on their own, but are implanted and mixed into the pure and original seeds of things. However, the nourishment of natural things consists of the fruits of those seeds which grow up in the four "wombs" or Elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note>.

Nature, therefore, as it is now, gives us nothing in the world that is pure, but has mixed all things with many impurities. This serves as a "spur of necessity," frequently reminding us that we should begin to learn the knowledge of chemistry <note>original: "Chymistry"; at this time, chemistry and alchemy were intertwined sciences of transformation.</note> from our very cradles. As long as we are shut out of Paradise and living in the outskirts of this world, we ought to till and cultivate the EARTH—that is, the whole frame of the world—through wonder, investigation, and knowledge of both the Visible and Invisible Earth (or "clay" <note>original: *Limus*, referring to the biblical dust or slime from which Man was formed.</note>). We should labor to obtain our bread and other necessities for this present life as Nature’s laborers—not lazily, but by the sweat of our brows. By this means, and by taking up the Cross which we should bear with patience, our industry might be stirred up in this **LAND of LABOR** to attain the fruits of earthly and celestial wisdom, lest base and sluggish idleness make us waste away.

<summary>This page explains that because the Fall introduced impurities and disease into the "seeds" of all things, nature is no longer pure. Consequently, humanity must practice "Chymistry" and diligent labor to extract sustenance and wisdom from a corrupted world.</summary>

...and pine away, or (because we are more prone to all kinds of sin and vice) by doing nothing, we should learn to behave wickedly.

<margin>He who has learned to know God and himself has managed the Earth with good stewardship <note>original: "husbandry"</note>; through too much self-indulgence, men grow worse and become like brute beasts. However, idleness—the pillow and cushion of the Devil—is removed by labor and diligence.</margin>

And this is the true purpose of Mankind's Creation: that in the fear of God and love of one's neighbor, one should manage the Earth, recovering what has been lost. We should not be idle; instead, by walking in the Light of Nature—not following our own will, but according to the will of our Creator—we should remain the instrument, home, and dwelling place <note>original: "Tabernacle"</note> of God. We must walk in the ways of the Lord to avoid evil and idle thoughts. Through Nature, we should explore the wonderful works of God by studying earthly <note>original: "Temporall"</note> and heavenly <note>original: "Cælestiall"</note> observations. In this way, we make known the invisible works of God, celebrating the infinite wisdom, power, and perpetual goodness of the Creator in admiration of the marvelous works, wonders, and mysteries which he has revealed.

But to move from food, let us turn to medicine <note>original: <term>Physick</term></note>. Regarding this, no one is so foolish <note>original: "sottish"</note> or stubborn—unless they would rather eat the husks and shells along with the kernel, as earlier and more primitive generations have done—that they would dislike this "separatory art" <note>The practice of chemistry or alchemy, used to extract pure essences from raw matter.</note>. This art teaches us how to rightly distinguish and separate the good from the bad, the profitable from the unprofitable, and the dulling <note>original: "Stupefactive"</note> substances from the pure. It separates the mineral spirit from the flower <note>original: "Anthos" (Greek: <term>ἄνθος</term>); in alchemy, the "blossom" or essence of a substance.</note>, the uniform substances <note>original: "Homogeneans"</note> from the diverse ones <note>original: "Heterogeneans"</note>, poison from healing medicines and balms, light from darkness, life from death, and day from night.

Putrefactions <note>original: "Putrefications"; the process of decay or breaking down substances</note> are the only true correctives of all medicine <note>original: <term>Physick</term></note>.
Just as death separates eternal and perishing things, so fire separates the good from the bad, and the quintessence <note>original: <term>Quint-Essence</term></note> from the body.

[This art separates] the visible from the invisible; that which is pure and heavenly <note>original: "Celestial"</note>, the kernel and marrow, from that which is earthly <note>original: "Terrestrial"</note> and impure—the rinds, membranes, covers, shells, husks, and dregs. These are the outer "coat and cottage" of medicine <note>original: <term>Physick</term></note> which are enemies to the human body. We separate these from the Soul, its inhabitant: the super-elemental mystery known as the Quintessence <note>The "fifth essence," thought to be the purest and most potent part of a substance</note>. This is the true internal, suitable, friendly, and matching balsam <note>original: "BalSom"; a healing or soothing agent</note> of our bodies. In this way, at last, that life-giving <note>original: "quickening"</note> essence may be obtained. Its power <note>original: "faculty"</note> rises higher and faster once it is loosened from its chains, producing far greater virtue and more effective results than it did before. <margin>Harmony in contraries</margin> All venomous things contain a balsam agreeable to human nature, and there is no poisonous creature that does not have within it an antidote against its own poison. In its own way, every creature is good; though it may be poison to man, it is often common food for another creature. Spiders are good for hens and sparrows, toads for serpents, and serpents for stags and storks. However, these forms of medicine work better when they are extracted rather than when they are drowned in "matter," which always hinders and restrains the power and operation of the secret <note>The "secret" refers to the hidden medicinal property or essential power within a substance</note>.

Even the Soul or "Form" <note>In the philosophy of the time, the "Form" was the essential nature or life-force that gives a thing its identity</note> of every kind of thing has not only more, but far more excellent virtues and operations than either the physical body or the raw matter of that same thing. Since everything receives its being from its Form, the more of the Form it possesses, the more "Entity" <note>True existence or being</note> it has. And this...

<meta>This page continues the argument for the superiority of chemically refined (Spagyric) medicines over traditional, bulky, and often toxic herbal mixtures.
