Even the very enemies of **Chemistry** <note>original: "Chymistry"</note> are forced to admit this, compelled by their own conscience and convinced by what they have seen with their own eyes <note>original: "ocular demonstration"</note>.

->Hence many advantages follow.<-

<margin>Reasons why Spagyric medicine <note>original: "Spagyrick Physick"; medicine prepared through chemical extraction and purification</note>, when properly prepared, is preferred over the other irregular <note>original: <term>Hetroclite</term></note> mixtures found in shops.</margin>
First, because many sick people will take it more easily and willingly. Sometimes patients are so reluctant that they would rather die than drink a great amount of those muddy, puddle-like potions <note>original: "pudly potions"</note> which ruin a person's physical constitution <note>original: "complexion"</note>. These are mixtures that even the physician who prescribed them and the apothecary <note>a person who prepared and sold medicines</note> who mixed them would hate and refuse to drink themselves in a similar situation.

Second, though these medicines are used frequently, they do not harm the stomach. Nothing prevents them from doing their work; they quickly affect the body and are processed by it. Because they are already refined <note>original: "separate"</note>, they are carried by natural force without hindrance to their intended destinations <note>original: "known lodgings"</note> in the body. This prevents the harsh, earthy parts from sticking to the internal organs <note>original: "inwards"</note> and causing ulcers or making those who take them frequently sickly, as happens with the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> use of ordinary medicines.

Third, because any harmful qualities are completely removed from, or at least easily controlled within, these **Essences** by mixing them <note>original: "permixture"</note> with other most excellent properties. It cannot be denied that this **Spagyric** art is so necessary that no physician can be considered safe without it. Often, in a single simple substance <note>an ingredient, usually a plant, used on its own for medicinal purposes</note>, there are different components, sometimes with contrary qua— <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("qualities"), continuing on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page argues that Spagyric (chemical) medicines are superior to traditional potions because they are easier for patients to swallow, less damaging to the stomach and organs, and more refined by the removal of harmful "earthy" impurities.</summary>

<page-num>100</page-num>

qualities, of which one may be harmful and the rest healthful, as we see in **opium** and **honey**. These can be known in no other way except by the separation of their substances, which cannot be done without this Art <note>the "Art" refers to Chemistry or Alchemy</note>. By this Art alone, the followers of **Galen** <note>original: "Galenists"; traditional physicians who relied on the ancient Greek system of balancing bodily humors</note> may prove their principles <note>original: "Axioms"</note>. They claim that all bitter things are "hot" in nature; yet although **opium** is exceedingly bitter, its **numbing power** <note>original: "stupefactive vertue"</note> is what dominates. **Roses** and **chicory** <note>original: "Succory"</note>, though they are bitter, are also used as cooling agents. The knot of this contradiction must be cut with an **anatomical knife**—**fire**; for through this, we come to understand the "temperature" <note>the specific balance of qualities like hot, cold, moist, or dry</note> of single herbal ingredients <note>original: "simples"</note> by separating their substances. Thus, we find that in **opium** there is a sweet, **narcotic sulfur** <note>In alchemical theory, "Sulfur" represents the soul or combustible principle of a substance</note>, and a bitter, hot **salt** that penetrates through a fine breakdown <note>original: "subtile resolution"</note> and causes sweating without any **numbing power**.

<margin>Poison reduced to its **essential essence** <note>original: "Arcanum"</note> is not poison, but a sovereign medicine. In this way, the base metals <note>original: "earthy Planets"</note> are healed of their "leprosy" <note>impurities or corrosion</note>, and foul smells are turned into sweet fragrances by the process of chemical heating <note>original: "digestion"</note>.</margin>

And what is much to be admired is that, although the poison of metals is most deadly, it may be so corrected and improved by the help of this Art and of **fire** that it may be taken into the whole body without danger. Skilled physicians know this well; they know how to pick and choose the best things out of the worst. This is plain by the example of **arsenic**, which, despite its unruly toxicity <note>original: "malignity"</note>, is tamed artificially with **saltpeter** <note>potassium nitrate</note> by the assistance of **fire**.

**Minerals**, whose spirits exceed ours in refinement <note>original: "subtilty"</note>, and precious stones, ought to be considered medicinal. When they are rightly prepared, they far excel all plant-based medicines <note>original: "Vegetables"</note> in performing cures.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument for the medicinal superiority of minerals and metals over plant-based remedies, focusing on their stability and concentrated power.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<sig>H 3</sig>

**First**, Because so great a power <note>original: "vertue"</note> and such strong operations cannot be imprinted upon such soft matter as that of plants and living things, so as to retain and hold such an impression. Nor can it be that vegetables, which are prone to decay <note>original: "obnoxious to corruption"</note>, should free a man's body from decay as effectively as the spirits of perfect metals <note>the "noble" metals, gold and silver</note> do, which are not subject to decay.

**Secondly**, It is clear that minerals and imperfect metals <note>base metals like lead, copper, or iron</note> are endowed with great medicinal powers, as appears in surgical medicines <note>original: "Chyrurgical medicines"</note>; there is hardly any ointment prepared that is not made of an imperfect metal or mineral. Therefore, perfect things have undoubtedly received far greater force and powers from the Almighty.

**Thirdly**, Because Nature—which desires to bring forth living things and plants fit not for just one action alone but for many functions and performances—could by no means so balance the mixture of those bodies that they should attain such admirable power and possess the solid and stable nature of a balsam <note>original: "Balſom"; a concentrated, healing essence that preserves against decay</note>.

**Fourthly**, Because stones require a long time to form, but perfect bodies are generated in a shorter space. Nature, therefore, by the length of time, can adorn precious stones and other metallic <note>original: "metallary"</note> bodies with a greater faculty of working, for they are not distracted with the variety of sensory and physical functions <note>original: "sensible and movable offices"</note>.

**Add also**, That precious stones are more to be commended than others for their excellent balance and splendor, which in the **Bohemian Garnet** <note>original: "Bohemian Garnat"</note>...

<summary>This page lists four reasons why minerals and metals are medicinally superior to plants, arguing that their lack of biological decay and their concentrated nature make them more potent "balsams" for the human body.</summary>

The <term>Garnet</term> <note>original: "Garnat"</note> is such that it can hardly be spoiled or corrupted by any force of fire, and that is only because of the stability <note>original: "fixation of spirits"</note> that may be seen in it. All things come from one principle and return to one. But when that one unique Nature—the essence and matter of all things—came forth upon the stage of this world by the pleasure of God, it brought with it various wonderful bodies of many distinctions. The Soul of the world is a kind of united life, filling, gathering and knitting together all things, that of the three sorts of Creatures—the Intellectual <note>spiritual or angelic beings</note>, Celestial <note>the stars and planets</note>, and Corruptible <note>earthly things that die and decay</note>—it might make up one Mass and frame of the whole world, by the virtue which it hath from the Ideas or pattern it maketh all things both natural and artificial to be fruitful.
</margin>

All things flow from one Fountain, which after they have done their work and quitted the Stage of Vanity <note>the temporary and often illusory world of mortal life</note>, are returned to their own places, where they are blessed with unchangable Rest: That universal Spirit which liveth in and quickeneth this whole Mass, which worketh all in all, and filleth the whole world; that Power of God, which comprehendeth all the world in it, *Agrippa* <note>Henry Cornelius Agrippa, a famous Renaissance magician and philosopher</note> calleth it the subject of all <term>mirability</term> <note>the quality of being wondrous or capable of performing miracles</note>, the <term>Being</term> <note>original Latin: "Ens"</note> that cannot be comprehended by sense; *Avicen* <note>Avicenna, the Persian polymath and philosopher</note> saith, it is the Soul of the world poured into all things, building on the authority of *Plato*, the *Arabians*, and *Chaldeans*; but this we must hearken to without any superstition or Idolatrous worship, giving honor only to **One God**, and ascribing to him his glory, which we will not give to another.

Nature, I say, is that medium which by an Harmonical consent <note>a state of resonance or agreement between different levels of existence</note> joineth the lowest things to the highest and sometimes is called Animal, sometimes Vegetable, sometimes Mineral, according to the diversity of the subject or <term>receptacle</term> <note>the physical form or body that "holds" the universal spirit</note>, and doth often work even to amazement in the three Families of Nature, as hath been manifested, among other things, by a

<margin>by infusing into them hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> qualities which are commonly called the Quintessences <note>original: "Fifth Essences"</note>.</margin>

This Spirit has now assumed a natural body; it is that which first moves and rules Nature in all natural things. It preserves all things, and all inferior things are governed by it through a kind of <term>Harmonical consent</term> <note>the idea that all parts of the universe are in sympathetic resonance or agreement with one another</note>.

*Albertus Magnus* <note>a 13th-century German friar and philosopher famous for his work in the natural sciences</note> writes that in his time, gold was found in the bodies and heads of some who were hanged. In his *Book of Minerals*, he says that gold may be found everywhere: "There is nothing," he says, "composed of the four elements in which gold is not found..."

If the ancients did not know something that they heard was known to others—even in very distant countries—they would risk all the hazards of sea and land to find it out. 

Undoubtedly, if *Galen* <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity</note> had lived at the same time as *Paracelsus* did, he would not have envied him, but would have reverenced his learning. Above all, he would have wanted to learn the preparations of those high Masteries <note>original: "Magisteryes"; highly concentrated medicinal substances</note> and Elixirs. He would not have refused to blow the coals, mix the ingredients, or watch the work. 

He would have accepted any condition as long as he could have gained this worthy science. He would have ignored the rage of the bad-tempered <note>original: "Colerick"</note> and gloomy <note>original: "Melancolick"</note> Phileraftian <note>likely a derogatory term for traditionalist physicians who followed ancient Greek authorities exclusively</note> physicians, who have not even learned the ABCs of <term>Spagyric Medicine</term> <note>original: "Spagyrick Physick"; medicine produced through alchemical separation and recombination</note>. He will never suffer those secrets to be known—especially in these corrupt and perilous times, when honor and baseness, virtue and vice, and truth and lies are equally esteemed by the malicious world.

For many years, I have noticed that many have begun well but, deluded by these toys, have come to a bad end.

By this means, this **divine spagyry** <note>The spagyric art is the alchemical method of preparing medicines by separating and recombining the essential components of natural substances</note>—the most wonderful and approved art, though for many ages suspected of the greatest uncertainty and most abominable deceit—has been very much disparaged by the unrefined <note>original: "ruder ſort"</note> (as often happens to the best things) and cast aside as contemptible. This includes other deeper sciences, even by those who intend to practice the same business, who undervalue it as being unfit to earn a living. What is said or spoken against abuse, imposture, and wicked arts is not a reason why things that are otherwise good may or ought to be condemned. For what thing is there that will not turn to man's hurt or ruin if it is abused? Thus, the better a thing is, the more destructive its abuse becomes.

<meta>This page begins a new chapter, transitioning from the previous page's philosophical exhortations to a specific medical discussion regarding the differing theories of healing proposed by Hippocrates and Paracelsus.</meta>

## CHAPTER 4.

### How, and by what power <note>original: "virtue"; referring to the inherent medicinal property or efficacy of a substance</note>, medicine <note>original: "physic"</note> acts upon the human body and cures its diseases.

<margin>In the book *On Winds* <note>original: "de flatibus"; a treatise attributed to Hippocrates</note>.</margin>
**W**hat **physicians** commonly argue and debate is **whether**, according to **Hippocrates's** **maxim**, **CONTRARIES** are to be cured with **CONTRARIES**, or **Like** with **LIKE**, according to **Paracelsus**?
**Note** that these **maxims** may both be accepted within the **anatomy** of **nature**, though they seem to contradict one another. Thus, many fail to understand the **minds** of the **philosophers**—which to them seem to be in conflict—because they do not know how to reconcile them through a timely and suitable interpretation. For **medicine** <note>original: "physic"</note> is nothing other than an opposition of those things that are desired: namely, a restoring of the strength and **balsam** <note>original: "Balosom"; in Paracelsian medicine, this refers to the internal "mummy" or life-force that preserves the body from decay</note>, and a removing of excesses or impurities that cause diseases. ***Paracelsus***, therefore, does not find fault with ***Hippocrates*** for saying that hunger is to be cured with food, thirst with drink, and fullness with eva- <margin><insert>contrast</insert></margin> <note>The text cuts off here at the word "evacuation."</note>

<summary>This page introduces a debate between the medical philosophies of Hippocrates (healing by contraries) and Paracelsus (healing by likes), suggesting that both can be reconciled through a proper understanding of nature's internal "balsam."</summary>

<keywords>Hippocrates, Paracelsus, medicine, physic, contraries, balsam, anatomy of nature, Greek medicine, alchemical medicine</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the principles of healing, moving from the comparison of Hippocrates and Paracelsus to a critique of Galen's interpretation of "contraries."</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<sig>I 2</sig>

-evacuation; emptiness <note>original: "inanition"</note> with fullness <note>original: "repletion"</note>, labor with rest, idleness with labor; and generally, that contraries cure contraries. But he is point-blank against *Galen*, who applied that principle of contrariety from *Hippocrates* chiefly to those bare and naked qualities which *Hippocrates* himself utterly disclaimed. For *Galen*, by an unhappy mistake, referred the first and principal ideas of cures to cooling <note>original: "refrigerations"</note>, heating <note>original: "calefactions"</note>, moistening <note>original: "humectations"</note>, and drying <note>original: "exsiccations"</note>, along with their related actions.

The only and exclusive powers <note>original: "NATURES"</note> of medical <note>original: "physicall"</note> remedies, as has been said—or those Hippocratic virtues—are what perform the cures; the physician is but the minister or servant. This very same Nature, which is our Life and Balsam (or balsamic mummy <note>In Paracelsian medicine, "mummy" refers to the vital life-force inherent in the body that preserves it from decay</note>), preserves the body from all corruption. It does this by means of the saline moisture—that is, the lower balsam that springs up in the lower realm from the higher realm. 

I say, this very Nature of ours is its own proper physician within the human body. Sometimes it works wonders even when physicians—to their great disgrace and the shame of their profession—could do no good and have left their patients to their dire predictions <note>original: "prognosticks"</note>. This internal Nature asks nothing of the external physician except restoration <note>original: "instauration"</note> (or, as it is commonly called, fortification) applied to the diseased part by a most pure medicine when the body’s own nature is insufficient. And thus, the medicinal balsam acts like an assistant <note>original: "coadjutor"</note> or private counselor, assisting the vital, radical, and natural balsam because of the sympathy and common agreement between

...them. In this way, it recovers the natural decayed strength. Once this strength is restored, it is capable—as an internal and unknown antidote—of chasing all its enemies out of its kingdom <note>original: "Monarchy"</note> by the sole power of the <term>vital faculties</term> <note>The inherent powers of the living body to function, sustain life, and heal itself.</note>.

To attempt to cure a disease with contrary qualities is to provoke internal disturbances <note>original: "intrinsick commotions"</note> that completely overthrow Nature, which is already too weakened and exhausted by internal conflicts <note>original: "intestine quarrells"</note>.

Furthermore, opposites <note>original: "Contraryes"</note> will not willingly accept one another. If they do not join together, work upon one another, or interact with each other, then there is no true action and reaction <note>original: "action and passion"</note>. Where there is no interaction, no true natural effect can follow.

Therefore, medicines should not be contrary to the afflicted part, but very similar to it. They should have the same external nature as the internal nature of the affected area, because of the <term>harmony</term> <note>The idea that the human body and the universe are perfectly synchronized.</note> between the great and little world. In this way, the internal nature that stands in need of help may be both strengthened and assisted by the abundance of that external nature. 

Man is therefore called a <term>microcosm</term> <note>A "little world"; the belief that a human being is a miniature representation of the entire universe.</note> or "little world," because the whole world preserves, nourishes, and cures him. When the "fruits" of the Earth, Air, Water, and Fire within the microcosm are sick, they must be restored by similar "fruits" from the <term>macrocosm</term> <note>The "great world"; the universe or environment outside the human body.</note>. Thus Nature strengthens and helps its own nature. For Nature, when strengthened and assisted by its own kind, more forcefully drives out all its

enemies, seeing that every Nature is naturally the best preserver of itself. Thus, Nature is not only our companion but our friend and ready helper. It alone is the true <note>original: "genuine"</note> physician of all diseases, as *Galen* <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), a Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> testifies in the 13th book of his *Method of Healing* <note>original: "Method"</note>. It is the "First Mover" of every cure; without its strength and vigor, all medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note> is in vain and serves no purpose. When Nature remains in its proper balance <note>original: "Temperature"</note>, it is medicinal in itself. It heals its own infirmities by its innate <term>mummy</term> <note>In 17th-century medicine, "mummy" referred to a vital, healing substance or life force inherent in living bodies.</note>. When that inward nature is not the medicine, all diseases prove fatal. 

It is well enough known that everything, by a kind of natural instinct, desires to be perfected and preserved. On the contrary, it most vehemently hates <note>original: "abhorre"</note> its own destruction and desires as much as possible to be kept from it. Daily experience makes this plain, such as when any part of our body is wounded with a sword or other weapon. Those who are wounded perceive immediate <note>original: "presentaneous"</note> relief <note>original: "succour"</note> from Nature, as if someone were hastening to help. Nature is so tireless <note>original: "unwearyed"</note> that it will never be at rest until it has first cured the ugly wound and restored the wounded part to its former health <note>original: "soundnesse"</note>. 

And while some say that opposites <note>original: "contraryes"</note> are to be cured by opposites, they are not entirely mistaken—if they do not focus on the physical qualities, but rather the powers <note>original: "vertues"</note> of a contrary nature. For there are as many harmful and opposing powers to Nature as there are good and healthful ones. The goodness of these [healthful powers] is always busy with, and consists of, the preservation of Nature, just as the continual malice of the others is to...

However, contrary qualities <note>Traditional Galenic medicine focused on balancing "qualities" like hot, cold, moist, and dry. The author argues that simply opposing these is less effective than using "virtues."</note> are not rooted out by contrary qualities; instead, they are irritated and provoked to conflict with each other. Through this discord, Nature is weakened more than strengthened, because Nature is not a mere quality but a virtue. She chooses to be assisted by virtues rather than qualities when she seeks to prevail and fight against her cruel enemy. For it is not the physician who hunts out the disease, but Nature herself (who is the <term>Internal Mummy</term> <note>In 17th-century Paracelsian medicine, "mummy" referred to a vital, healing life force inherent in living bodies.</note> or inward balsam) who expels all evil contrary to herself. When her own inward strength fails, she is to be supplied with outward help by her servant, the physician.

<margin>
The inward physician does the work when the human physician fails.
</margin>

Indeed, sometimes the best medicine may be to use no medicine at all, but to leave the operation to the sole <term>Archaeus</term> <note>original: "Archæus"; in Paracelsian thought, this is the "inner alchemist" or vital force that governs the body's functions and health.</note> or Art of Nature. For the nature of the inner body cures more diseases than the physician does with all his medicines. Therefore, if anyone is saved from a raging plague <note>original: "pestilence"</note> by opium—which is considered extremely "cold"—it is not because of the coldness of the opium. Rather, it is because of the "specific poisonous virtue" of the opium, which possesses a higher degree of medicinal power <note>original: "poyson"</note> than the particular venomous power of that plague.

Thus Nature destroys one poison by another; it subdues a weak evil with a stronger one and fights against her enemy with both healthful and harmful weapons. In this way, she keeps her own territory safe and drives her enemy out of her possession by any means necessary. Just as winter does not destroy summer, nor summer winter, but instead one gives way to the other, so one quality does not destroy another. For without virtue <note>original: "vertue"; meaning the inherent power or essence of a thing</note>, a quality is dead and purely incidental <note>original: "accidentall"; in historical philosophy, an "accident" is a non-essential property or outward symptom rather than the thing's true nature</note>. Consequently, it cannot provide any life or substance, which a medicine must necessarily do if it is to truly help Nature.

It is also worth noting here that the roots of diseases in the human body are neither "hot" nor "cold" by nature. However, because nothing can exist without heat, the disease is also said to be either hot or cold, even though those external symptoms and byproducts <note>original: "Accidents and Excrements"</note> are merely signs of the disease, and not the disease itself. For the most destructive diseases and "traitors" of the body do not arise from the physical matter of the body or from the four humors <note>Traditional medicine focused on the balance of four fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note>. Instead, they come from the nature of the "Seed," or the <term>Astras</term> <note>original: "Aſtra’s"; referring to celestial or star-like influences within the body that govern health</note> and invisible <term>mechanic spirits</term> <note>Spiritual forces that act as internal builders or craftsmen within the body</note> of the <term>Three Principles</term> <note>In Paracelsian alchemy, these are Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury—the fundamental building blocks of all matter</note>. These spirits also build their outward "house" and habitation using physical shells. 

These "forgers" and invisible stars of disease were unknown to the ancient doctors. <term>Physic</term> <note>original: "Phyſick"; the art of medicine or the medicine itself</note> is a spirit, not a physical body, which only a "magician" or wise man can perceive. Therefore, the physical "body" or "earth" of <term>simples</term> <note>Individual medicinal herbs or plants used as ingredients</note> should be discarded, and only its inherent power, "heaven," or <term>Astrum</term> <note>original: "Aſtrum"; Latin for "star," meaning the celestial essence or spiritual power of a substance</note> should be taken. For in the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>original: "Microcoſm"; the human being viewed as a miniature version of the entire universe</note> and in medicine, it is necessary

...that the life should work upon the life, and the Spirit upon the Spirit, through the separation of the impure body—just as the intangible Sun melts snow and causes it to vanish away. Such is the nature of all "Secrets" <note>original: "Secrets"; in this context, it refers to *arcana*, or the hidden, concentrated medicinal powers of a substance</note>: they work without physical matter or a body because diseases themselves are not physical bodies. This is the true and living Anatomy. This "Mechanic" <note>original: "Mechanick"; refers to the *Archeus*, the internal formative spirit or "inner chemist" that Paracelsus believed directed the body's functions</note> and Forger of diseases must be subdued and destroyed at its root and origin, just as a whole tree cannot be destroyed by cutting a branch, but only by destroying the seed. Thus, the Mechanic Forger—or the principle of a **fever's** <note>original: "Feare's"; likely an OCR error or archaic spelling for "Feaver's" (fever), though Paracelsus did believe emotional states like fear could generate physical illness</note> generation—has its home in the root, not in the branch. Similarly, the grass that grows of its own accord is prevented from growing not by uprooting <note>original: "evultion"; from the Latin *evulsio*, meaning to pull or pluck out</note> but by the corruption of the earth itself.

<margin>See the first Tract of the second book of *Great Surgery*. Degrees and complexions are not considered in diseases.</margin>
When the center, root, and seed of diseases are plucked up and removed, the work is finished. It is not the smoke arising from a fire, but the fire itself that must be quenched. A physician who cures by "complexion" <note>original: "complexion"; the specific balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) in an individual</note> is like someone who would try to extinguish a harmless flame while ignoring the fire in the coals. That which grows from the seed should not be mistaken for the disease itself; rather, in performing a cure, the root of the seed, which contains the medicinal virtues, must be taken in hand.

<margin>In his book *Of Ancient Medicine*.</margin>
When *Paracelsus* says that "like things preserve their like, and are destroyed by their opposites," he does not mean the "first" or "second" qualities <note>the primary qualities of hot, cold, moist, and dry used in traditional Galenic medicine</note>, which he always calls **recollatitious** <note>original: "Recollaceous"; likely referring to secondary, non-essential, or "collected" qualities rather than the internal essence</note> and invalid. Instead, he means the substance—or at least the **Chironic** <note>original: "Cherionie"; likely referring to the *Chironic* power (named after the centaur Chiron), used by Paracelsus to describe the essential healing power within a medicine</note> or Hippocratic powers and virtues—as appears in the 18th chapter of the first tract of the second part.

...of his *Great Surgery* <note>original: "Chyrurgye"</note>, and in other places. Similar things are said to be the remedies for diseases because they share the same <term>anatomy of nature</term> <note>the shared structural and spiritual blueprint between the universe and the human body</note>, and because they possess similar <term>signatures</term> <note>physical characteristics that indicate a plant's or mineral's medicinal use</note>, qualities, and roots. That "like is preserved by like" should be understood in this way: namely <note>original: "viz."; an abbreviation for the Latin *videlicet*</note>, that the <term>salt</term>, <term>sulphur</term>, and <term>mercury</term> <note>the *Tria Prima*, or three primary substances Paracelsus believed composed all matter</note> of the <term>microcosm</term> <note>the individual human being</note> is preserved by the <term>salt</term>, <term>sulphur</term>, and <term>mercury</term> of the <term>macrocosm</term> <note>the great world or universe</note> when they correspond to one another. And just as in the microcosm there are various types of sulphur (one for the head, another for the heart, etc.) and various mercuries and salts, there is also a corresponding variety of sulphur, salt, and mercury found in herbs and minerals within the great world. 

The use of fire <note>referring to alchemy or chemical laboratory work</note> reveals their agreement, operation, and differences. Because he <note>referring to Paracelsus</note> classifies all physical diseases according to those three substances from which our bodies are made, and according to the waste products <note>original: "excrementitious superfluities"</note> resulting from food and drink, he categorizes them accordingly. Those diseases in the human body which arise from kindled and flaming <term>sulphur</term>—as sulphur may be suppressed or intensified in four ways, namely by the four <term>elements</term>—he called <term>sulphurean</term> diseases; such are all inflammations and fevers. Those that result from moisture he called <term>mercurial</term>.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Paracelsus's medical theories, specifically detailing how the three primary substances—Mercury, Salt, and Sulphur—lead to various diseases when they are altered by heat, digestion, or celestial influence.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>

Mercury can be raised above its natural level in three ways: by the heat of an accidental digestive power <note>original: "Vertue"</note>, by the heat of exercise and labor, or by heat coming from the stars <note>original: "Astra's"</note>. Through the heat of digestion, it is distilled, and so causes all types of <term>apoplexies</term> <note>sudden loss of consciousness or motion, often associated with strokes</note>. Through the heat of exercise, it is <term>sublimed</term> <note>an alchemical process where a substance is heated into a vapor; here, it refers to the substance rising to the head</note>, leading to madness and frenzy. Through the heat of the stars, it is <term>precipitated</term> <note>turned from a liquid or vapor into a solid deposit</note>, which causes gout in the hands and feet, etc., resulting from eating and drinking things that contain too much <term>tartar</term> <note>original: "Tattar"; calcified deposits or impurities believed to settle in the body and cause blockage</note>.

He calls those diseases born from salt "saline" and "nitrous." Salt destroys health in four ways: by <term>resolution</term> <note>dissolving into liquid</note>, <term>calcination</term> <note>burning a substance until it turns to powder</note> (thereby losing its liquid and moist balance <note>original: "Temperament"</note>), <term>reverberation</term> <note>a method of intense heating in a furnace</note>, and <term>alkalization</term>. These processes breed grievous diseases such as ulcers, scabs, <term>tetters</term> <note>a general term for skin diseases like eczema, psoriasis, or herpes</note>, ringworms, the itch, and the like; all of these are diseases arising from salt that has been dissolved. Overindulgence <note>original: "Surfeiting"</note> and gluttony, which destroy digestion, are the causes of the salt's destruction. Excess and luxury dissolve and melt the salt of man. The stars also push the salt out of its proper state.

This salt can be changed and turned into all kinds of salts, and as the <term>transmutation</term> <note>the transformation of one substance into another</note> occurs, so does the disease. He says, therefore, that the <term>sulphur</term> ignited in the human body is to be quenched with the sulphur of the "great world" <note>the macrocosm or universe</note>, which harmoniously agrees with the other. However, he who considers the purpose for which he spoke will find that such a remedy will be contrary to the disease; for to quench such burning sulphur in the human body (namely, if the feverish fire be uni-

Likewise, he says that ulcers proceeding from salts must be cured by <term>Salts</term>. But if we observe his intent, such salts are contrary to those which caused the disease and are quite the opposite of the disease itself. This is because they are flesh-forming <note>original: "incarnative"</note> and healing <note>original: "consolidative"</note>, such as frankincense, mastic, myrrh, and aloes. From this, it is plain that he sometimes uses the word <term>Salt</term> for whatever substance can melt, be dissolved into a watery moisture, and then be dried and hardened by fire—such as the thickened sap and juices of trees and herbs.

<margin>Our Nature cures all diseases when the obstacles <note>original: "impediments"</note> are removed: we are to assist Nature against these obstacles, which are the causes of diseases.</margin>

Just as there are three principles from which all <term>Medicine</term> <note>original: "Physick"</note> proceeds—<term>Mercury</term>, <term>Sulphur</term>, and <term>Salt</term>—there are likewise three sorts of diseases: Mercurial, Sulphurous, and Saline. All ulcers are cured by flesh-forming <term>Mercury</term>; all pus-filled, sticky sores by <term>Salt</term>; and all inflammations by <term>Sulphur</term>. This requires no further argument to confirm it.

Although medicines <note>original: "Medicament"</note> are contrary to the disease, they must of necessity be entirely gentle <note>original: "courteous"</note> to Nature. For Nature seeks a sweet peace in every conflict, and achieves it only by the help and assistance of her friends; if she sinks or fails, the <term>Physician</term> can do no good.

On the contrary, if she remains and is kept...

<margin>A stupendous and true story.</margin>
as was seen in the *New City* at *Prague* in the year <note>original: "Anno"</note> 1602, in a certain peasant <note>original: "rustick"</note> or countryman of *Bohemia* named *Mathem*, about 36 years of age. For the space of two years, through a strange and unheard-of dexterity of the throat, he would many times hide a large <note>original: "of a good bignesse"</note> *iron* knife in his huge and wide throat as if it were a sheath while among his drinking companions <note>original: "pot-companions"</note>. He would thrust the horn handle <note>original: "horny haft"</note> in first, imitating a *juggler* <note>original: "Jugler"; here meaning a magician or street performer</note>, and while in that state, he would call for a large drink of *ale* and finish it. Afterward, at his pleasure, he would use a unique skill <note>original: "singuler art"</note> to pull it out again by the point. 

But—I do not know by what dismal and witless foolhardiness—it happened on the day after *Easter* in that same year that he swallowed it so far that it went entirely down into his stomach, so that he could not get it up again by any means. After he had kept it there for seven full weeks and two days, feeling half-dead from the fear <note>original: "apprehension"</note> of unavoidable death, the point of the knife finally began to force its way out near the opening of the stomach by a natural impulse. This was aided by the application of drawing plasters <note>medicated bandages intended to "draw out" substances or infection</note> made of *lodestone* <note>original: "Load-stone"; a naturally magnetized mineral</note> and other ingredients. 

When the man perceived this, he begged with great persistence <note>original: "importunity"</note> that it might be cut out, even though many tried to persuade him otherwise for fear he would die while it was being done. At last, his request was granted, and by *God’s* blessing—with many prayers for success in such a risky <note>original: "doubtfull"</note> case—it was successfully perfor[med]... <note>The text ends mid-word; the catchword "med" on the original indicates the word is "performed."</note>

<meta>This page continues the miraculous medical account of a Bohemian man who swallowed a knife, concluding with his recovery and another story about a man who swallowed stones.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>

<note>original: "...perceived the point, it was extracted"]</note> by Florian Mathis of Brandenburg, the chief surgeon <note>original: "Chyrurgion"</note> of the kingdom and city, upon the Thursday after Whitsunday <note>The feast of Pentecost, seven weeks after Easter</note> at seven o'clock in the morning. The knife was nine finger-widths long. His stomach had changed the knife's color, looking exactly as if it had lain in a fire for a long time. It is now kept among the Emperor’s collection of rarities and has been shown as an incredible miracle to many people in both the court and the city. 

After a few weeks, the countryman could eat, drink, and sleep without any kind of pain or trouble, as he told me himself. This was after the <term>surgeon</term> <note>original: "Chyrurgion"</note> had applied such treatments as he saw fit. Thus, by God’s help and the many gifts given by generous people to the poor fellow, he was made as healthy as he had ever been in his life. Furthermore, it cost him nothing—which is contrary to the established <term>medical rules</term> <note>original: "Physitians Aphorismes"; these were traditional medical principles, often from Hippocrates, which were considered absolute truths at the time</note>—and shortly afterward, he was married.

Likewise, in the year 1606 at Prague, a certain Silesian <note>A person from Silesia, a historical region now mostly in Poland</note> man swallowed forty-six white flints in the presence of many people in order to earn money. He had gathered these stones at the riverbank; they weighed almost three <term>apothecary pounds</term> <note>original: "Physick pounds"; a measurement used by doctors where one pound is 12 ounces</note>. The smallest of them was about the size of a pigeon’s egg, and all together they filled nearly four of my handfuls. By this bold and risky stunt, he went about earning his living for many years together without any damage to his health, etc.

## Chapter 5.

### *The Duty of Nature's Minister, the Physician.*

**A**ll common philosophy was not restricted to **Aristotle**, as **Petrus Ramus** <note>original: "P. Ramus"; a French humanist and logician known for his critique of Aristotle</note> has soundly proved; nor was the entire *Light of Nature* <note>A term used by Renaissance thinkers to describe innate human reason or divine insight into the natural world</note> confined to **Galen** <note>A Greek physician whose medical theories dominated Europe for over a thousand years</note> and limited to him alone, as witnessed by **Paracelsus** <note>A Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine, challenging Galenic tradition</note>. No man ought to deprive another of the liberty of human ingenuity—that *Light of Nature*, the power to discern and judge as well as himself. The **Greek** Monarchy <note>Refers to the intellectual dominance of Ancient Greek authorities over the sciences</note> is at an end. 

Therefore, he who would be an excellent physician must be free from every kind of sect (for no man can be said to be truly and thoroughly learned who is restricted to the basic principles of only one discipline) and not be tied to the opinion of any single author. Instead, he must follow the naked truth and subscribe to it alone, always remembering that saying of **Horace** <note>A leading Roman lyric poet; the author is likely referring to Horace’s famous line "Nullius addictus iurare in verba magistri," meaning "not bound to swear allegiance to any master"</note>.

<summary>This page argues that medical knowledge and philosophy are not the exclusive domain of ancient authorities like Aristotle or Galen. It calls for physicians to exercise intellectual freedom, using their own reason and the "Light of Nature" to seek truth rather than following a single medical sect.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Galen, Paracelsus, Petrus Ramus, Horace, medical ethics, Light of Nature, physician's duty, intellectual liberty</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for intellectual independence, transitioning from the critique of ancient Greek authorities to the value of synthesizing knowledge from all schools of thought.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

### *I think and judge as I find cause to:*
### *My rule is not another person's mind.*

<margin>Wherever the storm carries me, I am driven as a guest; I am not bound to swear allegiance to the words of any master. <note>original: "Quo me cunque rapit tempestas, deferor hospes: Nullius addictus jurare in verba magistri"; a quote from the Roman poet Horace emphasizing intellectual independence</note></margin>

This is not to say that other men's inventions should be completely dismissed by stubbornly adhering only to one sect. Indeed, all sects, no matter how many there are, may well be admitted, because in every one of them there is something excellent which is not found in the others. This was the view of the most noble and wise Giovanni Pico della Mirandola <note>original: "Picus Mirandula"; an Italian Renaissance philosopher famous for his "Oration on the Dignity of Man"</note>, the "Phoenix of Philosophers" and the inimitable model of the most profound intellect and diverse learning.

There is no book so lowly or bad that it does not have some good in it which even the best authors have sometimes overlooked. This later age, says Quintilian <note>original: "Fabius"; referring to Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, the Roman rhetorician</note>, has endeavored to make the former ages more complete. Because knowledge thrives as human intellect <note>original: "ingenuity"</note> is improved, many loathsome errors of the Heathen have been cleansed away—as if by a second melody—by men of greater wisdom who followed them.

<margin>All secrets are destined by divine decree to be discovered.</margin>

Doubtless, there are more secrets still hidden in the treasures of wisdom and nature than we currently perceive. These secrets—destined for specific times and nations by an unchangeable decree until the end of the world—are to be sought out by those with wise hearts.

For Nature, being undoubtedly vast and cyclical <note>original: "Circularly"; likely referring to the Renaissance idea of nature as a perfect, self-renewing system</note>, can hardly be fully understood by any mortal man because of the shortness of human life.

<summary>The author argues that while one should remain independent of any single "master," there is value to be found in every school of thought. He suggests that knowledge is progressive, with later generations refining the work of the past to uncover the vast, divinely ordained secrets of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Pico della Mirandola, Horace, Quintilian, intellectual independence, progress of science, divine decree, nature's secrets</keywords>

<margin>Many errors of the ancients are discovered by daily experience, which yet has not reached its end either.</margin>

As the case stands, neither the **medicine** <note>original: "Physick"</note> of the ancients nor that of *Theophrastus* <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), a Swiss physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note> is to be totally rejected. Nor should they be so fully embraced that if a better way is found, it shouldn't also be received; for one day teaches another, and tomorrow may be a teacher to today. Both should be compared together, and whatever is best in both should be kept.

For being only human, they have their failings: in some places they make mistakes, in others they write things that contradict each other, and they work at cross-purposes. Sometimes they even differ from themselves; they are deceived in many things, and no single person sees everything. The Holy Spirit alone has the complete or full knowledge of all things; He distributes it to every person according to their particular measure—blowing where it pleases <note>original: "listeth"; a biblical reference to John 3:8</note>—and keeping many things for Himself so that we might always acknowledge Him as our only teacher. 

A true **physician** should be the servant, not the master, of Nature, and a **philosopher** skilled at healing according to the conclusions of *Hippocrates* and *Galen*. But since there are several schools <note>original: "sects"</note> of philosophers, some—following the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> manner—will look "below the moon" <note>referring to the sublunary sphere, the earthly realm composed of the four elements</note> for the elemental nature of things. Others, far more excellent and more truly deserving the name of philosophers, investigate the <term>Arcana</term> <note>secrets or hidden mysteries</note> and more hidden things of Nature. They enter into the very inner rooms and sanctuary of Nature and have the true knowledge and experience of Nature's Light, which creates a truly skilled **physician**. *Valentin Weigel* <note>original: "Valentinus Weigelius"; a German theologian and mystic (1533–1588).</note> and *Peter Winzius* <note>original: "Petrus Winzius"; a contemporary follower of Weigel's ideas.</note> were men educated and enlightened not in the sensory school of fools, nor in the rational school of scholars, but in the third school of perfect men. These men gave themselves wholly to this pursuit, as the manuscripts of their skill and the enduring records <note>original: "engraven monuments"</note> which they have left behind are no less than divine witnesses to it for eternity. By the favor of the Most High, these works will come to light in their own time—unless the unworthiness <note>original: "indignity"</note> and ingratitude of the world keep them back. 

This would occur so that, according to the good pleasure of the Divine Will, the minds of those who read them (who are still held within this "hell" of the body under the yoke of misery) might be helped by divine Grace. Through a serious knowledge and lamentation of our Fall, and through frequent and daily contemplation of heavenly things—along with the <term>annihilation</term>, <term>abnegation</term>, sacrifice <note>original: "immolation"</note>, and <term>mortification</term> <note>The spiritual practice of "putting to death" sinful desires or the ego.</note> of themselves through *Christ*—they might cast all shadows behind them and turn inward to themselves, into the temple of the heart. 

In this way, I say, they might, by a daily practice of piety, bring out 그 that huge talent and treasury which is hidden and shut up within themselves. Otherwise, they remain like miserable mortal men who do not know themselves, and consequently know nothing else, lazily neglecting God within themselves and in their blindness and...

Therefore, *Wimpeneus* <note>Johann Wimpina, a 16th-century medical writer and supporter of Paracelsus</note> learnedly shows that the greatest and most serious diseases nowadays are cured by the *Paracelsian* <note>A follower of the chemical-based medical system of Paracelsus</note> in three ways.

And since we have come to the subject of *Tartar*, I will speak a little more about it.

<margin>Every organ has its own proper digestion, separation, <term>emunctory</term>, and excretion within itself. The first digestion in the stomach is not the final digestion, but a preparation <note>original: "apparation"</note> for the digestions of the specific organs.</margin>

It is reduced into *Sulfur*, *Mercury*, and *Salt* <note>The *Tria Prima*, or three primary alchemical principles that Paracelsians believed made up all matter</note>, as is evident in the three main <term>emunctories</term> <note>Organs or pathways through which the body carries off waste</note>: excess *Salt* is separated by the urine; excess *Sulfur* is divided and purged through the bowels; and *Mercury*—the moisture of that which provides nourishment—passes through the pores as sweat if there is too much of it.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Paracelsian physiology, specifically how the body processes food and drink and why "Tartar" (stony or gravelly deposits) forms when the internal organs fail to separate nutrients from waste correctly.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>

...to our health. Nature takes nothing but that which is pure. The stomach, which is the instrument of man's *Archæus* <term>Archæus</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, the vital internal force or "inner alchemist" that directs life and health</note>, or the internal, innate chemist <term>Chymist</term> which God has planted in man, as soon as it takes anything into itself, it separates that which is impure, dreggy, and tartar-like <term>Tartarous</term> <note>Related to "Tartar," the stony deposits formed from food and drink</note> from the pure nourishment. If the stomach is strong in its full separative power, the pure nourishment passes to the limbs <note>original: "Members"</note> for nourishment, and the impure goes out in succession. If the stomach is weak and feeble, the impure part is also sent through the mesenteric <term>Mesaraick</term> <note>Modern: mesenteric; veins that drain blood and nutrients from the digestive tract</note> veins to the liver, where it is separated and digested a second time. The liver, therefore, in its turn, separates the pure from the impure—namely <note>original Latin: *viz.*, short for *videlicet*</note>, the "ruby" from the "crystal." The "ruby" <term>Rubie</term> <note>In this context, likely referring to red blood or the vital part of nourishment</note> is the nourishment of all the body parts, such as the heart, brain, etc.

<margin>
The first separation of tartar, which happens in the liver, turns into "moss"; the second, which is in the stomach, turns into "hail"; the third, in the kidneys and bladder, into a small stone. Every man has "moss" and "hail" in him, but not necessarily a stone.
</margin>

The "crystal" <term>Christall</term> <note>referring to clear or watery waste</note>, or that which is not nourishment, the liver sends to the kidneys <note>original: "reines"</note>. This is urine, which is nothing else but the salt pressed out of the "mercurials" <term>Mercurials</term> <note>Substances associated with the Paracelsian principle of Mercury, representing fluidity and volatility</note>, forced into a liquid state <note>original: "resolution"</note> by the force of separation. The liver breaks this down into water and then throws it out. If the liver is weak and cannot separate it well, it sends it to the kidneys in a slimy <note>original: "mucilaginous"</note> and stony state. There, for want of good separation—that is, when the spirits of salt (namely <note>original Latin: *viz.*</note> of the flesh and of the urine) are united—the spirit of the salt intervenes through the power of predestination <term>predestination</term> <note>In this context, a natural destiny or pre-set chemical process</note>. It is then thickened <note>original: "coagulated"</note> into sand or tartar <term>Tartar</term>, appearing either as clumps or slime.

Tartar <term>Tartar</term>, therefore, is the waste <note>original: "excrement"</note> of food and drink, which is thickened by the spirit of salt.

For by divine decree, every kind of food contains a poison—a Tartar-like, slimy impurity—situated right next to its medicinal or nutritional part <term>physick</term>.

There are four sorts of Tartar, arising from the four kinds of nourishment provided by the **Elements** by which we are sustained.

The first sort arises from the use of those things which grow in the **Earth**, such as legumes <term>pulse</term> of all kinds, herbs, and so on, upon which we feed.

The second comes from the nourishment which we receive from the **Water**, such as fish and shellfish.

The third is found in the **Flesh** of beasts and birds...

The fourth kind comes from the **Air** <term>Firmament</term>, which is most like **alcohol** <term>Spirit of wine</term> in its subtle nature and has the strongest influence of all. If the air is infected by the vapors of the earth, water, and sky, it eventually affects us as well. We see this in those acute and deadly **astral diseases** <note>diseases believed to be caused by the influence of the stars or heavens</note>, such as **pleurisy**, the **plague**, and **prunells** <note>original: "Prunells"; a historical term for a type of burning fever or inflammatory sore throat</note> or raging, burning illnesses. Those four kinds of **Tartar** <note>stony or slimy metabolic deposits</note> show themselves in the **urine** and are identified by the **art of separation** <note>the alchemical process of chemical analysis or distillation</note>. By this analysis, it becomes clear from which kind of Tartar the disease originates. He who knows what sort of food a person eats may also know their disease; conversely, he who knows the disease may know what the person eats. The disease is cured by the same element that was the cause of it. If **Galen** <note>an ancient Greek physician whose theories of "humors" dominated Western medicine for centuries</note> and his followers had possessed the true knowledge of these waste products <note>original: "Excrements"</note> from food and drink—which for the most part breed all our diseases and which **Paracelsus** categorizes under the names of **Poison** and **Tartar**—then **choler** <note>yellow bile</note> and **melancholy** <note>black bile</note> would have been rooted out of the **physician’s garden** <note>meaning the medical profession's standard practices and theories</note> long ago.

Anyone who does not understand this **tartarous matter** of diseases, which proceeds from the excessive waste of food and drink, cannot possibly understand how the cause of diseases afflicts us by destroying the framework of the **little world** <note>the human body, viewed as a microcosm of the universe</note> and taking away our life. ...[separa]tion <note>This continues the word from the previous page's catchword "tion."</note> separates the **Tartar** <note>stony or slimy metabolic deposits</note> from the nourishment. This is done by our **natural heat**, or the **microcosmic** <term>relating to the human body as a "little world" reflecting the universe</term> heat of the Sun and Moon that is within us. By this heat, the nourishment is digested, like fire that burns up wood, and from it good blood is produced—provided it is not hindered by sickness or the weakening of the **separative power** <note>original: "vertue"</note> of the stomach, liver, and kidneys. If weakened, it must be strengthened by its like—that is, by the **macrocosmic** <term>relating to the great world or the universe at large</term> heat of the Sun and Moon, if it could be obtained. This is the most simple matter which the Almighty God generated from the spirit of the world (which is one and the same as the spirit of our body) for the restoration and conservation of human nature. Alternatively, it can be strengthened with those things containing the power of the Sun and Moon, which is brought from potential into action by art <note>original: "deduced by art into act"; referring to alchemical processing</note>—that is, turned into a simple spirit like the spirit of our life, which is achieved through resolution <note>the chemical breakdown of a substance</note> and conjunction with the nourishment.

<margin>
Tartar differs according to the locations of the body's pores and passages, such as the mouth, the bottom of the stomach, the intestines, liver, bladder, kidneys, flesh, blood, and marrow.
</margin>
But if the **Archæus** <term>the internal "chemist" or vital force believed to govern the functions of an organ</term> of our stomach, liver, or kidneys—which separates the pure from the impure—is infected, or if their separative power is hindered by any external accidents, then the waste products <note>original: "excrements"</note> remain in the **chyle** <term>Chylus; the milky fluid of digested food before it turns to blood</term>. This causes stomach diseases in the stomach, **liver diseases** <note>original: "Jecorall"</note> in the liver, and **arthritic diseases** <note>original: "Athriticall"</note> in the "glue" <note>synovial fluid or connective tissues</note>, sinews, ligaments, and joints. This breeds the gout in the hands, feet, and knees through the freezing <note>original: "congelation"</note> of this "glue" by the **spirit of salt** <term>a historical term for hydrochloric acid or a concentrated saline essence</term>, in addition to those diseases of the **kidneys** <note>original: "Reines"</note> and intestines. Therefore, the Archæus of the stomach must destroy the Tartar of the Elements so that it is not turned into the "Tartar of Man"; for the spirit of the Sun is the lord and master of **coagulation** <note>the process of a liquid thickening into a solid or semi-solid state</note>.

His works, which were printed for the public good through the encouragement and at the expense of that most renowned and reverend **Ernest**, Prince Elector of **Cologne**, have given his adversaries reason to charge him with implicit and evident contradiction. This is because he wrote in a <term>magical style</term> that is difficult for the unskillful and unlearned. He wrote this way not for the common people <note>original: "vulgar sort"</note>, but for himself and the knowledgeable children of <term>Wisdom</term> trained in the school of <term>Magic</term>. He did not write for the <term>sophistical alchemists</term> who are bewitched by a greedy desire for gold. He was also inflamed <note>original: "incensed"</note> with a righteous hatred against some of the most wretched, wicked, and inhumane <term>physicians</term> and <term>druggists</term> of his time, who often plotted to poison him to shorten his life. If he had written more plainly, then those wandering <note>original: "circumforaneous"</note> and common <term>alchemists</term> would have surpassed all other <term>physicians</term> and debased <note>original: "prostituted"</note> the Art, doing great wrong and injury to Nature.

He concealed his mysteries under common and various names; therefore, we must not mistake these comparisons <note>original: "similitudes"</note> for the truth itself or for what he intended by them. For there are few who understand the <term>physical secrets</term>—that is, the hidden power of **God**—or the <term>magical words</term> in **Paracelsus**. ...and the expert physician only, who is more religiously and holily instructed, in the administration <note>original: "exhibition"</note> of their medicines, they will follow the steps of pious and venerable antiquity. They will imitate that most commendable and religious custom of the **Hermetic physicians** <note>followers of the alchemical and philosophical tradition attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, often associated with Paracelsus</note>, who always make it a practice to pray for a blessing upon their endeavors, striving to be equal to those who will not take in hand even the smallest matter without divine invocation <note>calling upon God for help or guidance</note>.

<margin>The power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of medicine lies in the dosage: for if we take too much saffron or treacle <note>an ancient medicinal compound, often used as an antidote</note>, it is poison; if too little, it will do no good. Thus, success depends only on the experience of the physician.</margin>
Whoever uses any created thing <note>original: "Creature"</note> without craving a blessing, God considers it as nothing other than theft and robbery. We who profess **CHRIST** ought to administer medicine in that wonderful name of **JESUS**; for so the <term>Doctor of the Gentiles</term> <note>a traditional title for Saint Paul</note> commands the *Colossians*, saying: <margin>Colossians 3:17 & 1 Corinthians 10:31</margin> *“Whatever you do in word or deed, do all in the **NAME** of the **LORD JESUS** Christ, giving thanks to him, and to God through him.”* God’s blessing is to be obtained by prayer: *“Call upon me in the day of your trouble; I will deliver you, and you shall glorify me,”* says our Creator.

First, then, before we give or take any medicine <note>original: "Phyſick"</note>, we are to pray to the good and great God that the medicine which he has ordained as the means of healing may work divine and healthful effects, so that his name may be glorified.

Secondly, after we have recovered and made the patients well, let us never cease to give him hearty thanks. This is a matter of gratitude, which should be considered a duty in itself, and also serves to avoid the heavy wrath of God against unthankful men in the future.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual dimensions of health, suggesting that sickness is often a form of divine discipline or a call to repentance when physical remedies fail.</meta>

->[ 177 ]<-

<margin>Job 33:19, 20</margin>
For every sickness is, as it were, a way to make amends <note>original: "expiation"</note> or an offering to appease God <note>original: "propitiatory of sacrifice"</note>. Either the just Judge, through this natural divine retribution <note>original: "innate revenge"</note>, penance, and discipline <note>original: "scourge"</note>, calls a person to improve their life for the future; or, through this fatherly visitation and the burden <note>original: "imposition"</note> of the cross—which they ought to endure patiently—they may become an example to themselves and their neighbors, leading to a more fervent fear and worship of God. For God many times permits some people to fall into many great illnesses whose minds have been seriously <note>original: "greivously"</note> infected and disordered <note>original: "distempered"</note> by the joyful and pleasant health of the body <note>original: "flesh"</note>. This occurs alongside the continuation of their sins, leading to the loss of their eternal health; for physical health is worth nothing—and is even a form of condemnation to us—if our sins are not forgiven. Moreover, sins weaken the powers of the soul and make it unable <note>original: "impotent"</note> to naturally govern the body. Therefore, the powers of the body are weakened <note>original: "enfeebled"</note> and hasten toward death. Or else the sick person is held under this yoke and restraint <note>original: "bridle"</note> of weakness <note>original: "infirmity"</note> as if by a healthy purification <note>original: "Purgatory"</note>—though few are actually reformed by their sickness—to guide them toward the duty of piety at the very least. In this way, that loose lack of restraint <note>original: "licentiousness"</note> in sinning is pruned and cut off, which the person would continue to abuse if they were to live and be well any longer.

<margin>A physician begins by appeasing God’s wrath. Job 33:26.</margin>
In these diseases that arise from resisting astral influence <note>original: "Astrall, impression"</note> and the wrath of God, no physical <note>original: "Corporall"</note> medicine can do any good (because God has shut the doors of Nature). Only celestial and spiritual medicine is effective; namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note>, through serious repentance and heartily bewailing the sins that we have...

Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who integrated spirituality with medicine.</note> calls them divine diseases <note>original: "Deal diseases," from the Latin *Deus* for God</note>, which come from the "Being of God" <note>original: "Ens of God," referring to the divine essence or influence</note>. It is God who inflicts all such diseases and who alone works through both good and bad men. The cure, Paracelsus says, is found through faith; or, once the spiritual purging is complete, the physician may then attempt to use his medical skills. The causes of those diseases that come from the Being of God are impossible to discover <note>original: "unsearchable"</note>. In these cases, the cure must be sought through faith, not through nature. Also, in the healing of divine diseases—those that come from the Being of God—or in the divine <note>original: "deificall"</note> or faith-based cure, the predestined time <note>original: "Terme"</note> or period set according to God's pleasure must be respected.

## CHAPTER VI.

### Concerning that one and only chief Medicine of the most Ancient Philosophers.

<margin>This hidden wisdom <note>original: "occult Minerva," referring to the Roman goddess of wisdom and war, used here as a metaphor for deep alchemical knowledge</note> of philosophy and this uniquely precious jewel is beyond price.</margin>
<margin>( ! )</margin>
**F**urthermore, I shall provide here what still relates to that chief and universal philosophical medicine <note>original: "Phisick"</note>, compared to which we never read of a greater gift of wisdom that divine generosity <note>original: "bounty"</note> bestowed upon humanity. Not even the rational soul <note>original: "Reasonable Soul," the aspect of the soul responsible for logic and intellect</note> itself—which, next to God in Heaven and Earth, cannot conceive of or discover anything under Heaven bestowed upon mankind more admirable, more sublime, more noble, or more excellent than this <gloss>underlined: most secret Secret of secret Secrets</gloss> <note>This likely refers to the "Philosopher’s Stone" or the universal panacea sought by alchemists.</note>—is its equal. 

Through this secret, wonders—indeed, all things—may be accomplished, both regarding the planets of "inferior astronomy" <note>This refers to alchemy, where "inferior astronomy" is the study of metals on Earth as spiritual counterparts to the "superior astronomy" of the stars.</note>, whose imperfections and impurities <note>original: "drosse"</note> it causes to vanish by piercing them with its most powerful influence (for it separates all external sulfurous qualities <note>original: "Sulphureity," a term in alchemy for the combustible principle</note> and impure earthliness <note>original: "Terrestreity"</note> from both metallic and human bodies), as well as the recovery and preservation of the failing and lost health of the human body through its fiery vigor, besides infinite other things; to say no-

<summary>The author introduces the "Secret of Secrets," a universal medicine and alchemical stone that purifies metals and restores human health, claiming it is God's greatest gift to mankind after the soul.</summary>

<margin>See the *Monas Hieroglyphica* <note>original: "Monade," referring to the 1564 book by John Dee which uses a single symbol to represent the unity of the cosmos</note> of **John Dee** of London, and the works of **Roger Bacon** <note>a 13th-century friar and philosopher associated with early science and alchemy</note>.</margin>
[...continued from previous page] thing now of its magical and super-celestial use. When the generative <note>original: "Gonetick," likely from the Greek *gonos* for seed or generation</note> influence of the beams of the sun and moon and the "fourth revolution" <note>likely referring to the final stage of the alchemical process or a specific planetary cycle</note> is finished upon its native earth, it is absolutely endowed with all created influential power—both in the elemental world and in the celestial or super-celestial world. It is the most wonderful of all wonders; for as God is wonderful in all His works, so He usually hides His wonderful gifts in wonderful men. 

All of antiquity, as well as the truth found in all nations and languages concerning the tradition of this doctrine, bear witness that this is so. This is further confirmed by the consensus of all those most learned men who, in every age, have lived with the greatest admiration and praise of many. Moreover, besides the eyewitness observation <note>original: "ocular inspection"</note> and confirmation of many in our own time, it is easy to determine this from their writings, which are woven with so many **hieroglyphical**, **magical**, and **mathematical** veils <note>original: "coverings"</note> within such a great and certain series of **philosophical truth**.

Who then would not admire and embrace such a great gift from the greatest God, the immortal reward for one's study, pains, and virtue? To the pious and holy <term>philosophers</term> <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists or seekers of natural wisdom</note> dignified by nature and education, it guarantees and assures the removal of old age and the renewing of youth, perpetual health, and honest food and clothing. This is achieved without hindrance to our neighbor—not by usury and fraud, nor deceptive goods, nor by the oppression of the poor (by which most of our rich men are enriched today)—but by the industry of work and the labor of one's hands.

God forbid, therefore, that by slighting the example of the ancients, I should either deny such precious wonders of God or obscure the wonderful powers of nature. For he who despises knowledge is himself despised by the glorious and high God for rejecting this most true <term>art</term> <note>original: "Art," referring to the practice of alchemy or "Hermetic philosophy"</note>. Nor would I do what is worse: revile these wonders as most people do, as if they were merely the speculations of idle men or the empty dreams and fictions of a foolish and senile mind. Such critics, in the eyes of wise men, only betray the weakness of their own judgment and openly bear witness to their own folly. Therefore, those who revile and are ignorant of these divine banquets—whom the <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: "Phylosophers"</note> call fools—are not to be admitted to them. Likewise, those who dote on their own fantastical dreams are to be utterly excluded.

At this point, some readers may be listening to hear whether, trusting in my own ingenuity, I dare to boast of the preparation of this substance here. They may wonder if I ambitiously claim for myself absolute knowledge of this art for the sake of showing off, as those covetous <term>mountebanks</term> <note>original: "Mountebanks," charlatans or swindlers who sold fraudulent medicines, often from a public stage</note> and greedy <term>pseudo-philosophers</term> <note>original: "Phylosophists," a pejorative term for those who pretend to be philosophers for profit or status</note> are accustomed to do.

<margin>Here I would be regarded as a signpost <note>original: "Index"</note>, or as one standing at the door to direct others the way they should go—helping them more than myself—or as a whetstone to sharpen and encourage them.</margin>
But because I promised the courteous reader a little earlier that I would set down at least those things which I had personally tested <note>original: "made tryall of"</note>, I was unwilling to openly and wickedly falsify anything concerning the undoubted certainty of this matter—which is the behavior of a cheater, not a wise man.

For this sacred and divine <term>art</term> and <term>science</term>, which belongs not to <term>sophists</term> <note>original: "Sophisters," those who use clever but fallacious arguments</note> but to <term>philosophers</term>, which the

They are also those who measure their happiness by heaven and the love of God, the giver of every good thing.

<margin>By wisdom, the heart is made constant.</margin>

And lastly, they are those who—without any evil intention, and ignoring the shallow thirst of human desires—most humbly desire, with fear and trembling, to use such great gifts for the purpose that belongs to the Master of Nature: namely, the praise and honor of God, and the good of His [people...]

By these people, I say—among those who follow the **"Children of the golden doctrine"** <note>The "golden doctrine" refers to the spiritual and natural philosophy of alchemy, where the ultimate "gold" is the knowledge of God and His creation rather than mere bullion.</note> (whose gold is the Almighty God)—that most rare Good, which is to be preferred above all riches, is justly and rightly sought out for the sake of human health. A philosopher must desire nothing but wisdom, which is concerned with divine things. Therefore, a true philosopher has never sought after nor desired riches, but is instead delighted with the **mysteries of nature**. For truly, he who is an <term>adept</term> <note>An "adept" is a master who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy, possessing the Philosopher's Stone or the Elixir of Life.</note>—or one who has attained this wisdom—may carry in his purse not just the value of a single kingdom, but nearly the wealth of the entire world. In God and with God, he possesses all things in the world as a lord, and in the fear and service of God, he commands the whole of creation. However, that gift is acquired neither by anger nor by forced violence. It comes either through the immediate inspiration of the Most High or through the expert, firsthand <note>original: "ocular"</note> demonstration of a wise master in this art.

That this is beyond all controversy, no man who applies himself to philosophy...

<margin>It is not the first, but the second birth that produces it.</margin>
Whatever the cures may be, they are healed by this universal medicine—just as a carpenter shapes all kinds of timber—unless the person has reached the final limit <term>the predestined time of death</term> appointed for them by the Supreme Being, or the disease is inflicted by God, beyond the laws of nature, as a punishment and special affliction.

But as has been said, no one can make use of this unique and heavenly gift except the person on whom God Himself has bestowed it. He alone both enlightens the darkness and obscures the clarity of these mysteries, so that no one can understand the simplest things unless He enlightens them, nor can anyone remain ignorant of the darkest secrets if He illustrates them. Such a great power is given by the rich and special grace of the Most High Creator.

Therefore, *Lullius* <note>original: "Lullius"; referring to Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and logician often associated with alchemical writings</note>, that divine and most perfect philosopher, rightly concluded that between the Artist <term>the practitioner of the alchemical Art</term> and God (the First Cause), there ought to be an agreement without conflict. He believed the First Mover, as the primary source of Form, should move the intelligence or soul of the Artist toward a true understanding, so that He may reveal to him what is hidden in the mastery <note>original: "magistery," a term for the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone or the Stone itself</note> of this Art. Blessed will be the person whom the Lord God shall be pleased to inspire with the gifts of His grace. For it is the Lord of Heaven who knows the hearts of those through whom He would use His design and measure.

<unclear>Not</unclear>withstanding, we see that men sometimes offend not only against God by their ingratitude, but also against their innocent neighbor by using strange schemes unbecoming of an Adept <term>a master philosopher who has attained the secret of the Great Work</term> philosopher.
