--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine nature of the alchemical "Art," warning against those who reveal its secrets out of pride and contrasting them with the true, humble "children of Hermes."</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>
<meta>catchword: I say,</meta>

Previously, two public philosophers from various nations in our time abused the gifts of God by defying those most horrific curses <term>anathemas</term> established by philosophers. Afterward—as every person is undoubtedly the architect of their own fortune, driven by the skill that the astral <note>original: "sidereal"</note> spirit causes and encourages—they came to a sorrowful and tragic end. This brought perpetual disgrace to their names, fitting for their unworthy publication of this most true philosophical art. 

Their lives were miserably wasted and cut short by the wrath of **GOD**, the righteous avenger, both for their arrogant pride—with punishment and repentance following their irritating talkativeness—and for their deceptive violations of the sacred vow of silence <note>original: "Harpocratical silence," referring to Harpocrates, the Greek god of silence and secrets</note>, which they practiced by turns to hide the truth for their own safety.

<margin>The origin of the philosophical mastery <note>original: "magistery," the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>. Most people act differently now; they boast to others that they have clear knowledge of it, when it is only imaginary. Thus, they hinder themselves through their own gullibility and self-persuasion, so they cannot benefit or progress to learn any more.</margin>

In contrast, there were those more ancient and skillful philosophers who were born under a fortunate astrological sign. These were the "children of Hermes" <note>followers of Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy and Hermetic philosophy</note> who first discovered this science. Among them, nothing was more ancient than Truth, and nothing was more foul and abominable than falsehood and deceit. They judged it much safer and more worth their labor to *actually possess* the secret without the recognition of a foolish, ignorant crowd than to merely *seem* or be thought to possess it. They also endeavored to leave an untainted memory for future generations. They did not act as many gullible people imagine—that, being deceived by their own vain fantasies, they would then deceive others in the same way. Such a thing is not the characteristic of an honest man.

<summary>This page warns against the "loquacity" and pride of modern pseudo-philosophers who reveal secrets for fame, contrasting them with the ancient, honest "children of Hermes" who valued truth and silent mastery over public reputation.</summary>

<margin>In the first age of the world, God made this known through the Light of Nature.</margin>
I say, these private (rather than public) secretaries of Nature, who have the **Natural Light** <note>Reason or divine intellect inherent in humanity</note> fresh and flourishing within them, have followed Reason as the best guide according to the ability God gave them. All of them, with the greatest attention of both body and mind, press chiefly toward one and the same end and goal of **Virtue**. They account nothing more glorious than that they might peaceably rejoice, prudently and in **quiet silence**, with a *sound mind in a sound body* <note>original: "Mind truly Sound in a Sound Body," a reference to the Latin phrase *Mens sana in corpore sano*</note>, according to the **fear of God** and **love** of their **neighbor**.

This is **Philosophy Adept** <note>The state of having achieved the highest mastery of alchemical and spiritual knowledge</note>, which *Paracelsus* explains in his work, *The **Tincture of Physicists*** <note>original: "Tincture of Physicks," likely referring to Paracelsus's *De Tinctura Physicorum*</note>. He describes its benefits as **long life**, free from all infirmity until the moment of **natural death**, and the honest support of that long life in this **valley** of miseries, so that we might serve **God** without poverty or doing harm to our **neighbor**.
<note>The original OCR reads "vaſe" (vase), but "vale" (valley) was likely intended in the common phrase "vale of miseries" or "vale of tears."</note>

Though there may be many hunting after this happiness with a kind of great and continued greed of mind, they have been persuaded that they should never attain it by any other means or **Arts** except through a wonderful and most hidden comprehension of all the virtues of the whole Creation, flowing and running together into one certain mass. This is to be accomplished on the **Road**, or the **King’s Highway**, and through **Philosophical Reason**.

<margin>The defect of Nature must be supplied by the industry of Art.</margin>
All these spiritual virtues and active qualities—being gathered and concentrated into one mass through the **great help** of ingenuity and **Art**, like a microcosm <note>original: "the lesser world"</note>—are stronger when united than when they are dispersed.

<margin>The life of man is the astral balsam <term>Astral Balsam</term> <note>In alchemy, a life-sustaining fluid or "salt" believed to originate from the stars and preserve the body from decay</note>, the balsamic impression, the celestial and invisible fire, the enclosed air, and the tincturing spirit of salt.</margin>

[or] be without a spark of this spirit. This celestial spirit—which is one and the same as our natural spirit—is our **natural heat** when its breathing in our body is not lessened or hindered by outward things. Through this heat, everything is digested for the sustenance <note>original: "sustentation"</note> and multiplication of every individual part. It digests the nourishment that man takes and produces good blood in all the limbs. As long as the blood is pure, it remains the strong, vital, pure, and sound spirit of the heart, so that the whole body lives orderly and well. 

But if it is hindered by sickness so that it cannot perform its function <note>original: "office"</note> well, the nourishment is not well digested <term>concocted</term> <note>original: "concocted"; in early medicine, this referred to the "cooking" or ripening of nutrients into blood and humors by the body's internal heat</note>; and that produces bad blood, which weakens the vital spirit of the heart. From this comes old age, that "house of oblivion." At last, there follows a full extinction, consumption, and dissipation of that spirit, which is natural death. So that the consumption and dissipation of this spirit may be prevented (as much as nature allows), that spirit and natural heat in human bodies—when weakened and hindered—must be increased and strengthened so that it may better perform its duty.

But since every active force <term>agent</term>, when it begins to act, does not move toward anything beneath itself, but toward that which is equal, similar, and suitable to it; therefore, this strengthening must also come from its like. That is, by that celestial heat of the sun, the moon, and the other planets, or through those things in which the power <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "virtue"; here meaning the inherent power or medicinal property of a substance</note> of the **SUN** and **MOON** is most potent and abundant.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Fifth Essence," specifically comparing the life-giving "supernal fire" found in celestial bodies to the destructive "elemental fire" found on Earth. It argues that medicine must be derived from the former to truly preserve the body.</meta>

<margin>The Celestial Spirit, or that Spirit of the world, and the Natural Spirit of our body is one and the same Spirit; and therefore the Heat of the Sun and Moon, generated from the very pulse of the spirit, is a thing more **concoct** <term>concoct</term> <note>original: "concoct"; meaning more "cooked" or refined by natural heat, signifying a higher state of maturity or perfection.</note> and consequently more perfect.</margin>
...or is less restrained by matter. For these things work more quickly and perfectly, and do more readily beget their like; and, what is easier, the spirit or that **supernal Fire** <term>supernal Fire</term> <note>A divine or heavenly fire associated with the stars and the sun, believed to be the source of life and growth.</note> is extracted from them by art. To this spirit, the heat that burns not like the **Elemental**, but that which makes all things fruitful, and Light giving life to all things, are proper. But burning heat, consuming all things, and darkness, making all things barren, are proper to the **Elemental and Inferior Fire**.

That same burning heat is therefore excluded, as are all diverse and contrary things, such as all the inferior elements. For this and all things else that include a natural composition in them (so far as they are yet drowned in a thick, gross matter, and as yet not separated from it) are subject to **corruption** <note>Decay or the natural breakdown of physical matter.</note> and **transmutation** <term>transmutation</term> <note>The process of changing from one state or substance into another; here implying instability.</note>. But **Medicines** ought to be preserving and very durable, and remote from corruption. For whereas they should preserve the human body from corruption, they ought first to be of a long and lasting nature; otherwise they would corrupt rather than preserve.

<margin>One Like added to its like maketh it more Like.</margin>
Besides, it is but in vain to think to preserve a corruptible body by a putrid and corruptible thing, to cure the weak by a feeble thing, or to form a thing by a thing subject to deformity. Every corruptible, weak, and feeble thing added to its like increases it, and so that corruptibility is increased, not diminished—as we see in some (and truly too many) **Physicians** of our time who labor in vain to cure men of their maladies...

<margin>
The innate heat and **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture</term> <note>In ancient and medieval medicine, the essential fluid that maintains life and bodily heat.</note> of the **microcosm** <term>microcosm</term> <note>Literally "the little world"; the human body seen as a reflection of the universe.</note> are sustained by the **macrocosmic** <term>macrocosmic</term> <note>The "great world" or the universe at large.</note> heat and moisture of the sun and moon, as they are part of that one and the same celestial and natural spirit of ours.

Therefore, those who wish to preserve that vital spirit (which is the radical moisture and heat, the innate **mummy** <term>mummy</term> <note>In alchemy and early medicine, "mummy" refers to the vital life force or a spiritual essence within the body, not an Egyptian corpse.</note>, and which has its seat in the center of the human heart as the support <note>original: "sustentacle"</note> of all our life) in young people, and repair it when it withers in the elderly—and, as much as possible, make them young again, thereby bringing human life into the greatest state of health—must seek out not the elemental heat, but that celestial heat of the sun and moon. This heat dwells in the more incorruptible substance that is found beneath the sphere <note>original: "globe"</note> of the moon. To make this like our heart or spirit, it must be prepared and made into a medicine and a most pleasant food. Once taken by mouth, it can immediately penetrate and pass through the human body, keeping everything from decay <note>original: "incorrupt"</note>, especially the flesh that is joined to it. It nourishes the power and spirit of life, increases and restores it, digests every "raw" thing <note>referring to undigested or crude humors in the body</note>, trims away all excess of any quality, makes the natural moisture abundant, and strengthens, ignites, and increases the weak natural heat or fire. <page-num>212</page-num>

[...from living as long as the] philosophers, his ancestors, in this wicked world and true valley of miseries, of which they—as strangers and pilgrims of this world—never so much as dreamed.

<margin>
<insert>The Good vs. the Goodness of Plato</insert>
God is to be seen beyond the horizon of eternity and on the other side of the wall of Paradise, which is the proper place for those who contemplate him. He who remains united with God in Christ, like God and the angels, never grows old. In vain will those husks and scraps of *Paracelsus* rise up, as they usually do, to exclaim that this interpretation is forced and far-fetched.
</margin>

**GOD** is the center of all creatures. The more any being draws near to him, the more blessed, less variable, and less changeable <note>original: "mutable"</note> they become. But the farther anything departs from that center—the One, which is to say the immutable will of God—toward the circumference, variety, and plurality of created things, the more unhappy, imperfect, and changeable it is. Blessedness is found in unity, not in the circumference; peace and the rest of souls are found in Christ, not in the world.

Therefore, he who by the immense goodness of God (which goes before us without ceasing) shall forget all things and leave behind sensory and temporary <note>original: "sensible and Temporall"</note> things—which are to be used only during our passage through life—and shall be united to that one center, grows young rather than old. This is the true "Long Life" of the Cabalists <term>Cabalists</term> <note>Followers of Kabbalah, a form of Jewish mysticism that influenced many Renaissance thinkers.</note> and of *Paracelsus*, which he so often and so earnestly begged for through prayer and holy hope in his hymns and soliloquies: the true Enochian life <term>Enochian life</term> <note>A reference to the biblical figure Enoch, who "walked with God" and was taken to heaven without dying (Genesis 5:24).</note>.

Conversely, he who is not joined to this most unified, fountain-like, and only Unity by clinging to it <note>original: "adhesion"</note> must necessarily perish forever. He will be separated from light and life by the "second death" and be cast into the utter darkness of the misty <note>original: "Caliginous," from the Latin *caligo* meaning darkness or fog</note> world. This deprivation and lack of the sight of God is the most bitter of all punishments.

For all diseases require their own proper cure and reject any other remedy: physical medicines do no good for mental or supernatural distempers, nor can mental medicines be profitable for bodily diseases. This also is to be considered: that many times we are corrupted in our mother's womb, sometimes in birth and education, and by many various accidents we may be hindered and kept from attaining the natural term of life, as Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus</note> very often observes in his books.

But not to forget what we intended, and wander wider than the bounds of a preface, I shall now draw to a conclusion.

Whatever advantage therefore I have made by my labors, watchings, studies, and travels <note>original: "peregrinations"</note>,

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his work, urging critics to stop their petty attacks and instead contribute their own knowledge to the public good.</meta>

For the glory of God and the advancement of my neighbors' well-being, do not let those critics unnecessarily annoy and disrupt the hard work and proven experience of others with their proud and hasty criticisms. Do not let them act like *Aesop's Crow* <note>In the fables of Aesop, a crow (or jay) dresses itself in the beautiful feathers of other birds to appear more important than it is, only to be exposed as a fraud.</note>, or reproach others for a "patched-together and mixed hodgepodge" of good and bad elements. Separating the grain from the chaff and truth from falsehood is sometimes a long and difficult task; let those who have labored and worked hard in similar cases be the judges of that.

Nor should they—like *Timon* the misanthrope <note>original: "Man-hater"; Timon of Athens was a legendary figure famous for his hatred of human society and his rejection of his fellow man.</note>—allow a spiteful and rude spirit to be passed down to future generations. They should not reveal to the entire world their cruel and hateful lack of humanity or their unjust hatred of the Truth by opposing it. If they do, they risk being completely excluded from the company of learned men and, instead of receiving an answer, being labeled stubborn enemies of humanity and opponents of public safety—who (as mentioned before) deserve to be dismissed <term>casheer'd</term> <note>To be "cashiered" is to be dishonorably dismissed from a position or cast out of a group.</note>.

And let them not frighten those who are seeking the truth, who receive our labors and faithful efforts with kindness and appreciation. Or, if they are able to discourage anyone, let them instead share their own sources of knowledge, since they are given an opportunity here to publish their own *Observations*. Let them bring their lit candle out from under the basket <note>A reference to the biblical teaching in Matthew 5:15, which advises people not to hide their light or knowledge.</note>, lest the 
<margin>Matthew 21:19</margin>
curse of the fig tree <note>In Matthew 21:19, Jesus curses a fig tree for failing to produce fruit; the author warns that those who do not share their knowledge are like the unproductive tree.</note> fall upon them. They should set aside all pointless arguments over words, academic <note>original: "*Scholastick*"</note> questions, and useless debates. For such things are what a clever and argumentative *skeptical philosopher* is inclined toward—one whose goal is not to find what is *True*, but only to argue about it, using quarrelsome words to maintain any position and discard whatever he pleases. Instead, let them be urged and encouraged...

<margin>For who can find out the limits of medicine?</margin> ...inspired <note>original: "voked," likely meaning provoked or called forth</note> by my example, as becomes good and sincere citizens of the medical <note>original: "Physicall"</note> and alchemical <note>original: "Spagyrick"</note> state and profession, to produce better things than these from their own experience. They should assist poor *Lazarus* <note>A biblical figure representing the sick and poor; the author argues for practical charity over theoretical debate.</note>, not with logical <note>original: "Sylogistical"</note> or merely ritual <note>original: "Levitical"</note> words, but with a Samaritan’s *help* and *aid*. 

If they shall do this, and cast away the marks <note>original: "Signatures"</note> of cursed laziness—the behavior of drones who stay in their hollow cracks and clamors, which at least make a terrible noise—they may become like bees. After that, in a grateful agreement, godly love, and mutual duty, they may join together with us into a union and alchemical honey-making <note>original: "Spagyrick mellifice"</note>, separating themselves from the common crowd and abstaining from all corruption <note>original: "fornication," used here to mean professional or spiritual impurity.</note>. They may *really* and indeed maintain and defend the excellence of chemistry against all those who reproach it, and with their ingenuity and learning—without envy or evil speaking—endeavor to make this, our good effort, better than the work itself. 

There is no doubt that after other secret sciences which still lie in the dark, that ancient, true, and philosophical medicine may soon be restored to its lost luster and ancient splendor. This medicine is not yet fully known because of the long passage and damage of time, as well as the unworthiness of our age (due to the combined sins of men). Its restoration will be to the most healthful advantage of all mankind and the due honor of alchemical physicians <note>original: "Spagyrick Phisitians"</note>. May that immense sea of divine mercy be pleased to use their endeavors and pains as an instrument and pen to accomplish such a healthful and saving work.

<meta>This page begins the main text of Paracelsus's "The First Book of Philosophy." It follows the frontispiece and introductory materials, establishing the foundational argument that all things in the universe share a single, common origin or "matter."</meta>

# THE
## First BOOK
### OF
# PHILOSOPHY
->Written to the Athenians<-
->BY<-
->**Theophrastus Paracelsus H. H. H.**<- <note>original: "Theoph. Parac." The initials H. H. H. likely refer to "Helvetius, Eremita, Hohenheim," referencing his Swiss heritage, his life as a hermit/wanderer, and his family name.</note>

---

### TEXT I.

<margin>All creatures in the world have only one principle <note>principle: a fundamental source, basis, or "first cause" from which things arise.</note>.</margin>
**A**LL created things are of a frail and perishing nature, and at first all had only one single principle or beginning. In this principle, all things under the vault of heaven <note>original: "Cope of Heaven," a term describing the sky as a cloak or covering over the earth.</note> were enclosed and lay hidden. This is to be understood in this way: that all things proceeded out of one *matter*, and not every particular thing out of its own private matter by itself. This common matter of all

<summary>Paracelsus introduces his philosophical system by asserting that all created things are mortal and originate from a single, unified source of matter rather than from individual beginnings.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, First Book of Philosophy, cosmology, unity of matter, principle of creation, Athenians</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Mystery" (Mysterium Magnum) from Page 1, elaborating on its role as the universal source of all created things and using an analogy of human biology to explain its nature.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>The First Book of Philosophy,</header>

<margin>What the Great Mystery is.</margin>
<margin>The Great Mystery is the mother of all things.</margin>
...things is the **Great Mystery** <note>original: "Great Myſterie." Also known in Latin as the *Mysterium Magnum*, this is Paracelsus's term for the primordial "mother" or womb of all creation—a chaotic, formless state containing the potential for all things.</note>, which no certain essence and prefigured or formed *idea* <note>idea: in this context, a spiritual archetype or blueprint that gives specific form to matter.</note> could comprehend, nor could it be defined by any property, as it was altogether void of color or any nature based on the elements. The scope of this **Great Mystery** is as vast as the firmament <note>firmament: the vault of the heavens or the sky.</note>. And this *Great Mystery was the mother of all the elements, and the grandmother of all the stars, trees, and creatures of flesh.* Just as children are born of a mother, so all created things—whether they possess senses or not—were uniformly brought out of the **Great Mystery**. Thus, the Great Mystery is the only mother of all perishable things. They all sprang from it, not in a sequence or continuation over time, but all at once in a single creation—sharing the same substance, matter, form, essence, nature, and inclination.

---

## TEXT 2.

<margin>How human urine is made.</margin>
That this *Mystery* was such as no other creature ever was or ever saw, and yet was the *first matter* <note>first matter: or *materia prima*, the original, unformed substance from which the universe was created.</note> from which all mortal things proceeded, can be best understood by looking at *human urine*. Urine is composed of *water, air, earth, and fire*, yet it is none of these individually, nor is it like any one of them. Nevertheless, by another process of generation, all the elements arise from it and then pass into a third generation. However, because urine is merely a created thing, there may be some 
<margin>The Great Mystery was not created.</margin>
difference between it and the Mystery. For the *Great Mystery is increased*, and was prepared by
->that<-

<vocab>Great Mystery, Idea, Elements, first matter, human urine, creation, Philosophy</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus defines the "Great Mystery" as the formless, universal source of all matter and elements, using human urine as a biological analogy for a substance that contains all four elements while remaining distinct from them.</summary>
<keywords>Great Mystery, Mysterium Magnum, First Matter, Four Elements, Cosmology, Alchemy, Paracelsus, Urine Analogy</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Mystery" as a non-reciprocal source of creation, explaining that while things come from it, they do not return to that primordial state once they have been formed into specific objects.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Written to the Athenians.</header>
<sig>A a 3</sig>

...that great Artificer <note>Artificer: a skilled craftsman or inventor; here referring to God or the creative force of nature.</note>. Never shall there be any like it; nor does this return or is it brought back again unto itself. *For just as cheese never becomes milk again, so neither does that which is generated* [from the Mystery] *return into its first matter* <note>first matter: or *materia prima*, the original, unformed substance from which the universe was created.</note>. And although all things indeed may at length be reduced to their original nature and condition, they do not return again to the *Mystery*. That which is once consumed can by no means be recovered again. But it may return into that which existed before the *Mystery*.

### T E X T. 3.

<margin>How the Great *Mystery* is the mother of all things.</margin>
Furthermore, although the *Great Mystery* <note>Great Mystery: the *Mysterium Magnum*, the primordial source of all creation.</note> is indeed the mother of all things, both *perceivable and unperceivable* <note>original: "sensible and insensible"</note>; yet not all things that grow, nor the living creatures, nor similar things, were formed directly within it. Instead, we should consider it this way: it left and assigned general mysteries to all things—that is, to humans and living creatures. To those of one kind, it gave a mystery to propagate themselves according to their own form. In the same way, it bestowed a unique mystery on every other thing to produce its own shape by itself. From the same origin also (which the *primary mystery* ordained), those mysteries spring from which another mystery may be produced. For a star (also known as *manure* <note>original: "mucke". In the philosophy of Paracelsus, "stars" or *astra* refer to the internal vital forces or essences of things. Here, he suggests decaying matter acts as the "star" or generative source for certain insects.</note>) is the mystery of beetles, flies, and gnats, etc. Milk is the mystery of cheese, butter, and such things. Cheese is the mystery of worms that breed in it. So again, worms are the mystery...

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>This page explains that once the Great Mystery has created something, that object cannot return to its original formless state, using the analogy of cheese never being able to turn back into milk. It further describes how the Great Mystery established specific "sub-mysteries" that allow living things and substances to propagate and transform into new forms.</summary>
<keywords>Great Mystery, first matter, Artificer, propagation, spontaneous generation, Paracelsus, transformation, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Mystery" (Mysterium Magnum), concluding an analogy regarding "perished cheese" to distinguish between the primordial, uncreated source and the specific, created mysteries that follow it.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>The first Book of Philosophy,</header>

<margin>There are two types of mysteries.</margin>
...of spoiled cheese <note>The author uses cheese as an analogy: once milk becomes cheese, it cannot return to milk; similarly, once the Great Mystery creates something, that thing remains distinct.</note>. Thus, there are two types of mysteries: the **Great Mystery**, which is uncreated, and all others of the same kind, which are called **special mysteries**.

---

## TEXT 4.

<margin>All things emerged from the Great Mystery at the exact same time.</margin>
Since it is clear that all perishable things sprang and arose out of the **uncreated mystery**, we must understand that no created thing was brought forth earlier or later than any other. Nor were things created separately; rather, they were brought forth all together at once. For the **Greatest Secret**—namely, the goodness of the Creator—placed or laid all things together within that which is uncreated. At that stage, they did not exist in their final form, essence, or with specific qualities; instead, everything lay hidden in the uncreated state, just as an image or a statue exists within a piece of wood. For just as the statue is not seen until the excess <note>original: "wast"</note> wood is carved away so that it may appear, so it is in the uncreated mystery. Both the physical or perceptible and the imperceptible <note>original: "sensible and insensible"</note> emerged and achieved their own form and type through a proper and established separation. This involved no violent hacking or hewing; instead, everything naturally transitioned into its own form and essence.

<margin>The wonderful skill of the Creator.</margin>
No craftsman could ever be found who was so precise and diligent in the work of separation, nor who possessed the skill to make even the smallest and lightest grain useful and imbue it with life.

<summary>This page explains that all creation emerged simultaneously from the "uncreated mystery" through a process of separation, likened to a sculptor revealing a statue hidden within wood.</summary>

### TEXT 5.

<margin>A comparison between the mystery and a medicine.</margin>
Understand it in this way: not as if a house were built out of the **Great Mystery** <note>The *Mysterium Magnum*, a term used by Paracelsus to describe the primordial, uncreated source of all things</note>, or as if living creatures were first gathered together and laid in a heap and then perfected, nor that other growing things were made that way either. Instead, consider how a **physician** makes a compound of many virtues <note>original: "vertues"; here referring to the specific healing powers or medicinal properties of plants and minerals</note>, even though the physical matter is only one thing. Within that substance, none of the virtues that lie hidden inside it are visible. 

<margin>How all things were in the mystery.</margin>
So we must think that all sorts of creatures under heaven were set in order and put into the **Great Mystery**—not perfectly according to their physical substance, form, and essence, but in another subtle manner of perfection (unknown to mortal man) by which all things were enclosed into one. We were all created from that which is frail and mortal, and we are born much in the manner of **Saturn** <note>In alchemical philosophy, Saturn represents the "prime matter" or a state of potential where all qualities exist in a dark, hidden, or "unseparated" state</note>, who, in the separation of himself, brings forth all forms and colors, yet none of them appear visible within him. 

Since the mysteries of Saturn yield such births, then the **Great Mystery** undoubtedly contains this miracle: in its separation, all other superfluous things are cut off. <margin>What kind of separation Saturn has.</margin> Yet, there is nothing so unnecessary or useless that it cannot bring forth some other growth or suitable matter from within itself.

## TEXT 10.

<margin>The first separation was made in created things.</margin>
<insert>Separation = Variety</insert>
<margin>The harvest is the end of all things that came out of the mystery.</margin>
<margin>The sympathy and antipathy of things is the cause of their mutation.</margin>
Inasmuch, then, as the *great mystery* <note>original: "mysterium magnum," the primordial matter or source from which all things were created in Paracelsian philosophy</note> was full of such Essence and Divinity, with the addition of the most eternal One, the SEPARATION occurred before any individual creature existed. When this began, every creature sprang forth and appeared in its own majesty, power, and free will. In this state, it shall continue to flourish even to the end of the world—or *that great harvest*—when all things shall be pregnant with fruit, which shall then be gathered and carried into the barn. *For the harvest is the end of its fruit, and it intends nothing other than the physical <note>original: "corporall"</note> destruction of all things.* 

And though the number of creatures is almost infinite, there is only one single harvest when all of Creation shall be reaped and carried into the barn. This harvest, the end of all things, will be no less admirable than that *great mystery* was wonderful in the beginning—notwithstanding that the free power of things is the cause of their mutual attraction <note>original: "affection"</note> and destruction. For there is nothing that does not possess both love and hatred <note>original: "sympathy and antipathy," the natural forces of attraction and repulsion between substances</note>. Free will flourishes and is active in virtues, but it is either a friend or a foe in our works. But these things do not belong to separation itself. For separation is the <term>sequestratrix</term> <note>original Latin: a female separator or divider; in alchemy, the force that distinguishes one substance from another</note> that gives to everything its own form and essence.

### TEXT 11.

<margin>The first elements rose out of the separation of the mystery.</margin>
<margin>What fire is.</margin>
<margin>What the air is.</margin>
When the *great mystery* <note>original: "mysterium magnum," the primordial source of all creation in Paracelsian thought</note> first separated all things, the *first separation was that of the elements*. Thus, before all other things, the elements broke forth into their own action and essence. The fire was made into the heavens and the wall of the firmament <note>the celestial sphere or the boundary of the visible universe</note>. The air was made into a void space, in which nothing appears or can be seen, occupying that place where there is no substance or physical <note>original: "corporall"</note> matter. This is the coffer <note>a storage chest</note> or enclosure of the invisible destinies. 

<margin>What the water is.</margin>
The water turned into liquid and took its place within the channels and hollows of the center, inside the other elements and the sky. This is the repository <note>original: "hutch," meaning a chest or storage box</note> of the nymphs and monsters of the sea. 

<margin>What the earth is. It is sustained by the pillars of the Archaltes.</margin>
The earth was solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note> into dry land. It is not supported by any of the other elements but is propped up by the pillars of the *Archaltes* <note>original: "Archaltes," a term used by Paracelsus to describe the occult or spiritual forces that sustain the physical weight of the world</note>. These are the strange and wonderful works of God. The earth is the chest for those things that grow and are nourished by it. This kind of separation was the beginning of all creatures and the first distribution of both these and all other living beings.

# Written to the Athenians.

<margin>The separation of fire and its nature.</margin>
The second separation followed the first, which emerged from the elements. Thus, everything that lay within the fire was transformed into the heavens. One part of it was formed into an ark <note>original: "Ark," meaning a chest or vessel for preservation</note> or cloister <note>original: "cloyster," an enclosed space</note>, while another part emerged from it like a flower from a stalk. In this way, the stars, planets, and everything else in the firmament <note>the celestial sphere or the sky</note> were brought forth.

<margin>The stars emerged from the second separation.</margin>
These sprang from the element, but not in the way a stalk grows with its flowers out of the earth (for those grow out of the earth itself). Instead, the 
<margin>How the stars emerged from heaven.</margin>
stars came out of the heavens through separation alone, just as "silver flowers" <note>likely a reference to the alchemical process of "arborization," where minerals crystallize into tree-like shapes</note> rise and separate themselves. Consequently, all the firmaments are separated from the fire. But before the firmament was separated from the fire, every single bit of it was merely the element of fire. For just as a tree in winter is nothing but a tree, but puts forth leaves, flowers, and fruit when spring arrives—provided that which can be separated is indeed separated—this marks the time of its harvest and separation. There was a harvest just like this in the separation of the *great mystery* <note>original: "mysterium magnum," the primordial source of all creation</note>, which could no longer withhold or delay itself.

They are separate from the element of Air, yet they must necessarily live within it and cannot change their location for another.

## TEXT 14

<margin>The separation of the water and its various forms.</margin>
Through the separation of the elements, the water was gathered into the place that the <term>mystery</term> <note>original: "mysterie"; in the philosophy of Paracelsus, this refers to the *Mysterium Magnum*, the primordial source or "mother" of all things</note> had assigned to it. Therefore, everything that lay hidden within the elemental power and property of water was more completely divided by a second separation. The water was split into many specific "mysteries," all of which had their <term>matrix</term> <note>the "womb" or originating source from which things grow</note> from the element of water. One part of it became fish, which exist in many forms and types—some like beasts, others salty. Much of it became sea plants, such as <margin>Sea plants: coral, trines, and citrons.</margin> *corals*, *trines*, and *citrons* <note>While "citron" usually refers to a fruit, in this context it refers to a "sea-citron," a type of marine organism or sea slug</note>. A great deal of it became sea monsters that act against the usual and natural course of the elements. A large portion became <margin>Nymphs, Sirens, Drames, Lorind, and Nesder.</margin> *Nymphs*, *Sirens*, *Drames*, *Lorind*, and *Nesder* <note>These are specific classes of water spirits or elemental beings in Paracelsian philosophy</note>. Some of these are rational creatures that possess something eternal within their bodies and reproduce; others die completely, and some are separated again over time. For the complete separation of the element of water has not yet finished. As the "great harvest" <note>likely referring to the apocalypse or the final cosmic resolution</note> approaches, new life may arise every year within the element of water. This separation occurred at the same moment as the separation of the other elements, as part of a single day’s work and through the process of <term>sequestration</term> <note>original: "sequestration," the act of setting apart or dividing materials</note>. Thus, everything living in the water was created and revealed all at once—in a single minute and moment of time—through this separation.

### TEXT 15.

<margin>The separation of the earth and its types.</margin>
In the same way, when the Element of Earth was separated from the rest, the earthly separation occurred—specifically, *the separation of all things that grow, or once grew, in or out of the earth*. For at the first creation, the four elements lay hidden in all things alike within the <term>Great Mystery</term> <note>original: "great mysterie"; the *Mysterium Magnum*, the primordial source or "womb" of all matter in Paracelsian philosophy</note>. These things were also separated in the same manner and in a single instant, and they were divided among themselves one after another by a second separation, which is elemental. Through this kind of elemental separation out of the Element of Earth, <term>perceptible and imperceptible</term> <note>original: "sensible and insensible"</note> things, as well as *those that are eternal and those that are not eternal*, were parted from one another, with every thing obtaining its unique essence and free power. Everything that was of a woody nature was made into wood.

<margin>The difference between minerals.</margin>
The next category was mines of metals. A third became *marcasite, talc, bismuth, garnets* <note>original: "pomegranate"; historically, garnets were often called "pomegranates" because their red crystals resemble the seeds of the fruit</note>, *metallic cobalt, millstone* <note>original: "milsto"</note>, and many other things. A fourth became precious stones of many sorts and shapes, as well as stones, sands, and lime. A fifth was made into fruit, flowers, herbs, and seeds. A sixth was made into *sentient living creatures*, some of which share in eternity, such as *humans*, while others do *not*, such as *calves, sheep, etc.* Of these, many kinds and differences might be listed; for many more types were separated in the earthly element than in any other.

<margin>Earthy things are propagated by seed.</margin>
For all things are propagated by the seed of two—that is, by the coming together of father and mother—which was not <insert>predestined</insert> <note>the original text ends with the fragment "pre-" and the catchword "destinated" at the bottom of the page</note>.

### TEXT 16.

<margin>How the temperaments were brought forth. <insert>contrast</insert></margin>
After the four Elements were, from the beginning, separated from each other out of the single matter (as has been said)—in which matter, nevertheless, their temperament and essence did not yet exist—*the temperaments and natures emerged through that separation.* <term>The hot and dry</term> qualities went into the heavens and the <term>firmament</term> <note>the sphere of the stars</note>, each part clinging to its own property. The *hot* and *moist* went into the air, by which the hot and moist are invisibly separated. The *cold* and *moist* turned into the Sea and the adjoining parts. The *cold* and *dry* declined into the earth and all earthy things. Even contrary things arose from the separation of the Elements, which have no likeness at all to the Elements they came from. 
<margin>Lime comes out of the fire, yet is not of a fiery nature.</margin>
An example of this is *lime*, which in respect to its own nature is not fire, even though it arises out of the fire. The cause of this is that during the separation of nature, the dissolution moved too far away from the fiery nature.

<margin>Four sorts of fire.</margin>
<margin>Different degrees of fire.</margin>
the Element; for fire has both cold and moist within it. There is a fourfold fire. Therefore, the colors that arise from fire are not always alike. One fire causes a white and azure <note>original: "azure"; a bright blue color</note> color. The dry fire makes a red and green. The moist fire makes an ashy and black. The moist fire casts a color like saffron and red. For this reason, one production <note>original: "procreation"; referring to what is generated or brought forth by the fire</note> is hotter than another, because one fire is of a higher or lower degree than another. Nor is there just one simple and single fire and no more, but there are some hundreds of fires, yet not a single one is of the same degree as another. The production <note>original: "procreation"</note> of each of them, therefore, comes from its own source <note>original: "subject"</note>, ordained as a kind of mystery.

## TEXT 17.

<margin>Various temperaments of water.</margin>
<margin>The differences between waters.</margin>
Neither did water obtain only one kind of <term>complexion</term> <note>original: "complexion"; in early science, this referred to the specific combination of the four qualities—hot, cold, moist, and dry—that defined a substance's nature</note>. For there were infinite waters within that Element, which yet were all truly waters. The philosopher cannot understand the Element of water as being only cold and moist in itself. It is a hundred times colder, rather than more moist, and yet it is not to be attributed to heat as much as to coldness. Nor does the Element of water exist <note>original: "live and flourish"</note> only in one degree of cold and moist; no, neither is it fully and wholly of a single degree. Some waters are springs <note>original: "fountains"</note>, which are of many sorts. Some are seas, which are also many and diverse. Others are streams and rivers, none of which is like another. Some watery Elements were distributed into

Whatever is made of water eventually turns back into water; that which is made of fire returns to fire; that of earth returns to earth, and that of air returns to air.

### TEXT 18.

<margin>What the mineral moistures are.</margin>
<margin>Why brimstone burns.</margin>
Similarly, we must think of the Element of earth in this way: all things that come from the earth retain its nature. And although mineral liquors <note>original: "mineral liquors"; likely referring to liquid chemical substances, acids, or fluid ores</note> might be mistaken for fire, they are not actually fire. <term>Brimstone</term> <note>original: "brimstone"; an archaic name for sulfur</note> does not burn simply because it is made of a fiery element. For something cold will burn just as well as something hot. That which burns to ashes is not the true Element of fire, but rather the "fire of the earth." This fire should not be mistaken for the Element itself. It is not the Element, but only the consumption <note>original: "wasting"</note> of the earth or its substance.
<margin>Water can be made to burn.</margin>

<margin>How a philosopher should name things.</margin>
...it watery fire. Again, although the "fire of the earth" <note>likely referring to sulfur or other combustible minerals</note> will burn and blaze, it should not be considered fiery in nature, even though it resembles fire. A philosopher is foolish and overly reliant on the senses <note>original: "sensuall"</note> if he names an element based only on what he perceives. Instead, he should understand that the element itself is something very different from such a fire as this. And why? Not everything that moistens is the Element of water.

<margin>Earth may be reduced to water.</margin>
Even the Element of earth can be turned into water, yet it remains earth still. Similarly, whatever is in the earth belongs to the Element of earth. For it exists and is defined by the properties of the source from which it originated and which it resembles.

<margin>Flint and chalcedony produce fire.</margin>
A person can strike fire out of a flint or <term>chalcedony</term> <note>original: "calcedony"; a type of silica mineral</note>. That spark is not elemental fire, but rather a powerful expression <note>original: "expression"; here meaning a manifestation or pressing out</note> resulting from extreme hardness.

### TEXT 19.

The Element of air contains many offspring within it, yet all of these are still merely air. Every philosopher should well understand that no element can produce something from itself that is not of its own nature.

<margin>Like produces like.</margin>
Like always gives birth to its like. Therefore, since air is invisible, it cannot produce anything visible from itself. And because it cannot be felt <note>original: "impalpable"</note>, it can produce nothing that can be touched. Therefore (as I might put it), it does <term>melosinate</term>.

Nevertheless, such types of miracles and consequences eventually dissolve back into the air, just as nymphs turn back into water—exactly as a man is consumed by rot and turned to earth because he came from there.

## TEXT 21.

<margin>What the fourth separation is.</margin>
As we have seen a threefold separation made out of the mystery <term>Mysterium</term> <note>The "Mystery" or "Mysterium Magnum" refers to the primordial source or "mother" of all things in alchemical philosophy</note> into three sorts of forms, it remains now that we consider the fourth and last separation of all, after which there shall be no more. For then all the others shall perish and be no longer a mystery. After the fourth, all things shall be reduced into their first principle, and only that shall remain which was before the *Great Mystery* and is eternal. This is not to be understood as if I could be turned into anything, or as though anything could be made of me after the last separation, except by death; for I shall be brought to nothing, just as regarding my beginning, I came out of nothing.

<margin>Back into their fundamental principle.</margin>
All things are brought again into their original source. When they are turned into nothing, then do they consist in their first Being. First of all, then, we must look for that which is the first of all. And what it is that goes into "nothing" is no less than a mystery. My soul within me was made of something; therefore, it does not become nothing, because it was formed from something. *But out of nothing, nothing is made; nothing is generated.* <note>original Latin: *Ex nihilo nihil fit*. This is a fundamental principle of ancient philosophy stating that matter cannot be created from nothing, used here to contrast the soul's origin with the body's dissolution.</note>

<margin>The difference between a man and an image.</margin>
A picture drawn on a table <note>original: "table"; here meaning a wooden panel or board used for painting</note>, as it is a picture, was doubtless made of something. But we were not made of "something" in the same way as an image appearing in the air. And why is that? It is because we came out of the *Great Mystery*, not out of the *pre-created mystery*.

<margin>He refers here to the body. A comparison between man and a wooden panel.</margin>
Therefore, we are brought to *nothing. If you wipe off a picture with a sponge so that nothing of it remains, the panel is just as it was before. Thus, all creatures shall be reduced to their first state—that is, to nothing. The reason all bodies must return into nothing is because of that which is eternal within rational bodies <note>original: "bodies rationall"; humans, who possess the faculty of reason and an eternal soul</note>.

<margin>The ultimate matter is most wonderful.</margin>
The last separation of this kind is the ultimate matter <note>original: *ultima materia*; in alchemy and Paracelsian philosophy, this is the final, most refined state of matter after all impurities and temporary forms have been stripped away</note>. Then many procreations, mixtures, conversions, alterations, transmutations, and similar things will be done—all of which are beyond a man's ability to discover.

## TEXT 23.

<margin>Which mortal things are eternal.</margin>
<margin>Man is a companion to the eternal.</margin>
The final separation is the dissolution of all creatures, where one thing consumes and perishes after another; through this process, the lifespan of all these things is understood. When creatures first existed, they contained no inherent total ruin within them, because a new seed always takes the place of the old, decayed thing. Thus, there is something eternal—not subject to ruin—within things that are mortal, achieved through the renewal of another seed. This is something the philosopher <note>original: "the Philosopher"; likely referring to Aristotle or the Scholastic school of philosophy, which Paracelsus often critiqued.</note> does not know. No seed admits or creates that which is eternal. Yet it does admit rot <note>original: "putrefaction"</note> when that which is eternal is taken back into the eternal. 

In this regard, man alone among all other creatures has that which is eternal within himself, joined with that which is mortal. According to what has been said, the mortal and eternal are joined together. Understand that the mortal part prepares an essence in the stomach <note>original: "stomack"; in Paracelsian medicine, the stomach was seen as the "inner alchemist" or *Archeus* that transformed physical matter into life-sustaining energy.</note> and supports the needs of the body. The only reason for this is so that the eternal part of man might live forever, and the mortal part might die according to its own frailty. As the body is, so is the eternal part that comes from that body. This is what confuses all philosophy: that the mortal should dominate and rule as it pleases over that which is eternal, and that this eternal part should also depend on man—who...

By this, he is made more a companion of that which is eternal than if both his mortal and eternal parts flowed from himself. From this, we may conclude that all creatures should live together—the rational and the irrational—one being useful to another, with the eternal planted into the mortal, and these two dwelling together. 
<margin>Destruction comes from opposing forces.</margin>
Therefore, philosophy teaches that all those things cannot be destroyed and consumed that live together *without squabbling and fighting, without guile and deceit, without good and evil.* <note>The author suggests that conflict arises from the binary of "good and evil" or opposing qualities; without these "contraries," a thing would be immortal.</note> Otherwise, destruction would occur if one part should oppose another. Those in whom the eternal does not dwell have no knowledge or judgment. But those things in which the eternal exists cannot be lacking in understanding. When things came into conflict so as to clash with one another, one eternal being was forced to give an account and make restitution for wrongs done to another. 
<margin>What things are subject to destruction.</margin>
And since restitution <note>original: "recompence"</note> belongs to the eternal realm, it must not be repaid by that which is mortal. And though bodies may live in peace and endure one another, if anything is left behind here, it is that which is eternal. 

Therefore, only that which is eternal within us is judged. And though one person may exploit or judge another, yet all mortal things which contain the eternal must die whether they wish to or not, so that only the eternal shall remain here, without the company of the body. Thus, the judgment is finished. For only that is eternal, and there is no more of it to come in the final destruction of every mortal thing. Now is the time of judgment, when the instability <note>original: "fickleness"</note> of all things under heaven is proclaimed. If there were no reason for a thing to be fragile, the creature would never die; no death would exist within it, and all things would be eternal. The only reason for death is this: because we mortals do not live in righteousness. We do not practice right judgment among ourselves toward one another, nor have we received the power of the eternal to judge. These matters belong to the eternal realm. Since this is how things must be, it is necessary that we all be brought and come together. Thus, we have discovered the dissolution of all things.

## THE
# Second Book,
### Text I.

<margin>The distinction between the creating Gods.</margin>
**S**eeing then that there was something by which, when it was separated, all things were created, we must first conclude that there is a distinction between the Gods. It is this: since created things are divided into the *eternal* and the *mortal*, the reason is that there was another creator of the mysteries <note>In the philosophy of Paracelsus, "mysteries" refer to the hidden, formative forces or "seeds" of all physical things.</note> besides the supreme and most high God. For the most high Creator must be the judge and corrector of all creatures. He would know how much was bestowed upon them so that they might do either good or evil, even though they did not receive those impulses directly from Him. 

Moreover, creatures are always <margin>Humanity's inclination is toward evil.</margin> urged on and provoked toward evil, compelled to it by the fates, the stars, and by the hellish one <note>original: "infernall one"; referring to the devil or a demonic influence.</note>. This could by no means have happened if they had proceeded directly from the most high God Himself. If we had, we would not be forced into those qualities of good and evil, but would have had free will in all things without any such violent instigation; 
<meta>catchword: yet</meta>

This hierarchy explains why humans are subject to the influence of fate, astrology, and evil rather than possessing pure free will directly from the highest divine source.</summary>

<margin>* or, They were ordained from the beginning.</margin>
Yet nevertheless, the creature <note>Refers to humanity as created beings.</note> does not have enough wisdom to know good or evil, or to understand the eternal and the mortal. For there are *many fools and madmen, and scarcely one wise man in a thousand; most are false prophets, teachers of lies, and masters of folly and ignorance*, who are considered most eminent, though they are nothing of the kind. And the reason is plain: for we are such creatures whose *masters* teach us no perfect good, but instead we are seasoned by the mortal God <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, the "mortal God" refers to an intermediate creative power or demiurge (like the stars or the "Archeus") that shapes the physical, decaying world, distinct from the supreme Eternal God.</note>, who had some power in the *great mystery* <note>original: *mysterium magnum*; the primordial source or "chaos" from which all things were separated and created.</note>; yet *they are ordained by the Eternal for judgment, both to themselves and to us.*

### T ᴇ x ᴛ 2.

<margin>The four Elements are the mothers of all things.</margin>
Now, if it were necessary that all things that were made should consist of and proceed from only four—as we know they did through the process of separation—then those four alone must be the matrices <term>original: "matrixes"; the wombs or formative environments</term> of all creatures, which we call the Elements. And though every creature is still an element, or may have some share of the element, it is not like the physical element itself, but like the spirit of the element. Nothing can subsist without an element. Nor can the elements themselves stand together <note>The author argues that elements do not mix to form substances; rather, every thing is governed by one specific dominant element.</note>. There is nothing that consists of four, three, or even two elements; rather, one element stands by itself apart, and every creature has only one element. They are completely blind who take that which is *moist* for the element of water, or that which *burns* for the element of fire. We must not limit

<meta>catchword: an</meta>

<summary>The author explains that humanity's lack of true wisdom stems from being shaped by a "mortal God" rather than the supreme Eternal. He then introduces the Four Elements as the unique "matrices" or mothers of all things, asserting that each creature belongs to only one specific element rather than a mixture of all four.</summary>

<header>Written to the Athenians.</header>

<margin>What an Element is.</margin>
An Element is related to a body, substance, or quality; what we see, however, is only the container or receptacle <note>original: "subject or receptacle"</note>. The Element itself is a Spirit; it lives and grows within those physical things, just as the soul lives in the body. This is the "first matter" <note>In alchemy, the primordial, formless state of all things.</note> of the Elements, which can neither be seen nor felt, and yet it is present in all things.

<margin>What the first matter of an Element is.</margin>
The first matter of the Elements is nothing other than the life which living creatures possess. If any creature dies, it no longer exists within any specific Element, but remains only in the "ultimate matter" <note>The final state of matter after it has lost its specific form or life-force.</note>, in which there is no taste, power, or virtue.

## Text 3.

<margin>All things consist of the four Elements.</margin>
Just as all things that could be created were made from four "mothers"—namely, the four Elements—you should further note that those four Elements were sufficient for all things that were to be created. It was not necessary for there to be more or fewer. In mortal things, no more than four natures can exist. However, in immortal things, the temperaments <note>A state of perfect balance that does not decay, unlike the volatile elements.</note> may exist, even though the Elements themselves cannot.

<margin>How the Element differs from the temperament.</margin>
Whatever I call an <term>elementure</term> <note>A physical thing composed of the elements and thus subject to decay.</note> can be dissolved. But on the contrary, the temperament <note>original: "temperature"</note> cannot be dissolved. For its condition is such that nothing can be added to it or taken away from it; nothing within it can rot or perish. And seeing as the elemental condition is mortal, as has been said, we must know that all things exist in four natures, and that every nature keeps the name of its specific Element.

<margin>The names of the four Elements.</margin>
For example, the Element of fire is hot; the Element of earth is cold; the Element of water is...

<summary>Paracelsus defines an Element as an invisible, spiritual life-force inhabiting physical matter, distinguishing between dissolvable mortal "elementures" and indissoluble immortal "temperaments."</summary>

<margin>The nature of the Elements is simple.</margin>
...is moist, and the element of air is dry. Here we must consider that each of these natures is unique and distinct on its own. For fire is only hot; it is neither dry nor moist. The earth is only cold; it is neither dry nor moist. The water is only moist; it is neither hot nor cold. The air is only dry; it is neither hot nor cold. Therefore, they are called Elements because they possess only a single, simple nature rather than a dual one. However, their presence in all living creatures must be understood as an Element that can exist within a physical substance and body where it can perform its function. The most important knowledge regarding the Elements is this: every one of them has only one simple nature—either moist, dry, cold, or hot.

<margin>The elements and simple spirits.</margin>
This is due to the nature of spirits. For every spirit has a simple nature, not a double one, and the Elements follow this same rule.

# TEXT 4.

<margin>The origin of Colic.</margin>
Although we mortals have compound qualities within us, such as being both hot and moist, this works much differently than the ancient philosophers imagined. For <term>colic</term> <note>original: "Colick"; a term for severe abdominal pain often thought to be caused by elemental imbalances.</note> belongs to the element of fire; yet it is not a combination of heat and dryness, but is purely hot. The same applies to the other <term>complexions</term> <note>original: "complexions"; the physical and mental constitution of a person, historically determined by the balance of the four humors or elements.</note>. Therefore, if we find any disease characterized by both heat and dryness, we may assume that two Elements are involved: one in the liver and another in the spleen, and likewise in other body parts. There are never two Elements in a single organ. It is certain that every organ has its own specific element, a matter we leave to <term>physicians</term>...

<margin>The elements are not mixed.</margin>
...to define. However, it cannot easily be claimed that two elements should exist together, or that one and the same element should be both hot and moist. Nor can there be any such compound. There are no compounded <term>elements</term>, for the reason given previously. Where there is heat, there is neither cold, nor dryness, nor moisture. Likewise, where there is coldness, none of the other qualities are present. The same may be said of moisture and dryness. Every <term>element</term> is simple and solitary by itself, not mixed in a composition. The possibility that <term>philosophers</term> <note>The author likely refers to Aristotelian or Scholastic philosophers who argued that elements could combine and transform into one another.</note> discuss regarding a joining together of the elements amounts to nothing. For no element of water has any heat in it, nor can there be any heat in moisture. Every element stands alone. Likewise, cold cannot by itself endure dryness; it exists purely on its own. And let this much be said so that the true essence of the elements may be understood. All dryness is a breakdown <note>original: "dissolution"</note> of cold. Just as moisture and dryness cannot be mixed, even less can coldness and dryness, moisture and heat, or heat and dryness join or exist together. For just as heat and cold are opposites, so heat and cold also stand in opposition to moisture and dryness.

How an element and compounds differ.

the difference between the elements and compounds is this: An element—and a mystery as well—can generate something else out of itself. What fire is and does.

<margin>
The element of water softens metals and stones.
</margin>
The element of water itself possesses such moisture that it can soften stones and hard metals. Physical <note>original: "substantiall"</note> water takes away that excellent power of softening <note>original: "mollifying"</note>, so that its power is not perfect.

<margin>
The element of air dries most scorchingly.
</margin>
The element of air is so dry that it can dry up all waters in an instant. But that force is taken away and weakened by physical air.

<margin>
The element of earth cools most intensely.
</margin>
The element of earth is so cold that it would reduce all things to their final state <note>original: "ultimate matter"</note>, such as turning water into crystal, and <unclear>*</unclear> into *duelech* <note>original: "duelech"; a Paracelsian term for a painful stone or tartar-like deposit formed within the body.</note>, living creatures into marble, and trees into giants. The fundamental principle of the elements that can be understood is this: to realize that they possess such excellent and rapid activity or effectiveness <note>original: "efficacy"</note> that nothing else can be found or even imagined like them. The things containing these elements are attracted and taken up by them, just as fate may become...

## Text 6.

<margin>A comparison between fire and the soul.</margin>
<margin>What fire is.</margin>
<margin>What the properties of all the elements are.</margin>
So that we may more fully understand what an element is, we must know that an element is nothing but a soul. This does not mean it has the same essence as a human soul, but rather that it has something similar to it. The difference between the soul of an element and the eternal soul is this: the soul of the elements is the life of all creatures. 

The fire that burns is not the element of fire as we see it; rather, its soul—which we cannot see—is the true element and life of fire. Now, the element of fire may be just as present in a green stick <note>original: "green stick"; fresh wood that has not been dried or seasoned.</note> as it is in a flame, but the actual "life" is not the same in the wood as it is in the fire. This, then, is the difference between the soul and the life. If fire lives, it burns; but if it remains in the soul—that is, in its elemental state—then it cannot burn. 

Nor does it follow that a cold thing must necessarily come from a cold element, for it often comes from a hot one. Indeed, many cold things come from the element of fire. Whatever grows does so from the element of fire, though in another form. Whatever is fixed <note>original: "fixt"; in alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that is solid, stable, and resistant to change by fire.</note> comes from the element of earth. That which nourishes comes from the element of air. And that which consumes comes from the element of water. To grow is the property of fire alone. When that fails or goes out, there is no more growth. Were it not for the element of earth, there would be no end to growth. It is the earth that...

It assigns specific roles to the four elements: fire for growth, earth for stability, air for nourishment, and water for consumption.</summary>

## Text 7.

<margin>The true elements are imperceptible <note>original: "inſenſible"; unable to be perceived by the five senses.</note>.</margin>
But although the elements are thus hidden and exist altogether invisibly and imperceptibly in other things, they still have the power to bring forth their mysteries. Thus, the element of fire sent forth the firmament <note>original: "Firmament"; the sphere of the heavens or the sky.</note>; not in regard to physical bodies, but in regard to their elemental essence. The Sun has another body besides what it received from the element of fire. Yet this heat is essentially within it. Its heat does not come from motion and rotation; rather, it comes from itself.

<margin>The Sun is hot even if it stood still.</margin>
The Sun would be warm as well as shine even if it stood still and never moved at all. Crystal <note>original: "Chryſtall"; likely referring to the "crystalline heaven" or the substance of the firmament in early cosmology.</note> made the Sun out of the element of fire, though the Sun has no other body but what it received from the element of fire.

<margin>The origin of the elements' bodies.</margin>

### Text 8.

<margin>Elementary things differ from one another.</margin>
Just as the fire brought forth various shapes and essences, the element of air produced the same in its own way. Fate <note>In this context, "fate" likely refers to the specific governing influence or vital force of that element.</note> comes from the air, yet it is not like any of the remaining three. Every creation produced both rational and irrational <note>original: "unreasonable"</note> creatures within itself. 
<margin>There are rational and irrational creatures in every element.</margin>
Heaven, as well as the element of earth, has rational creatures in the firmaments. The same is also true of the earth and water. Now, who is he that can tell us what the truth is...

### T E X T 9.

<margin>The various procreations of the Elements.</margin>
It is most obvious that from a single seed, the root sprouts into many shoots, then into the stalk; afterward the branches shoot out, and finally the flower, fruit, and seed appear. The various creations <note>original: "procreations"</note> coming out of the four elements follow this same pattern. All these creations that come from one element stick closely together, just as an <term>herb</term> <note>original: "hearb"</note> grows from a single seed—even though they are not all identically <note>original: "promiscuously"</note> similar to their source seed. The creatures made from the water include humans, other living beings, and the food for both. Each element clearly reveals its own <term>signature</term> <note>The "Doctrine of Signatures" was the belief that the physical appearance of a thing (plant, mineral, etc.) revealed its inner properties or medicinal uses.</note>, needs, and nourishment <note>original: "sustentation"</note>. It also hints at its path and arrival, which can be easily recognized by the stars. This is not because the stars guide and govern us, but because they keep pace with us and mirror the internal movements of our bodies.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four elements and their inhabitants, transitioning from the nature of the firmament (the heavens or sky) to a critique of human-centered perspectives that prioritize the element of earth over others.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>The Second Book of Philosophy</header>

<margin>A mistake about celestial minerals.</margin>
[The other elements] have their minerals too, just as we do. The celestial <term>firmament</term> <note>The "firmament" refers to the heavens or the sky, which in this period was often considered a physical element or structure.</note> has minerals consisting of both flowers and stones, which we may rank among the miracles. However, we may easily be deceived here and quickly run aground when we insist <note>original: "stickle"</note> so much on counting natural processes as prodigies <note>original: "prodies"; extraordinary events or omens</note>, or believing that this or that hue of the firmament foreshadows some singular event. In this way, we predict the future like prophets, whereas we should instead conclude that such things happen according to the natural course of the firmament. But if any such thing should happen at any time, we should believe that such was our course and state. Meanwhile, if any part of the elements is faulty, that same flaw will weaken the rest, for all things should run in a perfect and uninterrupted course. And though the other three elements serve to nourish us, they are also ready to serve the firmament, the air, and the water, as well as the things that are within them. One thing is nourished by another, like many trees in an orchard. We may take notice of the slips and failings of the firmament, just as the firmament observes our own defects. The same may be said of all the rest.

## TEXT II.

<margin>Earthly men are not happy.</margin>
It is a silly and vain philosophy to place all happiness and eternity in our element of earth. It is a foolish opinion to boast that we alone, out of all creatures, are the most noble. There are more worlds than one, nor are there none besides...

...confides us in our own. But this ignorance is much more capital <note>original: "capitall"; here meaning serious or primary</note>: that we do not know those beings who are made of the same element as us, such as the <margin>What the **Nocturnals** and **Gnomes** are.</margin> ***Nocturnals***, ***Gnomes***, and so on. Even though they do not live in the clear glory of heaven, nor have any light from the **firmament** <term>the heavens or the sky</term>—hating what we love and loving what we hate—and even though they are not like us in form, essence, or sustenance, there is still no reason to wonder. For they were created as such in the **Great Mystery** <note>original: "great Mystery"; in Paracelsian philosophy, the *Mysterium Magnum* is the primal matter or source from which all things were created</note>. 

We are not the only beings who were created; there are many more whom we do not know about. Therefore, we must conclude that there were more bodies than just one simple kind shut up in the Great Mystery. Although, in general, there was only the eternal and the mortal within it, no one can say in what various shapes and kinds they were brought forth. This doubt will be entirely removed when the eternity of all those things meets together <note>Referring to the end of time or the final restoration of all things</note>. Then, certainly, many unknown things shall be fully discovered and revealed in many ways—not only regarding those things that possess the eternal within themselves, but also those things that have supported and nourished that eternal essence.

<margin>The eternal is twofold.</margin>
There is a twofold eternity: one of kingdom and dominion; the other of decoration and honor.

<margin>Flowers have the eternal in them.</margin>
To suggest that flowers should not be eternal is completely contrary to **Philosophy**. Although they wither and perish, they shall ultimately appear at the general gathering of all things. There is nothing created out of the Great Mystery that shall not have an image beyond the firmament.

<meta>catchword: They</meta>

<summary>The author argues that human ignorance extends to elemental beings like Gnomes and Nocturnals, asserting that all created things, including flowers, possess an eternal image that will be revealed at the end of time.</summary>

## TEXT 12.

<margin>How the **Great Mystery** may be known.</margin>
<margin>There are only four elements in all things.</margin>
There should be neither more nor fewer than four "mothers" <note>In alchemy and Paracelsian philosophy, the "mothers" refer to the four elements (Earth, Air, Fire, and Water) as the source of all things.</note> of all things, as all births <note>original: "procreations"</note> show. It is not that the **Great Mystery** <term>the *Mysterium Magnum*, or the primal matter from which the universe was created</term>, which we are now discussing, can be fully explained through a universal demonstration of what it was like in the beginning. Instead, the Great Mystery is known and understood through the "last mysteries"—the final results—and by the creations that sprang and proceeded from the first. It is not the beginning, but the end that makes a person a master and a philosopher. The knowledge of a thing according to its perfect nature is only discovered at the end of its existence.

Possibly more elements could have been created than there are now; however, in the complete knowledge of all things, only four are to be found. Although we might assume it would have been easy for God—who created only four—to have made many more, when we see that all mortal things consist of only four, we can conclude that more than these could not exist together. It is most likely that when these four elements perish, others shall arise according to a different essence, unlike the previous ones; or that after the destruction of the creatures already made, there shall be a new **Great Mystery**, the knowledge of which will be greater and better than the first. We do not present this here as a fundamental principle <note>original: "fundamentall"</note>, yet whoever wishes to understand the beginning of the world must...

<summary>The author argues that the true nature of the Great Mystery is revealed through its final creations rather than its origins, maintaining that the universe is governed by exactly four fundamental elements.</summary>
<keywords>Great Mystery, four elements, four mothers, alchemy, philosophy, creation, mortality</keywords>
