<margin>Four worlds for the four Elements.</margin>
...we must necessarily consider that it had its origin in the Elements. Just as there are four Elements, there are four worlds, and in every one of them, a unique kind of being is taught how to survive according to its needs.

### Text 13.

<margin>The Elements are not all present in all things.</margin>
Even though all things exist within these four Elements, we do not mean that all four Elements are present in every thing, or that all four dwelt in every creature. The reason is that the world which is separated and created <note>original: "procreated"</note> from the element of fire has no need for air, water, or earth. Likewise, the world of air needs none of the other three. This is also true for the earth and the water. Regarding the elements, we do not teach that the world cannot be preserved without all four Elements; rather, we teach that every thing is preserved by that single element from which it originated.

And while I do not deny that the firmament <term>the heavens or the sky, often viewed in this period as a physical, crystalline sphere or a source of celestial influence</term> nourishes the world through its elementary virtues <margin>How the Firmament nourishes the earth.</margin>—which descend upon the earth in a fiery form—that nourishment is not strictly necessary. Nor will the world perish on its own, for it has enough within itself to sustain itself, just as the other world maintains itself without the help of the earth. For example: the "earth" belonging to the water contributes nothing to the water's actual essence; nor does the "water" belonging to the earth contribute to the earth's essence. The same applies to the air. However, it is not enough for every world to exist alone or by itself within its Element; rather, <margin>What the light of heaven is.</margin> the light from heaven acts as a kind of extract <note>original: "extract of the"</note> of the...

<summary>This page argues that while there are four elements, each element creates its own self-contained "world" where creatures do not depend on the other three elements for their basic survival. It also explains that while the heavens (firmament) provide nourishment to the earth, the elements are inherently self-sustaining.</summary>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>The second Book of Philosophy,</header>

<margin>Tronus, Turas, Samies.</margin>
...the four Elements, which are most excellent in their full and perfect nature <note>original: "propriety"</note>. But let no one think that the sun or the planets received their brilliance <note>original: "lustre"</note> or motion from the element of fire, but rather from the Mystery <term>a Paracelsian term referring to the hidden, spiritual source or primordial matter of things</term>. The brightness of the firmament that shines upon the world did not flow from the element of fire, but from the Mystery. The earth produces *Tronus*, the water produces *Ture*, and the air produces *Samies*: these do not come from the element itself, but from the Mystery, although they exist within the element. In this way, the four worlds that emerged from the Mysteries agree to help each other, and to nourish and sustain one another. This does not happen because of the nature of the elements, for they are the elements themselves.

### T E X T 14.

<margin>The source of man's life, sight, etc.</margin>
It is not from the elements that man lives, sees, hears, and so on, but from the Mysteries—or rather, from the monarchy <note>original: "monarchie"; likely referring to the supreme governing power of nature or a divine order</note>. This is true for all other things as well. The elemental body is merely an inn and a meal <note>original: "repast"</note>. Know also that whatever is eternal comes from the Mystery and is one with it. Dogs die, but their Mystery does not. Man dies, but his Mystery survives—and even more so his soul, by which he is many degrees more excellent than a dog. The same can be said of everything that grows. <margin>The Mystery of all things shall at last be revealed.</margin> This is the source of the common mistake: people think that all creatures that ever existed will appear in their physical forms <note>original: "essentially"</note> as they do now; rather, they will appear mystically in the final, great "New Mystery." <margin>What a mystery is</margin> We do not say that the Mystery is a substance <note>original: "essence"</note> like that which is immortal, but that it is perfectly a mystery.

<margin>How the mystery and the elements differ.</margin>
The element of fire has a mystery within it, from which the other three receive their light, brilliance, influence, and growth—and not from the element itself. Those mysteries may also exist without an element, just as an element may exist without a mystery.

<margin>What the elements are and what kind of mysteries they possess.</margin>
Observe further that the element of air has a mystery within it, by which all the other three, and the air itself, are nourished. This nourishment is not provided elementally by the air itself, but mystically by the element.

The element of earth has within it a mystery of dwelling <note>original: "mansion"</note> and fixation <term>the alchemical process of making a substance stable, solid, or non-volatile</term>. By virtue of this, the other elements continue and increase so that nothing perishes.

The element of water has a mystery of sustenance <note>original: "sustentation"</note> for all the others and preserves everything within them from destruction. In this respect, there is a difference between an element and a mystery: one is mortal and corruptible, coming from the elements; the other is durable within the final great mystery, in which all things shall be renewed, but nothing shall be made that did not exist before.

### T E X T 15.

<margin>The elements are separate.</margin>
We conclude, then, that all the elements cannot be joined together. Instead, they exist in isolation and are entirely unmixed—being either airy, fiery, earthy, or watery.

<margin>The elements nourish themselves.</margin>
We have also established this: that every element maintains itself and whatever originates from it as its own world. Therefore, a medicine made from the element of water will do no good for those things that belong to the element of earth or any other element; <margin>Nymphs, etc.</margin> it will only benefit the nymphs and sirens <note>original: "Nymphs, Syrenes"; in this context, these refer to the elemental spirits or beings believed to inhabit the water</note>, and...

<summary>The author explains that each of the four elements contains a unique "mystery" responsible for qualities like light, nourishment, and stability, distinguishing the immortal spiritual essence from the mortal physical element. He further asserts that the elements exist as distinct, unmixable "worlds," meaning medicines must match the specific element of the being they are intended to treat.</summary>

and such like. Thus, a medicine of the earth will not help the other three worlds, but only the living creatures of its own world. And so it is with the air: there are diseases and physicians—both skillful and unskillful—in the air, which have their particular motion there, as in their own world. The same may be said of fire.

<margin>The Nymphs mate with earthly things.</margin>
Now, if it should happen at any time that the Nymphs <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, Nymphs are elemental spirits of water.</note> mate <note>original: "couple"</note> with earthly things and beget children, that is to be attributed to the faculty or power of abduction <term>original: "ravishment"; here referring to the mystical "rapture" or drawing of a being from one elemental plane into another</term>.

<margin>So do Melusines and Trifertes.</margin>
Airy things, such as the Melusines <note>original: "Melofines"; spirits often depicted as serpent-tailed women, here classified as elemental beings of the air</note>, may abduct earthly things. The Trifertes <note>original: "Triferies"; likely referring to elemental spirits of the fire</note> are made out of the fire by earthly things. If, then, those three foreign worlds plant men in our world, as we have said, they are to be known in their whole essence as Gods in respect to us, because of that huge distance and very strange essence which they possess.

<margin>How elements may be joined.</margin>
But on the other hand, if any of us are caught away by them, there is a reciprocal abduction from us to them. Thus, one element has no need of another: one is but the cabinet or container <note>original: "receptacle"</note> of the other. Just as water and earth are separate from each other, so air and fire have their particular portions <note>original: "lotts"</note>, without any other contact <note>original: "contiguity"</note>; they are like walls, according to the inclination of the mysteries within all four elements.

<margin>There is one Judge from eternity. The source of the variety of religions.</margin>
There will be only one Judge who has eternal power, and who has been the sole Judge throughout all ages. This is the cause of all religions and the origin of religious men worshipping many gods—all of which customs are false and erroneous. For there was never any other but one God, who is the eternal Judge. It is a blasphemous foolishness to worship a mortal, frail, perishing, rotten creature instead of the author of all things and the ruler of eternity. Whatever is mortal has no power to rule and reign. There is, then, only one way and one religion, and it is madness to claim there are more.

When all creatures return to their predestinations in this way, then there will be a mystery. <margin>
Mortal things leave behind that which is immortal.

...without an element, and without a present essence; but the things that shall then remain will be more temperate and incorruptible. This must not be understood as referring to the spirit, but to nature, with this evidence: that something eternal takes the place <note>original: "cometh in the room"</note> of that which is mortal. For if a non-sentient plant perishes, its place is supplied by that which is eternal. Nor is there any one frail or mortal thing in all the world which does not substitute something eternal in its place. Nothing is empty or vain; no corruptible thing was created without a succession of that which is eternal. When all creatures come to an end, then those things that are eternal shall meet and come together, not only as nourishment, but rather to serve the function <note>original: "office"</note> or chief rule of nature in both the mortal and eternal realms. Thus, the eternal is a sign or token of the dissolution of nature, and not the beginning or principle of created things; it exists in all things, and no nature is devoid of it. And although the spirits of fate <note>original: "Fatalls"</note>, such as the <term>Melosines</term> <note>Water spirits from European folklore, often depicted as women with the tails of serpents or fish.</note> and Nymphs, shall leave the eternal behind them, we shall say nothing of their decay <note>original: "corruptions"</note> at this time.

<margin>
There is a fourfold decay of the creatures.
</margin>
As there are four worlds, so we must know that there is a manifold decay <note>original: "putrefaction"</note>. There is an earthy, there is an airy, there is a fiery, and a watery decay. Everything, and whatever was created with it—together with the eternal part that remains—is brought and turned to destruction. Yet those four types of decay shall bring back their eternal essence into a single likeness <note>original: "similitude"</note> with renown and glory, not through its works, but through its essence.

<margin>The Great *Turban* worshipped as *God*.</margin>

The Most High over all does not speak with mortals, nor does He send His *angels* to them from His throne and dwelling place to declare such things; rather, those things are fore-known and understood from the Great *Turban*, which many *Pagans* and *Jews*, clouded in their true sense and understanding, have worshipped as a *God*.

## TEXT 21.

For example: when a child is born, at the same time the *Evester* is born with him, and it is constantly manifest in him, so that it gives omens from the cradle to the very hour of death, and can show what will become of that infant. So, when one is ready to die, death does not seize him until the *Evester* has first passed sentence, either by a blow, bruise, or fall, or some other such occurrence. <margin>The Evester remains in the world after death.</margin> 
The Evester <term>Evester</term> <note>original: "Eveſter"; in Paracelsian philosophy, a spiritual "double" or subtle body that exists alongside every person and object, carrying their essence and destiny.</note> is united to the eternal. For a man's Evester remains on the earth after his death and suggests, in its own way, whether the man is in bliss or misery. We should not say that it is the spirit or soul of a man—as common people say—or that it is the dead man walking. Rather, it is the dead man's Evester, which does not depart from here until the final moment when all things shall be gathered together. This Evester performs remarkable things. 
<margin>The Saints performed miracles by their Evester.</margin> 
Holy men performed miracles by their Evester alone. Just as the sun gives off its heat, nature, and essence through its shining, so it is with the divining and prophetic Evesters within us, which we should trust. These rule and guide sleep, foolish dreams, the foreshadowing of things to come, the nature of things, reason, desires <note>original: "concupiſcences"</note>, and thoughts.

# THE
## Third Book.

### Text I.

<margin>Nothing exists without a body.</margin>
**E**verything that has a being must of necessity have a body. The manner and reason for this is so that we may know it is like a smoky spirit that has neither substance nor body, nor can it be felt. And though it is neither of these, yet both bodies and substances may proceed from it. Thus we may understand fuming arsenic <term>arsenic</term> <note>original: "Arfnicke." In alchemy, arsenic was often associated with volatility and the "smoke" produced during chemical transformation.</note>: after a body is generated, no more of that spirit can be seen, any more than if it had all been turned into a physical body. Yet this is not the case, for it still remains in its most subtle form in that place of generation. And so both the visible and the invisible are brought forth together through separation.

<margin>How all things are embodied.</margin>
In this way and manner, all things are propagated. Wood still has a surviving spirit from which it is separated. So do stones and all other things, without exception. For their essence <term>essence</term> <note>The fundamental, spiritual nature or quality of a thing that remains even if its physical form changes.</note> still remains just as it was when separated from them.

<margin>What man is and how he is made.</margin>
Man likewise is nothing but a relic and the remainder of

In alchemy, this is the waste material or residue left after refining a substance. Paracelsus suggests here that the physical human body is the material residue left over after spiritual separation.</note>, yet he is a creature most subtle in spirit. Indeed, he is that very spirit—that is, a discovery or sign <margin>A twofold eternal nature.</margin> of a twofold eternal nature: one of *Caleruthum*, the other of *Meritorium*. <margin>What *Caleruthum* is.</margin> *Caleruthum* <note>original: "Caleruth." A Paracelsian term representing the first or natural eternal principle—the primordial essence of matter.</note> is a mark of discovery in the first eternal. This seeks or desires the other, which is God. The cause of this is natural, because all things are drawn to and strive <margin>Like seeks its like.</margin> for that from which they originated; they desire those natures that are nearest to them. Whatever the Creator gave or used when He made a thing, that very same thing the created thing also earnestly desires and presses after. 

Yet we must know that the creature does not desire its Creator by nature or natural instinct, but rather seeks after the substance out of which it came. Thus, man's body does not desire God, but rather the matter out of which it was separated, for the body was not taken directly out of God. And that matter is the life and habitation where the eternal *meritorium* <note>original: "meritorium." A Paracelsian term likely referring to the divine or "earned" spiritual essence—the spark of grace—that dwells within the physical form.</note> dwells; in this way, everything returns to its own essence.

Now, since everything is greedily desirous of its origin <note>original: "originall"</note>—namely, of the mystery out of which it proceeded—we are further to consider that this mystery is everlasting life. That which comes from there is mortal; nevertheless, within the mortal there remains that which is eternal—namely, the soul—as may be learned elsewhere.

It focuses on how everything originates from an invisible "first matter" and eventually returns to it.</meta>

<header>The third Book of Philosophy</header>

<margin>How corruptible things return to their first state.</margin>
Even though corruptible things must return to their former state, it is so that durable things may be joined together, and thus a collection and union of things may be made. The form and substance of all things—both those that perish and those that are permanent—originate from the **spirit of smoke** <note>original: "spirit of smoak." In Paracelsian philosophy, this refers to a subtle, vaporous, or gaseous state of matter from which physical objects condense.</note>, just as hail or lightning comes from a cloud. These things have a physical body, but the matter from which they originate is invisible.

<margin>All things proceed from the invisible.</margin>
We must conclude that all things proceed from the invisible, but without causing any hurt or damage to it, and that matter always has the power to renew the same thing again. For this reason, the entire world passes away like a ship and returns again to the same matter of the **spirit of smoke**, and it engenders <note>original: "gendreth." To produce or give rise to.</note> and nourishes without any tangible essence. In this respect, that which was first may be brought forth a second time.

<margin>Platonic emanation</margin>
By this we also know that no creature was "begotten" <note>The author distinguishes between biological "begetting" and the "procreation" of matter from a spiritual source.</note>, but rather made and procreated. For the Highest Good <note>Refers to God or the Creator.</note> ordained in the beginning that everything should emerge from the invisible and be made physical, and then be separated from the physical body to become invisible again. Then all things are coupled and united again and brought back to the **first matter** <term>first matter</term> <note>original: "first matter" (*materia prima*). The fundamental, formless substance from which all things are created and to which they return.</note>. And though they are so united, there is still some difference and diversity among them. One receives hospitality from another, and one gives hospitality to another <note>original: "entertainement." In this context, it refers to the way one substance or quality resides within or is hosted by another.</note>. That **first matter** is the home of all things, both those with senses and those without; all must return to that place and condition. Whether they are rational or irrational, nothing can escape this change, but shall certainly return and hasten to the dwelling from which it came.

# Written to the Athenians.

<margin>The body is a solidified vapor.</margin>
Every physical body or tangible substance is nothing but a solidified vapor <note>original: "curdled fume." In this context, "curdled" means thickened or coagulated from a fluid or gaseous state.</note>. From this, we may conclude that there are many types of solidification: one for wood, another for stones, and a third for metals. However, the body is nothing but a vapor, rising like smoke out of the matter or <term>matrix</term> <note>The environment, source-substance, or "womb" in which something is formed.</note> in which it exists.

<margin>Bodies are formed, as it were, out of a vapor.</margin>
Thus, everything that grows out of the earth is a vapor rising out of the moisture of **Mercury** <note>In Paracelsian alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, volatile principle of matter, not just the metal itself.</note>. This moisture is diverse and sends out different vapors for herbs, trees, and other similar things. When this vapor breaks forth from its first state (matter, essence, or origin)—or as soon as it first breathes out of the <term>matrix</term> and touches or is stopped by the outward air—it is immediately solidified. This vapor then evaporates constantly and continually. As long as that driving force <note>original: "appulse." A term for an impulse or a striking against something.</note> remains warm, the thing will grow. When the internal boiling ceases, there is no more steam, and thus the solidification and growth come to an end.

<margin>Wood originates from the vapor of Derfes.</margin>
Wood is the vapor of <term>Derfes</term> <note>A specific term used by Paracelsus to describe the essential matter or "spirit" of wood.</note>. Within it lies the <term>specific</term> <note>The unique essence or blueprint of a particular thing.</note> matter from which it is made. Nor is it made of that vapor alone, but it may also be made of some other "deric" matter.

<margin>Herbs spring from the vapor of Leffas.</margin>
In the same way, <term>Leffas</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for the specific essence or "first matter" of plants and herbs.</note> is the boiling matter from whose vapor all herbs spring. <term>Leffas</term>, and nothing else, is the sole source <note>original: "predestination"</note> of herbs. God is much more wonderful in these <term>specifics</term> than in all other parts of nature.

<margin>Stannar is the mother of metals.</margin>
<term>Stannar</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for the "mother" or generative matrix from which metals are formed.</note> is the mother of metals, providing the first matter to the metal through its stream. Metals are nothing but thickened vapor from <term>Stannar</term>.

<summary>This page explains the Paracelsian theory that all physical matter—wood, herbs, and metals—originates as an invisible vapor or "fume" that solidifies upon contact with the air. It introduces unique alchemical terms for the source-materials of these substances, such as Derfes for wood, Leffas for plants, and Stannar for metals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of how diverse materials and qualities originate from a single, unified source, moving from the process of physical decay to the primordial "Great Mystery" of creation.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>The third Book of Philosophy;</header>

<margin>An example using colors to explain the Great Mystery.</margin>
In the beginning, there was only one thing, without any predisposition <note>original: "inclination"</note> or form, from which all things later emerged. That origin was nothing other than a balanced <note>original: "temperate"</note> color—for example, purple—which has no bias toward any other color, but is plainly seen in its exact mixture <note>original: "temperature." In this context, it refers to a balanced combination of qualities.</note>. Yet within it are all colors. Red, green, azure <note>original: "azure." A bright blue color.</note>, yellow, white, and black cannot be separated from it. And every one of these colors gives rise to many darker shades, yet each is thoroughly and correctly colored <note>original: "tinctured." In alchemy, a tincture is a substance that imparts its qualities or color to another.</note> by itself. And although various and opposing colors lie hidden within them, they are all concealed under one. In the same way, everything had its essence in the Great Mystery <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum." A Paracelsian concept referring to the primordial source of all creation.</note>, which the Supreme Creator <note>original: "supream workmaster." Referring to God as the divine artisan or architect.</note> later separated.

<margin>Crystal contains all the Elements within it.</margin>
Crystal will produce sparks <note>original: "strike fire"</note> not because of a fiery nature, but because of its solidity and hardness. It also contains the other elements within it—not in their essence, but in their physical matter—namely: burning fire, breathing air, moistening water, and the black, dry earth.

<margin>And color too.</margin>
In addition to all these, it contains all colors (though hidden) within the mixture of their qualities. This is like fire in steel, which reveals itself neither by burning, nor by shining, nor by showing any color. In this regard, all colors and all elements are present in everything. If anyone desires to know how all things could arise and permeate everything in this way, he must believe that all this happened and was precisely and accurately ordered by the Only One, who is the Creator <note>original: "former"</note> and Architect of all things.

<summary>The author explains that all diversity in the natural world—represented by colors and the four elements—originates from a single, unified source called the "Great Mystery." He uses the examples of the color purple and the properties of crystal to show how multiple qualities can exist hidden within a single substance.</summary>

<margin>Invisible things are made visible through physical bodies.</margin>
Even though nature, as we have said, is invisible within bodies and substances, that invisibility becomes visible through those physical bodies. Just as the essence of every thing is clearly seen in its properties <note>original: "virtues." In early modern philosophy, "virtues" referred to the inherent powers or medicinal properties of a substance.</note> and colors. Invisible bodies have no other form except for this kind of physical consistency. Observe, then, that invisible things contain all the Elements within them and operate within every Element. They can release the fire and power of their Element from themselves; they can breathe out *air*, just as a person does; they can also release *water*, as a person releases urine; they possess the nature of *earth* too, and originated from the earth. Consider it this way: the liquid or moisture of the earth boils daily, sending upward that subtle spirit which was within itself. In this way, invisible things and the Firmament <note>original: "Firmament." Historically, the sphere of the stars or the sky, believed to be a physical structure or the atmosphere.</note> itself are nourished, which cannot happen without such a vapor.

<margin>How the Firmament and invisible things are nourished.</margin>
Incorporeal <note>original: "incorporeall." Lacking a physical body; spiritual or non-material.</note> things can no more live without food and drink than physical things can. Therefore, stones grow out of the earth, but they grow from a spirit that matches their own nature. Every stone draws its own spirit to itself.

<margin>The origin of fiery dragons and ghosts.</margin>
From things such as these, ghosts, fiery dragons <note>original: "fiery Dragons." Likely referring to meteors, comets, or atmospheric phenomena interpreted as mythical creatures.</note>, and many others proceed. If invisible things, as well as visible ones, are active in their essence, it is due to the nature of the Great Mystery <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum." A term used by the philosopher Paracelsus to describe the primordial source of all things.</note>. It is like wood being set on fire by a candle or taper, which loses or wastes nothing of its own flame in the process. And even though the spirit is not physical, it must have something physical to sustain itself

<margin>
Invisible things are sustained by visible things.
</margin>
...alive, namely, wood. Similarly, all invisible things must be sustained, nourished, and increased by something visible. Along with these visible things, they shall also eventually perish and come to an end all together. Nevertheless, they still maintain their operation and activity within themselves without loss or damage to other things—unless there is an outpouring <note>original: "effusion." A pouring out or release of a liquid or substance.</note> of those physical <note>original: "corporeall." Physical or bodily in nature.</note> and visible things. Although this process is carried out by the invisible, it is discovered or known through the visible, and so on.

->*The rest (for the Author undoubtedly*<-
->*wrote more) cannot be found.*<-

---

# READER,

What I have done in the translation <note>original: "Verſion"</note> of these two singularly eminent men, **Paracelsus** and **Crollius**, has been rather as a translator than an interpreter, so that the authors' meaning <note>original: "ſence"</note> more than mine might be searched out. Although the translation is not so elegant and significant as the original, yet (if my judgment does not fail me) the matter is preserved entire and sound. In both tracts <note>A "tract" is a short piece of writing or treatise.</note> you will meet with some strange <note>original: "uncouth"</note> and unusual words which, for your better understanding (you who are not acquainted with such language), I have here alphabetically explained, as follows:

---

->A<-

**ADECH** is our inward and invisible man, which first shapes those things in the mind that afterward are done with the hands.

<summary>In this preface, the translator explains his goal of staying true to the original meaning of Paracelsus and Crollius rather than adding his own interpretation. He introduces a glossary to help the reader understand the specialized or archaic terminology used in the texts, starting with the term "Adech."</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Oswald Crollius, translation, alchemy, Adech, glossary, invisible man, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of alchemical and philosophical terms used by Paracelsus and Crollius, moving from the end of the "A" section through "C."</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<warning>Ink spotting and bleed-through from the reverse side are present across the page, though the text remains legible.</warning>

**Secrets** <note>original: "Arcana"</note>, secrets or mysteries. **A secret** <note>original: "Arcanum"</note>; or (according to Paracelsus) the hidden non-physical <note>original: "incorporeall"</note> power <note>original: "vertue"</note> in natural things.

**Archaltes**, the prop or pillars of the earth.

**Archeus**, the chief, exalted, invisible spirit: the hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> power <note>original: "vertue"</note>, craftsman <note>original: "artificer"</note>, <term>physician of nature</term> in everyone.

**Stars** <note>original: "Astra"</note>, stars; also the force and power of things achieved through chemical processes <note>original: "preparations"</note>.

---

**Bismuth** <note>original: "Bisemutum"</note>, the palest or lowest-quality <note>original: "worst"</note> sort of lead; it is <term>bismuth</term> <note>original: "Tin-glass"</note>.

---

## C

**Cabala**, that most secret knowledge which the Hebrew Rabbis say was given by God along with the Law of Moses.

**Caleruth**, a note or sign of desire, when a thing tends toward its "first matter" <note>The fundamental substance from which all things are created</note> and would return to the place from which it came.

**Cobalt** <note>original: "Cobaltum"</note>, a stone from which a substance is made that is useful <note>original: "behoofull"</note> for medicine. It is a mineral.

<meta>catchword: Derfes</meta>

<summary>This page defines key Paracelsian terms beginning with A, B, and C, explaining concepts like the "Archeus" (a person's internal natural physician) and identifying substances like bismuth and cobalt.</summary>
<keywords>Arcana, Archeus, Bismuth, Kabbalah, Cobalt, Alchemical terminology, Paracelsus, natural philosophy</keywords>

<page-num>64</page-num>

## D

***Derses***, a secret vapor of the earth from which <note>original: "whence"</note> wood grows <note>original: "groweth"</note>.

***Diamea***, spirits living among stones and rocks.

***Divertallum***, the generation that comes from the Elements.

***Drame***.

***Duelech***, a kind of <term>tartar</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, "tartar" refers to stony mineral deposits that form in the body, such as kidney stones</note> in the human body; a spongy stone that is very rare <note>original: "precious"</note>.

***Durdales***, spirits that have bodies and live among trees.

## E

***Enür***, the hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> vapor of water from which stones are produced <note>original: "bred"</note>.

***Evestrum***, is that perpetual substance of the firmament within the elementary world; it is understood as <note>original: "taken for"</note> a prophetic spirit foretelling things to come by preceding <note>original: "precedent"</note> signs and tokens. To **evestrate** is to speak through <note>original: "by"</note> that spirit.

## F

***Flage***, spirits that know the secret and hidden things of men.

<summary>This page defines occult and Paracelsian terms beginning with D, E, and F, focusing on elemental spirits (like Diamea and Durdales) and mystical vapors that produce physical matter.</summary>
<keywords>Derses, Diamea, Divertallum, Duelech, Durdales, Enür, Evestrum, Flage, Paracelsus, alchemy, elemental spirits, tartar, prophecy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of Paracelsian and alchemical terms, moving from the letter G through I.</meta>

## G

***Gabalam***, ***Gebalum***, a thing repaired <note>original: "repair'd"</note>, restored <note>original: "reſtor'd"</note>, or curdled.

***Gabalis homo***, such a man.

***Gamahaa***, is when a living thing is affected or influenced <note>original: "wrought upon"</note> by its figure <note>The shape or image of something</note>; for example, when a pigeon is struck dead from the top of the house only by thrusting a pin through a picture of it on paper. <note>This refers to the concept of sympathetic magic or "images" that hold power over the objects they represent.</note>

***Gnomes***, ***Gnomi***, are little men, dwarfs, or rather spirits with bodies living under the earth—pygmies <note>original: "Pigmies"</note> scarcely half a foot high.

***Gonetick***.

## H

***Hilech***; the star of medicine <note>original: "astrum medicine"</note>, or the spirit hidden <note>original: "hid"</note> in medicine.

## I

***Iliaster***, the first matter of all things, consisting of **salt**, **sulfur**, and **mercury**: generally it is understood as <note>original: "taken for"</note> the hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> virtue of nature, by which all things increase, grow, multiply, and are nourished. See the *Dictionary of Chemistry* <note>original: "Vid. Lex. Chym."</note>.

<summary>This page defines alchemical and mystical terms beginning with G, H, and I, including definitions of Gnomes as earth spirits and Iliaster as the fundamental "first matter" of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Gabalam, Gamahaa, Gnomes, Hilech, Iliaster, salt, sulfur, mercury, Paracelsus, alchemy, sympathetic magic, elemental spirits</keywords>

# L

<term>*Leffa*; *Leffas*,</term> is the juice <note>original: "juyce"</note> of the earth newly drawn into the roots of plants <note>original: "vegetables"</note>, by which they grow.

<term>*Lemores*, *Lemures*,</term> are the spirits of the element of water, not the shapes and ghosts of dead men, as the pagans <note>original: "heathen"</note> imagined.

<term>*Limboan*,</term> also known as <note>original: "alias"</note> <term>*Lymbus*,</term> is the first matter or seed of the world, or all things in it.

<term>*Lorind*,</term> is the movement <note>original: "moving"</note> of the waters with a musical noise <note>original: "noyse"</note>, and is a sign <note>original: "signe"</note> of some change at hand.

# M

<term>*Marcasita*,</term> the raw or unripe matter of metals <note>original: "mettalls"</note>.

<term>*Mechili*.</term>

<term>*Melosina*,</term> despairing women, now living in a fantastical, animalistic <note>original: "bruitish"</note> body, nourished by the elements, into which at last they shall be changed, unless they happen to marry a man. See the *Chemical Dictionary* <note>original: "Vid. Lex. chym."</note>.

<term>*Montans*.</term>

<summary>This page defines alchemical and elemental terms starting with L and M, including descriptions of water spirits (Lemures), the primordial "seed" of the world (Lymbus), and the mythical Melosina.</summary>
<keywords>Leffa, Lemures, Lymbus, Lorind, Marcasita, Melosina, Paracelsus, alchemy, elemental spirits, first matter, metallurgy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of Paracelsian and alchemical terms, moving from the letter N through S.</meta>

### N

<term>*Nesder*.</term>

<term>*Neuferani*,</term> spirits living in the air <note>original: "aire"</note>.

### P

<margin>and not of Rome</margin>
<term>*Penates*,</term> spirits of heaven and the element of fire. <note>In Roman mythology, Penates were household gods; Paracelsus redefines them here as elemental spirits of fire.</note>

<term>*Pyrotechny*,</term> the art <note>original: "Art"</note> of preparing or working things by fire. <note>original: "Pyrotechney"; from the Greek *pyr* (fire) and *techne* (art/skill), referring to the chemical and metallurgical arts.</note>

### R

<term>*Relollaceus*, *Relolleum*</term> is the virtue <note>original: "vertue"</note> or essential power derived from the complexion <note>In early medicine, the "complexion" referred to the specific balance of humors or qualities in a person or substance.</note>. There is a threefold *Relolleum*, for which see the *Chemical Dictionary* <note>original: "Lexicon Chymicum"</note>.

### S

<term>*Samies*.</term>

<term>*Spagyric*,</term> that which separates the false from the true, and the impure from the pure. <note>original: "Spagyrick"; a term coined by Paracelsus for alchemical medicine, derived from Greek words meaning "to tear apart" and "to bind together."</note>

<term>*Stannar*,</term> is the mother of metals <note>original: "mettalls"</note>, a secret fume from which metals are made.

<term>*Sylphs*,</term> are pygmies or dwarfs. <note>While modern fantasy often depicts Sylphs as air spirits, Paracelsus sometimes associated these terms with various types of elemental beings.</note>

<term>*Sylvesters*,</term> airy men, or airy spirits living in woods and groves. <note>From the Latin *silvestris*, meaning "of the forest."</note>

<term>*Sirens*,</term> sea monsters <note>original: "Syrenes"; "sea-monsters"</note>, bred from the nymphs.

<meta>catchword: Talcum,</meta>

<summary>This page provides definitions for Paracelsian terms starting with N, P, R, and S, focusing on elemental spirits like Sylphs and Sylvesters, and the alchemical process of "Spagyric" separation.</summary>
<keywords>Neuferani, Penates, Pyrotechny, Relolleum, Spagyric, Stannar, Sylphs, Sylvesters, Sirens, Paracelsus, alchemy, elemental spirits</keywords>

# T

<term>*Talcum*,</term> a bright, clear matter from which oil of **talc** <note>original: "Talk"</note> is made; there are four sorts of **talc** <note>original: "Talck"</note> for which you may see the *Chemical Dictionary* <note>original: "Lex. Chym."; refers to the *Lexicon Chymicum* by William Johnson or a similar work.</note>.

<term>*Trarames*,</term> the actions of the spirits and ghosts of dead men, which are heard but not seen.

<term>*Tronum*,</term> celestial <note>original: "caelestiall"</note> dew made from the air <note>original: "aire"</note>.

<term>*Truphat*,</term> the occult virtue <note>original: "vertue"</note> of minerals, which perfects <note>original: "preferring"; in an alchemical sense, to advance or mature a metal toward a higher state.</note> every metal <note>original: "mettall"</note>.

<term>*Tura*.</term>

<term>*Turban*,</term> an innumerable multitude of stars in the firmament of heaven; also a presage <note>An omen or sign of a future event.</note> from all things which the fourfold inferior world of the elements contains <note>original: "containeth"</note>.

# V

<term>*Umbratiles*,</term> <note>From the Latin *umbra*, meaning "shadow."</note> bodies that were once rotted and afterward made visible again by the stars through a magical virtue <note>original: "magicall vertue"</note>.

<term>*Undena*,</term> airy men and earthly spirits. <note>In many Paracelsian texts, *Undina* or *Undines* are specifically water spirits; here they are described as airy and earthly beings.</note>

<meta>catchword: Woar-</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the glossary of Paracelsian terms, defining alchemical substances like talc, supernatural entities like ghosts and Umbratiles, and celestial phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>Talc, Trarames, ghosts, Tronum, Truphat, Turban, Umbratiles, Undena, Paracelsus, alchemy, elemental spirits</keywords>

# W

<term>*Woarchadumie.*</term> <note>A Paracelsian term often used to refer to gold or the "philosophical gold" derived from metals.</note>

# Z

<term>*Zonnetes*,</term> fantastical bodies of the **Gnomes**. <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, Gnomes are elemental spirits of the earth; "fantastical" here suggests their bodies are made of a subtle, non-fleshly substance.</note>

<term>*Zundell*,</term> *fomes* <note>original Latin: fuel or tinder</note>, in English, **tinder**.

---

## THE END <note>original: "FINIS"</note>
